When Is Additional Retrieval Worthwhile? A Cost-Aware Study of Evidence Gain and Answer Reliability in RAG

Zhouting Zhao · Oct 27

Studied a cost-aware retrieval policy that uses a regularized linear model to trigger hybrid search only when incremental evidence is beneficial.

For you: Directly applicable cost-optimization technique for RAG pipelines to reduce unnecessary expensive retrieval calls.

Abstract

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can benefit from more sophisticated retrieval, but extra retrieval may be unnecessary for many questions and gains in evidence retrieval may not translate into faithful or correct answers. We study a cost-aware, two-action retrieval policy that chooses between a dense-retrieval-plus-reranking baseline and a hybrid lexical--dense retriever. Rather than classifying baseline retrieval as simply successful or unsuccessful, we estimate the question-specific incremental evidence-retrieval benefit of hybrid retrieval using features available before the routing decision. A regularized linear model and decision threshold are selected using document-grouped cross-validation and frozen prior to held-out evaluation. On 60 previously unseen QASPER training-split articles (188 questions, 169 evidence-evaluable), the policy triggers hybrid retrieval on 46 questions (24.5\%) and obtains the same evidence Hit@10 as always-hybrid retrieval (78.70\%, versus 76.92\% for always-keep). Paired, paper-cluster bootstrap intervals do not establish a reliable utility advantage. We further compare generated answers from a 4-bit Qwen3-4B model under the three retrieval policies. Provisional answer-level assessments show no clear correctness or faithfulness improvement after partial review, despite the evidence-retrieval result. The generation assessments remain model-assisted and lack independent human validation. These results expose an important distinction between retrieving relevant evidence efficiently and improving the reliability of the generated answer. The work is presented as a reproducible empirical study, not a claim of statistically established answer-quality superiority.

Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents

Milad Sikaroudi · Oct 27

Created Concordat, a Python library treating LLM agents as finite-state session protocols to enforce valid tool usage and guardrails at runtime.

For you: Directly applicable pattern for building deterministic, safe agent workflows and enforcing strict tool-call state machines in production.

Abstract

Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.

Hallucination-Aware Multi-Agent RAG via Internal Attention Rewards

Runsong Jia, Qian Chang, Mengjia Wu et al. · Oct 26

Adds an attention‑based hallucination‑risk reward to a multi‑agent RAG system, training LoRA adapters that improve accuracy and cut hallucinations on three benchmarks.

For you: Directly enhances RAG faithfulness with modest adapter training.

Abstract

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in retrieved evidence, but its outputs often contain claims unsupported by that evidence. Recent multi-agent RAG systems decompose the pipeline into cooperative decisions, yet they typically optimize all agents under a shared task reward such as EM or F1, which measures answer overlap rather than faithfulness to evidence. We propose a hallucination-aware multi-agent RAG framework that combines the task reward with an internal hallucination-risk signal computed from the generator's attention-head norms. Three role-specific LoRA adapters, a query planner, an evidence selector, and an answer generator, share a frozen backbone. A lightweight critic is trained once on TruthfulQA truthful/false pairs and then kept frozen, providing a complementary risk score that is added to the shared reward during cooperative PPO training. Across three backbones (Qwen3-8B, Llama3-8B, Llama2-13B) and three benchmarks (RAGTruth, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA), our method improves task performance and reduces hallucination rates over the strongest multi-agent baseline. Ablations and critic diagnostics show that the TruthfulQA-calibrated signal provides useful reward shaping that transfers across RAG tasks.

RubriX: A Fast, Fine-Tunable Non-Autoregressive Rubric Judge

Balamurugan Paramasivam, Sudalai Rajkumar · Oct 25

Developed RubriX, a non-autoregressive rubric judge using a LoRA-adapted encoder and cached level embeddings to score responses via cosine similarity without generation, reaching 75.4% accuracy at low latency.

For you: Drop-in evaluation component that could dramatically cut LLM-as-a-judge latency and cost for offline testing or monitoring.

Abstract

Rubric-based evaluation asks which level of a rubric a response meets. It is usually done by prompting a large language model (LLM) as a judge, which needs a generative call per judgment and reads every level description, so its cost grows with the rubric. We present RubriX, a rubric judge whose architecture is inspired by contrastive language models (CLM): a LoRA-adapted encoder embeds the input and each level description, and a softmax over scaled cosine similarities gives a probability for every level without text generation. Trained on public rubric data and proprietary synthetic data, RubriX reaches 75.4% exact accuracy on held-out rubrics, against 57.1% for TypeSafe's Jev and 49.8% for a Qwen3-8B judge. On eight human-labelled benchmark sets it is within 6 percentage points of Jev's accuracy on most of them, while Jev keeps a larger lead on two and ranks responses best on average; against the results reported in the G-Eval paper, RubriX outperforms G-Eval with GPT-3.5 on every SummEval dimension and both QAGS subsets. With level embeddings cached, RubriX's latency stays at about 28 ms per judgment as level descriptions grow from 10 to 3,000 tokens, while Jev's (approximate network time subtracted) rises from 44 ms to 211 ms and the Qwen3-8B judge's from 32 ms to 516 ms, making RubriX 7.7x faster than Jev and 18.8x faster than the Qwen3-8B judge at 3,000 tokens per level.

Retriever-Free Retrieval: Navigating Documents with Decision Models

Chao Wan, Paul Jünger, Beixi Du et al. · Oct 26

Introduced DecisionRAG, which uses pretrained language model decision modules to navigate corpus structures in a single forward pass without embeddings, outperforming dense retrieval.

For you: Novel retriever-free approach that could radically improve retrieval precision and latency in your enterprise search pipelines.

Abstract

While language models have become dramatically more capable, retrieval still largely relies on lexical matching or similarity in fixed embedding spaces. This creates a capability asymmetry: what the retriever fails to find, even the strongest generator cannot recover. We propose DecisionRAG, which brings the capabilities of pretrained language models to retrieval using decision models that jointly evaluate candidate branches in a single forward pass, without generating any tokens, and show that judging candidates in a shared context makes their relevance estimates comparable. Like a person browsing a library, DecisionRAG navigates the corpus's inherent structure from broad topics down to individual passages, deciding at each level which branches are worth exploring. Without task-specific training or a first-stage retriever, DecisionRAG outperforms dense retrieval with an 8B reranker by 13.8 R@1 points on MultiDoc2Dial and 17.4 Hit@1 points on Natural Questions. Across four benchmarks, including a corpus of 2.68 million Wikipedia paragraphs, retrieval takes just 0.7-1.8 seconds per query.

Lossless Compression of Proximity Graph Indexes for Efficient Approximate Nearest Neighbors Search

Francesco Benocci, Leonard Dahlmann, Francesco Gargiulo et al. · Oct 27

Developed a lossless compression method for HNSW proximity graph indexes using offline graph permutations and SIMD-accelerated integer compressors.

For you: Directly impacts vector database memory footprints and latency at scale, highly relevant for large-scale enterprise search infrastructure.

Abstract

Graph-based Approximate Nearest Neighbor (ANN) indexes, such as HNSW, are the backbone of modern vector search. However, their massive memory footprint creates a severe infrastructure bottleneck. While vector quantization is widely adopted to reduce the memory required to store data vectors, the graph's edge structure increasingly dominates total memory usage. Compressing these adjacency lists for online search presents a critical system challenge: because decompression occurs during query processing, i.e., while navigating the graph, memory savings cannot come at the expense of query latency. In this paper, we present a compression technique explicitly tailored for live HNSW routing. By applying offline graph permutations to artificially induce structural locality, we enable the use of SIMD-accelerated integer compressors, such as Stream VByte, for rapid on-the-fly decompression. Evaluated across diverse real-world workloads, we significantly outperform the current state of the art in both compression ratio and query latency. Our experimental results show that we achieve compression ratios up to 52.7% with negligible penalties in latency compared to the uncompressed baseline. Furthermore, our technique has been effectively deployed in production at eBay, achieving up to 44.3% reduction in edge storage with no degradation in query latency.

Do We Need an LLM to Know Why Sources Conflict? Decision Models for Knowledge-Conflict Typing in RAG

Parv Khatri, Harshul Jain, Mehak Kapoor et al. · Oct 27

Evaluated typed decision models for knowledge-conflict typing in RAG. They matched or exceeded flagship models in accuracy at 4-39x lower cost and 2.6-6x lower latency.

For you: Actionable technique for optimizing RAG latency and cost while handling conflicting source documents.

Abstract

When the sources retrieved for a query disagree, the right response depends on why they disagree: a question with several valid answers, a contested topic, stale information and a false source each call for different behaviour. Cattan et al. (2025) showed that telling a generator the conflict type improves its responses by about 24 points, but that predicting the type is hard even for frontier LLMs. We study whether typed decision models, which return probabilities over fixed options in a single forward pass instead of generating text, can do this classification fast and cheaply. Using the CONFLICTS benchmark and the original paper's prompt verbatim, we evaluate all 11 decision models served by one commercial inference gateway, from 7 providers, against Gemini models on identical inputs. Asked directly, the decision models reach 50.0--67.0% accuracy; 10 of the 11 match or exceed Gemini 2.5 Flash, the paper's model (61.3%), at 4--39x lower billed cost (2 were free) and 2.6--6.0x lower latency. The current Gemini 3.8 Flash (70.0%) is significantly more accurate than 8 of them, but sending each decision model's least confident quarter of queries to it gives a cascade statistically tied with it for 8 of the 11, at 25--37% of its cost. End to end, answers generated with the types of 10 of the 11 follow the expected behaviour as often as with the LLM's types. Decomposing the taxonomy into yes/no questions lowers accuracy for every model, and a repair fitted on one model does not transfer. Pre-specified diagnostics show that dates play no measurable role and that sensitivity to source order varies widely between models. Decision models detect a planted false source less reliably than the LLM as hard labels, but their probabilities still separate planted from clean sources. We release code, prompts and all model outputs at https://github.com/Parv17k/decision-models-conflict-typing.

RAG & retrieval90% match

Read the Route: Attention-Steered Hypergraph Retrieval for Multi-hop RAG

Jiate Liu, Liuyi Chen, Xiaofei Yuan et al. · Oct 26

HyperAddress uses specific LLM attention heads to guide Personalized PageRank on a hypergraph, improving multi-hop RAG retrieval in a single forward pass.

For you: Directly addresses multi-hop RAG retrieval limitations and inference costs without iterative generation steps.

Abstract

Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) answers multi-hop questions by organizing a corpus into a graph and retrieving along its structure. Existing retrieval methods face two limitations. Diffusion- and similarity-based methods expand retrieval along fixed edge weights or by similarity to the question, without resolving its implicit entity references, allowing relevance errors to accumulate across hops. In contrast, exploration-based methods let an LLM reason about the next hop, but require a separate generation at each hop, leading to high inference costs. We observe that an LLM can resolve implicit entity references from retrieved passages and a multi-hop question before generating any tokens. Further analysis identifies a small set of attention heads, termed addressing heads, that capture this capability across multiple hops in a single forward pass. Building on this finding, we propose HyperAddress, which runs Personalized PageRank on a description-labelled hypergraph, using addressing heads to determine the restart distribution and transition weights, thereby guiding multi-hop retrieval without any generation at query time. On MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and HotpotQA, HyperAddress outperforms the strongest baseline by 3.5 points of Recall@5 and 7.4 points of All@5 on average, with gains that grow with the number of hops. It answers more than twice as fast and builds its index at half the cost.

Memory Tax: Dissecting the Net Value of Agent Memory

Xuan He, Sining Zhoubian, Ruozhen Yang et al. · University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign · Oct 24 · ▲ 97

Analyzed memory systems in long-horizon agents, finding many underperform naive RAG due to a 'memory tax' where information organization obscures evidence.

For you: Crucial evaluation framework for deciding whether to build complex agent memory or stick with robust RAG.

Abstract

Memory is increasingly used in long-horizon agents, yet the net value of building memory over direct retrieval from raw history remains largely unclear. Through a large-scale evaluation across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, we find many dedicated memory systems underperform naive RAG in many cases, indicating a negative net value. Taking RAG as a reference, we dissect the net value of memory into the gains and losses over direct retrieval, termed memory benefit and memory tax. These gains and losses arise because memory process changes how the raw information is organized and accessed. We view such changes as the double-edged deviations from the raw history. They are originally designed and introduced to benefit the task, for example by reorganizing facts or distilling preferences, but some may also remove or obscure evidence, giving rise to memory tax. Since memory benefit reflects the expected gains of its design, we then focus more on the overlooked memory tax, study how it arises from the system and evaluation side, and generalize the concept to more application scenarios of memory. We hope the notion of memory tax can provide a new lens and shift more attention from standalone performance to the net value of memory, informing more reliable memory designs and evaluations. We will open-source the code and results to the community. Project Page: https://hexuan21.github.io/MemoryTax/

Tessera: Structure-Completed Evidence Units for Web Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Peichun Hua, He Sun, Zirui Zhuang et al. · Oct 27

Presents Tessera, a training-free evidence delivery layer that rebuilds raw HTML into fine-grained units with section paths and table headers, decoupling retrieval from reading.

For you: Directly applicable drop-in improvement for web RAG and document parsing pipelines that struggle with tables and structured content separation.

Abstract

Web retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) splits a page into fixed-size text chunks or, in recent visual pipelines, screenshot tiles, and the retrieved item serves at once as the unit of indexing, ranking, and reading. We trace answer evidence from source HTML to reader input over the full English Wikipedia and find that this coupling loses evidence in text and screenshot pipelines alike: evidence present in the source page often fails to reach the reader intact, particularly structured content such as tables and infoboxes, which chunking separates from the headers and context that give it meaning. Stronger extraction, context expansion, and larger reader budgets leave the loss in place. We present Tessera, a training-free evidence delivery layer that decouples what a pipeline retrieves from what its reader reads. Given the pages behind any ranked list, Tessera rebuilds their HTML into fine-grained evidence units, each completed with its page title, section path, and table headers, selects the units relevant to the query, and packs them into the reader context at an unchanged token budget. The index, the retriever, and the reader stay as they are, so Tessera applies to text, screenshot, and agentic pipelines. Across three question answering benchmarks, Tessera improves answer accuracy by 5 to 6 points on text and screenshot pipelines, consistently across readers, retrievers, and reader budgets, and outperforms strong HTML-aware and structure-aware RAG baselines under matched budgets. The gains carry over to multi-step agentic search. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/sarendis56/Tessera.

Context Is a Pipeline, Not a Window: Information Attrition in LLM Agent Harnesses

Yuvraj Sehgal, Mahsa Panahandeh, Naser Ezzati-Jivan · Oct 26

Measured information attrition in agent harnesses and discovered that fixed-length JSON slicing silently drops a third of tool outputs and breaks parsing.

For you: Essential practical warning for anyone building tool-using agents on top of custom prompt or harness wrappers.

Abstract

LLM agents are judged by two numbers: the size of the context window, and how often the final answer is correct. Both numbers miss a third thing: how much of a tool's output is lost before the model ever sees it. We measured this loss in a real tool-using agent. The agent finds the cause of injected faults by reading Linux kernel traces. The context window was almost empty: across 720 runs, the single largest prompt used only 5.75% of it. Yet a third of all tool results (3,827 of 11,189) were cut before they reached the model, and every cut result became broken JSON. The harness cut each result by slicing its JSON text at a fixed length. This does not shorten a result evenly. It deletes whatever data sits near the end. One field reached the model in only 4.7% of calls. A second field, in the very same results, reached the model in 99.3% of calls. Which field held the fault's evidence depended on when the faulty process started: early or late in the trace. The agent's diagnosis accuracy matched this same split. So we ran controlled experiments to test one question: did the data loss cause the drop in accuracy? This paper makes four contributions. First, we model an agent's context as a five-stage pipeline, with a measured failure at each stage. Second, we replay 864 real tool results, including one from a production trace where the shipped cut deletes all 128 records of the fault's evidence. Third, a controlled experiment shows that the cut's real cost appears first as extra work, not as wrong answers: 43% more tool calls, twice the computation, and yield falling from 0.61 to 0.31. The agent still finds the right answer when it has another way to reach it. Fourth, a survey of seven agent frameworks finds two that cut tool output the same unsafe way. Whether the data cut truly causes the accuracy drop is still an open question.

DynaFlow: Learning Free-Form Multi-Agent Orchestration

Ruhao Liu, Qi Li, Shizun Wang et al. · National University of Singapore · Oct 24 · ▲ 202

DynaFlow lets a main LLM generate executable workflows to coordinate fixed sub‑agents, raising multi‑hop retrieval and reasoning accuracy to 88.08% across six benchmarks.

For you: Drop‑in orchestration layer that boosts multi‑agent pipelines with minimal retraining.

Abstract

Multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their ability to adapt to diverse task requirements. We introduce DynaFlow, a framework that integrates free-form executable workflow synthesis into the action space of the main agent. The agent decides when collaboration is needed and generates task-specific programs that coordinate sub-agents through sequential or parallel execution, structured information exchange, and verification. Execution results and traces inform subsequent actions, enabling the agent to adapt collaboration as reasoning progresses. We train only the main agent through supervised fine-tuning on distilled trajectories, followed by refinement on examples filtered for unsupported premises, biased verification, and information-flow defects. All sub-agents remain fixed, separating orchestration learning from executor capability improvement. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, scientific question answering, and multi-hop retrieval, DynaFlow achieves 88.08% overall accuracy in Forced mode, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 10.90 percentage points with the same Qwen3.5-27B backbone. Workflow analysis finds more explicit use, verification, and refinement of upstream outputs after training, highlighting cross-stage information flow as a key dimension of multi-agent orchestration.

TAO: Tool-Aware Joint Scheduling for LLM-Based Agentic Serving

Shuhuai Li, Shengdong Ye, Dongdong Ge et al. · AIOR · Oct 26 · ▲ 15

TAO jointly schedules LLM inference and external tool execution, using a value interface and dynamic programming to cut latency by up to 26.8% and improve decode throughput by up to 29.5%.

For you: Directly applicable for optimizing production multi-agent systems that alternate between GPU LLM inference and CPU tool calls.

Abstract

Agentic services alternate between model inference on clustered GPUs and external tools on distributed CPUs. In this scenario, existing systems (1) overlook tool-return readiness in service evaluation, wasting limited resources, and (2) use heuristic strategies to decide admission and placement on GPUs, resulting in suboptimal scheduling decisions. To bridge this gap, we present TAO, a tool-aware joint scheduling framework for LLM-based agentic serving. Its value interface combines near-term tool readiness, node-specific cache credit with time-aware decay, and token footprint; a dynamic program jointly selects and places waiting sessions under capacity constraints. Across the 12 reported model-workload-concurrency settings, TAO reduces latency by up to 26.8%, reduces mean time to first token (TTFT) by up to 36.3%, and increases decode throughput by up to 29.5% relative to the best evaluated state-of-the-art baseline for each metric.

RAG & retrievalNEW85% match

GraphRAGProv: GraphRAG Meets How-Provenance

Wadie El Amrani, Luis Galárraga · Oct 27

GraphRAGProv executes SPARQL queries over source-grounded RDF graphs to compute how-provenance polynomials, allowing users to verify text-to-graph transformations and track exact source links.

For you: Directly applicable to enterprise RAG and search systems requiring strict traceability, provenance tracking, and answer auditing.

Abstract

GraphRAG systems often use knowledge graphs to retrieve relevant context for an LLM that produces the final answer. That makes answers more inscrutable and therefore less appealing for domains where transparency is paramount. We present GraphRAGProv, an architecture that executes SPARQL queries over source-grounded RDF graphs to compute answers and their how-provenance polynomials. These polynomials record which statement occurrences were used and how they were combined. Because each occurrence is linked to its source text, users can follow these links to check whether the extracted statements reflect the text and whether the query combines them to answer the question. Querying thereby becomes a way to inspect and evaluate the text-to-graph transformation. Our interactive demo examines 400 cases from two multi-hop QA benchmarks, revealing extraction and query errors even when the final answer is correct.

DCKV: Learned Transform Coding of the Prompt KV Cache in Hybrid Language Models

Egor Maximov, Aleksei Merkelov, Vasiliy Alekseev et al. · Oct 27

DCKV introduces an end-to-end trained differentiable codec for hybrid model KV caches, achieving over 55x compression with minimal accuracy loss on long-context benchmarks.

For you: Directly relevant for optimizing LLM inference costs, memory footprints, and KV-cache reuse in production RAG systems.

Abstract

Hybrid language models keep a key-value (KV) cache only in their few full-attention layers, but this cache is still the part of a prompt's state that grows with the prompt and that must be stored or moved to reuse it. We present DCKV, a differentiable codec for the KV cache and the first KV-cache codec that is trained end to end. Its transform, its quantizer and its entropy models, which are conditioned on the prompt tokens and on already decoded neighbouring positions, are optimized together against the frozen language model, both for the code length that the entropy models assign and for the change of the model's output. On Qwen3.5-2B, DCKV stores a compressed token in more than 55 times fewer bytes than bf16 at a loss of at most 1.8 points on three long-context benchmarks, and in more than 100 times fewer bytes at a loss of at most 3.6 points.

When Retrieval Is Not Enough: Obsolete Facts in Agent Memory

Oleeviya Babu Poikarayil, Tony Basil Babu, Abdulrahman Nahhas et al. · Oct 26

Investigated memory retrieval in agents and found that larger retrieved evidence budgets decrease accuracy due to interference from obsolete statements competing with new facts.

For you: Directly impacts long-context RAG and memory systems, showing that naive context expansion hurts fact-retrieval fidelity.

Abstract

Memory-agent benchmarks often collapse retrieval and answer generation into one score, making it hard to tell whether a failure comes from missing an update or from not using an update that was already retrieved. We examine this distinction on the single-hop FactConsolidation task of MemoryAgentBench. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on 6K-history BM25 accuracy drops from 45% to 32% when the retrieved evidence budget grows from 64 to 512 tokens, even though a gold-assisted post-hoc audit finds the newest statement in every retrieved set. Most of the errors occur on questions that also contain an obsolete statement version. Under a frozen prompt and equal four-statement evidence size, replacing one unrelated statement with the obsolete competing statement reduces accuracy from 100% to 44.6% for Qwen2.5 and from 100% to 87.8% for Qwen3-14B, with all 50 harmful paired flips classified as stale. A 32K-history replication likewise favors BM25-64 over BM25-512 (69% versus 58%). These results suggest that retrieving the correct fact is not enough. An obsolete competing statement can be more harmful than an unrelated piece of evidence of the same size.

Coverage Under Contradiction: A Benchmark for Negation-Aware Retrieval

Pasha Abrishamchian, Eugene Yang, Andrew Yates et al. · Oct 27

Introduces CoverageBench-Negate with roughly 63,000 original-edited evidence pairs across 6 datasets to evaluate whether retrievers surface corpus evidence that contradicts their parametric priors.

For you: Directly relevant for evaluating and improving RAG retrieval robustness when documents contradict LLM parametric knowledge.

Abstract

Coverage-evaluation benchmarks quantify whether retrieved evidence covers the different pieces of information needed to satisfy an information need. Existing benchmarks mostly consider factual claims that may already be known to pretrained retrieval components, leaving open whether retrievers find corpus evidence when it contradicts those priors. We study how retrievers behave when their parametric knowledge and the information in a corpus disagree. To do so, we develop a process to augment a nugget-based coverage benchmark with controlled contradictions. For each nugget, we create a negated counterpart and edit its supporting documents to support that negation while preserving the context. Applying this process to CoverageBench, a benchmark measuring information coverage, we create CoverageBench-Negate, spanning six datasets and roughly 63,000 original-edited evidence pairs (one per nugget-document link). We attach rich construction metadata to every pair and additionally provide out-of-family LLM judgments over all pairs and a human audit of a sample. In baseline experiments, BM25 separates original from edited near-twin evidence substantially better than dense retrievers (Qwen3 and Jina), which stay near chance. We release the benchmark on Hugging Face Datasets (nuggets, documents, pairs) together with the construction materials, including the prompts used to create it.

EmbeddingGemma 2: Unified and Efficient Multimodal Representations

Henrique Schechter Vera, Sahil Dua, Jovan Andonov et al. · Google · Oct 26 · ▲ 26

Presents EmbeddingGemma 2, an open multimodal embedding model supporting unified text, image, audio, and video retrieval with flexible dimensional scaling.

For you: A strong open-source multimodal embedding model offering tunable dimensions and resource efficiency for advanced enterprise RAG systems.

Abstract

We present EmbeddingGemma 2, an open multimodal embedding model which natively represents text, images, audio, video, and combinations thereof in a unified space. Our approach introduces improvements such as simplified per-layer embeddings and a multi-stage curriculum with modality and objective progression which ensure strong quality across unimodal, cross-modal, and mixed-modal evaluations. Notably, in comprehensive embedding benchmarks across all modalities, EmbeddingGemma 2 establishes a new Pareto frontier, outperforming both specialist models and models two to twelve times larger. Furthermore, our design offers flexibility for resource-constrained environments, supporting selective modality encoder loading, adjustable vision token budgets, truncatable embedding dimensions, and quantized weights. We present ablations analyzing key decisions and release EmbeddingGemma 2 to promote further research.

The Inbox as a Coordination Primitive for LLM Agent Societies

Karthik Sreedhar, Leo Yuan, Pradyumna Chari et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated an inbox primitive (pull-based messaging) instead of push-based message delivery for large LLM agent societies up to 16,000 agents.

For you: Directly relevant architectural pattern for managing context windows and token costs in complex multi-agent systems.

Abstract

Language-model agents increasingly coordinate in large groups, and current agent protocols focus on delivering messages. For these agents, however, the scarce resource is not delivery but reading: every message costs tokens, and the context is finite. Current infrastructure works by push: the sender decides when a message enters the recipient's context, so the recipient reads in arrival order and drops the oldest messages once its context is full. We study the alternative, pull, in which messages wait in an inbox outside the context and the recipient's own rules decide what it reads and keeps. We argue that the inbox is a coordination primitive: each agent sets its rules privately, and those rules decide whether other agents' requests reach it. In simulated societies of up to 16,000 agents, with every decision an LLM judgment, we report three findings. First, push fails as an agent takes part in more coordination at once: an agent leading 20 negotiations with a full memory proposes options already rejected 97% of the time, because it has dropped the oldest replies. Second, an inbox is necessary under heavy, noisy traffic. Where first come, first read misses every request, an inbox that first reads messages carrying a refundable deposit still finds about 60% of the agents a request needs, at every size from 1,000 to 16,000. On a real email network, losses compound: when half the agents adopt such an inbox, forwarded search recovers only 5% of full adoption, against 42% for one-hop search. Third, an inbox comprises four decisions (what to read, what to keep, what a sender pays, and what a reply may say), and the best rule for each depends on the traffic and the agent's role. Multi-agent coordination under load requires an inbox layer designed for the traffic each agent receives.

Evaluation & reliability85% match

Where Should the Evaluation Budget Go in LLM-Driven Agent Scaffold Evolution?

Sichun Luo, Yi Huang, Mukai Li et al. · Oct 27

Investigated evaluation budget allocation for LLM-driven agent scaffold evolution, showing sparse search-time feedback combined with targeted confirmation works best.

For you: Directly relevant for engineering teams optimizing the cost and reliability of automated agent evaluation loops.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM)-driven agent scaffold evolution improves agent workflows through iterative candidate generation and evaluation. Evaluation both guides subsequent search and determines the final scaffold, but evaluating every candidate on the full task set incurs substantial execution costs. We investigate how evaluation budgets can be allocated across these two roles. Our experiments show that sparse feedback can sustain productive evolution, while additional targeted evaluation improves final selection. Replay analyses further show that shared informative items and a common scoring reference support comparisons among closely matched candidates. Motivated by these findings, we propose \method, an evaluation allocation framework that combines sparse search-time feedback with targeted final confirmation. A short fully evaluated warm-up establishes a shared informative core and calibrates a response model whose item parameters remain fixed during search. After search, \method refits the model and applies paired successive halving, reusing accumulated observations and executing only missing evaluations. Across four benchmarks and two evolution frameworks, \method achieves higher mean performance than full evaluation in most settings while using approximately half as many evaluation cells.

Decision Models as Judges for Hallucination Detection

Haozheng Liu, Zhaoqian Xue, Zhongyin Wang et al. · Oct 27

Compared decision models against generative judges for hallucination detection across 9,852 items. A confidence cascade matches GPT-6-level judges at 1-3% of the cost.

For you: Directly applicable for cutting LLM evaluation and guardrail costs in production without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract

Large language models are widely used as judges of model outputs (LLM-as-a-Judge), but they are costly and can hallucinate or be overconfident. Decision models are a potential alternative. They return a predefined verdict with a probability for each label instead of free-form text and are much cheaper and faster. We test this promise on hallucination detection. We build binary benchmarks for five hallucination types (9,852 items from six datasets) and compare five decision models with the generative judge gpt-6-sol. Performance depends strongly on the type: the best decision model matches gpt-6-sol in balanced accuracy on context inconsistency and comes within 3 percentage points on factual errors, but trails it by 4-8 points on logical and instruction inconsistency. Decision-model probabilities are overconfident, though less so than the GPT-6 models, and separate correct from incorrect decisions only moderately. We also find that a confidence cascade of decision models improves on the best single model by 1.9-3.3 points on three types and matches or exceeds gpt-6-sol at 1-3% of its cost. Overall, decision models are a practical low-cost judge for some hallucination types but need to be validated before use.

TrawMem: Reframing Agent Memory as Task-Oriented Role-Adaptive Workspaces

Mingyu Zhang, Zixu Li, Zhiheng Fu et al. · Oct 27

Proposed TrawMem, which reframes agent memory into task-oriented role-adaptive workspaces that organize interaction histories by task roles and complementary relations.

For you: Directly relevant for improving long-context multi-agent memory retrieval beyond simple semantic similarity search.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents rely on memory systems to leverage long-term interactions, yet existing systems typically retrieve memories based on their relevance to the current query and use them as contextual evidence. However, relevance alone does not characterize how a memory contributes to task solving: the same memory may serve different roles across tasks, such as directly supporting the answer or linking to complementary memories with weaker semantic relevance but necessary information. We therefore reframe agent memory systems as Task-Oriented Role-Adaptive Workspaces, where memories are selected and organized by their task-oriented roles and relations. Based on this view, we propose TrawMem, which organizes interaction histories into local threads and constructs task-oriented workspaces by assigning roles and jointly selecting complementary memories. Experiments on LoCoMo and MemGallery with four open- and closed-source backbones show consistent improvements over representative memory systems, with macro-F1 gains of 2.23-3.14 points. On LoCoMo, TrawMem improves the LLM-Judge score over SimpleMem by 4.07 and 4.31 percentage points on Qwen3.5-9B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash, respectively, while requiring approximately 12.8% and 5.1% fewer context tokens, respectively.

Benchmarking Task Takeover Across Agent Handoffs

Wenyuan Li, Guang Li, Dongyuan Li et al. · Oct 26

Introduced HSC-Bench with 1,039 tasks to evaluate multi-agent handoffs, showing that structured handoff state significantly improves downstream task continuation.

For you: Directly applicable to designing reliable multi-agent systems and managing state across agent handoffs.

Abstract

As agents collaborate on longer tasks, responsibility may pass between models before the work is complete. The receiving model must build on prior decisions, observations, and actions while addressing the remaining requirements. Evaluating this transition requires connecting the information preserved at handoff to how the receiver continues the work, a relationship that task-success and memory-retention scores alone do not capture. We introduce the Handoff State Continuity Benchmark (HSC-Bench), comprising 1,039 tasks that evaluate two complementary aspects of task takeover: recovering relevant repository evidence and continuing from established conversational, tool, and file-work state. Comparisons across six receiving models show that structured handoff state improves repository evidence recovery for every model and continuation performance for most models. The strongest receivers differ between the two tracks, showing that evidence-recovery rankings alone do not characterize performance when continuing from inherited state. By combining these comparisons with checks of subsequent responses and actions, HSC-Bench evaluates both access to prior evidence and adherence to established commitments and completed work. This provides a basis for selecting receiving models and assessing handoff representations according to the work that must be carried forward.

Quantifying Claim Smoothing by LLM Research Agents

Tiasa Singha Roy, Manya Wadhwa, Greg Durrett · Oct 26

Identified that deep research agents smooth out evidential jaggedness in long-form synthesis and introduced SmoothScore to quantify this overconfidence.

For you: Directly applies to evaluating hallucination and grounding in complex multi-agent research or deep RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. In this work, we find that LLMs produce long-form research reports that read as confident syntheses but which fail to accurately reflect the underlying documents. We call this failure smoothing. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. Finally, each claim is scored with respect to the evidence to quantify the degree of smoothing done by the agent. Across 7 systems and 4 datasets, we find that agents consistently produce stronger claims than warranted.

TriageRAG: Failure-Aware Self-Training for Adapting Retrieval-Augmented Language Models to Specialized Domains

Samarth Varshney, Ritu Yadav · Oct 24

TriageRAG categorizes RAG self-training failures into four types—known, solved, context-failure, and retrieval-failure—and routes them to targeted training signals, improving domain adaptation.

For you: Directly applicable to improving enterprise RAG domain adaptation and diagnosing whether generation failures stem from the LLM or the retriever.

Abstract

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets large language models (LLMs) answer knowledge-intensive questions from retrieved evidence, but adapting a general-purpose RAG system to a specialized domain such as medicine is difficult when only an unlabeled corpus is available. Self-training approaches such as SimRAG address this by letting the LLM write its own question-answer pairs from domain passages and fine-tuning on the pairs that survive a round-trip consistency filter. We observe that these approaches treat every retained example identically: they never ask whether the student already knows the answer, already extracts it correctly from the retrieved context, or fails - and if so, whether the failure lies with the reader or with the retriever. To tackle this, we propose TriageRAG, a failure-aware self-training approach that probes the student on each self-generated question with and without retrieved context, triages it into one of four categories (known, solved, context-failure, retrieval-failure), and routes each category to the training signal that addresses it, including a parametric route that teaches the reader to answer when retrieval misses. Probing is cheap and repeated every round, so the training mixture follows the student, and the triage histogram doubles as a label-free diagnostic of where the domain gap lies. Experiments on 11 datasets spanning three domains with a Qwen3-4B backbone, and on CS-Bench with Qwen3-8B, demonstrate that TriageRAG outperforms a budget-matched SimRAG by 1.2-6.4 points and is the only method that recovers the closed-book level in every case where retrieval hurts the base model. A single ablation identifies the mechanism: round-trip filtering removes every training context that lacks the evidence, and a reader that never sees such contexts learns to over-trust noisy retrieval.

Ask Before You Answer: A Question-Driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture (QERA) for In-Context Learning

Jinhu Qi, Minda Hu, Wentao Zhang et al. · Oct 26

QERA is a question-driven evidence reasoning architecture for long-context tasks that converts user requests into checklists, queries context, verifies quotes with code, and repairs answers using about 4 LLM calls.

For you: Highly relevant pattern for complex RAG and in-context learning over messy enterprise user traces and chat logs.

Abstract

AI assistants are increasingly asked to reason over a user's own web traces: group chats, community threads, activity logs and revision histories. On CL-bench Life, a benchmark of such real-life context learning, frontier LLMs solve fewer than one task in five. We find that the request is not the specification: over 80% of the benchmark's rubrics share less than 60% of their content words with the final request, as for a rule set two turns earlier or every record that meets the user's criterion, and LLMs answering directly pass only 67% of these implicit rubrics against 81% of the others. We propose QERA, a Question-driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture that asks before it answers. QERA first turns the request into a requirement checklist and typed information needs, filtered by code for faithfulness to the user's words; it then answers from the whole conversation, asks the same questions again with quotes verified by code, and applies a guarded local repair. It needs no training or rubrics and makes about four LLM calls per task. On all 405 tasks with four frontier LLMs, QERA attains the highest solving rate on every LLM (21.0% on average, against 18.2% for the strongest of three inference-time baselines and 14.6% for direct answering) and leads every baseline significantly when pooled across LLMs, while using fewer tokens than PSCI and 4-5% of the tokens of Ctx2Skill. Ablations on GPT-6-Luna show that the gain is settled before the answer, by a fixed answer protocol (+3.8 rubric points) and the request-specific questions (+1.6), and that it is concentrated on implicit rubrics, with checking afterwards refining mainly numeric requirements.

Clearing the FoG: Training KG Agents to Retrieve the Evidence Behind Their Answers

Zongyuan Sun, Shuai Zhao, Yingjun Du et al. · Oct 27

FoG trains knowledge graph agents to retrieve supporting triples alongside answers using evidence-grounded synthesis and evidence-coverage process reward (ECPR).

For you: Directly addresses RAG grounding and traceability by training agents to explicitly retrieve and cite supporting evidence triples.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) can answer many factual questions correctly, but a correct answer is not necessarily a supported one. For an LLM agent, a knowledge graph (KG) plays two roles: as a source of answers, it supplies facts that the model lacks; as a source of evidence, it supplies the supporting triples that make an answer traceable and auditable. However, existing KG agents treat the KG only as a source of answers: their training data carries answers but not evidence, and their training rewards check the answer but not the retrieved evidence, so agents often answer correctly without retrieving the supporting triples. We propose FoG, a framework that trains KG agents to retrieve the evidence behind their answers. FoG consists of evidence-grounded synthesis, a KG interaction agent, and an evidence-coverage process reward (ECPR): the synthesis recursively hides explicit entities behind verifiable sub-questions, so that every question carries its supporting triples by construction; the agent records the triples returned by the KG; and ECPR credits each supporting triple to the turn that first retrieves it. Experiments on three KGQA benchmarks demonstrate that FoG enables 3B--4B open-source agents to outperform substantially larger baselines in answer accuracy and evidence coverage, with zero-shot cross-KG transfer.

Spine: Compiling Subtask Trees into Structured Context for Long-Horizon Coding Agents

Jiahong Xiang, Kunqiu Chen, Lingming Zhang et al. · Oct 27

Spine compiles subtask trees into structured context for long-horizon coding agents, aligning model context with subtask nesting to prevent context inflation and degradation.

For you: Directly applicable pattern for managing context windows and multi-step execution states in complex, long-running enterprise agents.

Abstract

Long-horizon coding tasks contain nested subtasks. Typically, agents record interactions sequentially into a growing history, causing context inflation and performance degradation. Compressing or trimming the history without explicit subtask boundaries can discard information needed by unfinished subtasks. Conversely, recursive subagent delegation isolates completed subtasks through child agents by assigning every subtask boundary to delegation, which conflates subtask granularity with executing agent choice, adding execution and coordination overhead. We introduce Spine, an interface and runtime that align model context with subtask nesting and completion. An agent declares explicit subtask boundaries through work scopes, allowing the same agent to execute nested subtasks inline while reserving delegation for independent parallel tasks. The runtime organizes execution in SpineTree, a persistent tree recording subtask relations, local interactions, completion states, and returned results. Before each model call, it compiles the tree into model context just in time, retaining unfinished subtasks' interactions and replacing completed subtasks' details with their results, while preserving prefix-cache reuse. We integrate Spine into Codex to build SpineCodex. With GPT-5.6 Sol, compared with native Codex, SpineCodex improves SWE-Milestone Score by 11.8% while reducing total cost by 32.4%, improves the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 17.3%, and improves Mean@5 on eight of the ten evaluated FrontierSWE tasks. On SWE-Milestone, it also achieves a comparable Score to Recursive Subagent at 73.7% lower total cost. We also integrate Spine into Pi through a reusable software package: with Gemini 3.8 Flash and Qwen 3.8 27B, it improves SWE-Milestone Score by 9.5-17.5% and the average ProgramBench test-pass rate by 4.3-8.7% compared with native Pi.

WAKMan: Workload Aware KVCache Manager

Suhas Kumar Morisetty, Vaibhavi Lokegaonkar, Sachin Mathew Varghese et al. · Oct 26

Introduced WAKMan, a workload-aware KV cache eviction manager that dynamically adapts eviction policies to live multi-turn, agentic, or single-turn production traffic without manual tuning.

For you: Directly impacts LLM serving costs and latency in production multi-agent systems and mixed-workload APIs.

Abstract

Prefix caching enables a Large Language Model (LLM) servers to reuse the Key-Value (KV) states and accelerate prefill computation stage for requests that share a prefix with earlier prompts, thus reducing latency and serving cost. With limited memory resource, the KV cache eviction policy becomes a decisive factor in determining the overall benefits of prefix caching. Production traffic may have single-turn, multi-turn, agentic, or a mixture of any combination of these request types. Our findings indicate that agentic steps return within seconds and favor recency, while popular prompts and long conversations return after many other requests and favor frequency. Across 20 mixes, no single policy is always best: vLLM's default strategy LRU provides 12% higher cache hits as compared to the frequency-oriented S3FIFO on agentic-heavy mixes but trails it by up to 22% elsewhere, and even a single workload parameter can flip the winner even for any particular mix. We present WAKMan, a workload-aware KV cache manager that picks its eviction policy from live traffic without knowing request types or reading prompts. It improves hit rate over LRU by 5 percentage points on average, with the lowest mean and worst-case optimality gap of all policies.

I-HITL: Inalienable Human-in-the-Loop

Samuel Breckenridge, Dani Vilardell, Wenhao Wang et al. · Oct 26

Formalized Inalienable Human-in-the-Loop (I-HITL) protocols to prevent autonomous agents from bypassing guardrails when harness systems are compromised.

For you: Critical security pattern for autonomous multi-agent orchestration systems executing high-stakes actions.

Abstract

Human oversight is a pillar of AI safety. It is often exercised by a human in the loop (HITL) who approves critical actions taken by an AI agent. AI agents' increasing ability to bypass guardrails, though, raises the question of how to enforce HITL robustly. We introduce the concept of inalienable human-in-the-loop (I-HITL). An I-HITL protocol ensures that an agent can initiate an action only with explicit human approval, even when the human interacts with a system (e.g., a local agentic harness) compromised by the agent. A strong I-HITL protocol additionally ensures such approval even when an adversarial human attempts to delegate approvals to an agent. To motivate I-HITL, we report on experiments with a popular agentic wallet platform in which raising an agent's spending cap requires HITL approval via emailed one-time passcodes (OTPs). Given access to the user's email and a task blocked by the cap, we find that ten different frontier models will often autonomously raise the cap, harvesting OTPs to bypass a HITL guardrail and surfacing other vulnerabilities. We formally define I-HITL using the concept of communication topologies and prove necessary and sufficient conditions for its realization. We show that an I-HITL protocol requires a trusted entity - a trusted computing device (or equivalent) - and characterize which such devices can and cannot realize an I-HITL protocol. We present a prototype I-HITL system called Countersign that can plug into and strengthen existing HITL systems using off-the-shelf consumer mobile devices.

From Generation to Scoring: Scaling LLM-Based Semantic Labeling at LinkedIn

Jian Sheng, Sriram Vasudevan, Sundara Raman Ramachandran et al. · Oct 26

They replace multi‑label generation with single‑candidate semantic scoring, using masked attention to score hundreds of candidates, cutting latency 1.9× while preserving accuracy.

For you: Directly applicable method to speed up LLM‑based classification or retrieval scoring in production pipelines.

Abstract

For efficiency, industrial large language model (LLM) labelers often pack many candidate labels into one prompt and generate a YES/NO answer for each, even when each decision depends only on the entity and one candidate. Generating these independent decisions as one answer list couples them, spends decoding on the list, and relies on the list being well-formed. In this paper, we present an end-to-end recipe that reformulates the labeler as semantic scoring, fine-tuning it on single-candidate prompts to return each decision as one answer-token score from a prefill-only pass. We also adapt Multiple-Item Scoring (MIS) to score hundreds of candidates in one sequence with attention masked between them. Deployed for profile qualification labeling at LinkedIn, semantic scoring changes at most 0.01% of decisions when candidates are reordered and matches the agreement with reference labels that packed generation reaches only with about 4.7x as many training pairs. The MIS-based stack raises throughput over the deployed stack by 1.24x offline and 1.9x in model serving under a one-second p99 latency target. For offline batch inference, we implement MIS-based scoring as a reusable, model-agnostic Ray Data API proposed for upstream adoption. Model serving uses MIS support contributed to SGLang.

Changxu Duan · Oct 25

Uses an LLM agent offline to propose and validate enterprise business rules from historical transactions, compiling them into deterministic SQL-backed cascade lookups for zero-cost inference.

For you: Directly applicable pattern for migrating brittle enterprise rule extraction from runtime LLM prompts to deterministic lookup logic.

Abstract

Enterprise sales-order systems rely on manually maintained configuration rules to determine fields such as payment terms, shipping points, and sales ownership. When these rules are incomplete or stale, operators must enter values by hand, and downstream errors spread into billing, logistics, and reporting. This paper studies whether missing or outdated rules can be recovered from historical transactions instead of being relearned as opaque statistical patterns. We introduce a knowledge-graph-guided workflow in which an LLM agent proposes candidate business keys, meaning column combinations such as customer and document type, validates them through SQL profiling, and compiles the resulting evidence into deterministic cascade lookups. Experiments on enterprise sales-order prediction and public relational benchmarks show that cascades are most effective when fields follow stable business rules, while learned graph models remain preferable when prediction depends on temporal or neighborhood patterns. Reinforcement Learning Done Right for Explicit Confidence Targets

Zefeng He, Yichen He, Yuan Lin et al. · Oct 26

Proposes RLEC, a reinforcement learning method enabling LLMs to dynamically abstain based on explicit user-specified confidence targets in the prompt.

For you: Directly applicable for building reliable production LLM systems that need tunable refusal rates to control hallucination costs.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) can produce plausible but incorrect answers when they are uncertain, which can mislead users and undermine model reliability. Training models to abstain with reinforcement learning is a natural approach, but most existing methods use a fixed error penalty and therefore cannot adapt their behavior to different confidence requirements at inference time. We study \emph{explicit confidence targets}, where a user specifies a target \(p\) in the prompt and the model directly chooses whether to answer or abstain, with the goal of achieving an accuracy of at least \(p\) among answered questions. However, learning to respond to different confidence targets with a single model has proven difficult, and prior attempts have failed to distinguish different targets. We show that this failure comes from two problems: the reward signal that distinguishes different values of $p$ is often lost during optimization, while the original error penalty grows without bound as $p$ approaches one. We propose RLEC (\textbf{R}einforcement \textbf{L}earning with \textbf{E}xplicit \textbf{C}onfidence Targets), which preserves the learning signal needed to distinguish confidence targets, introduces a stable reward family that keeps the desired answer condition unchanged, and uses reward scheduling to balance effective learning and training stability. Together, these designs enable the first successful training of a single LLM for explicit confidence target control. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and model scales show that RLEC largely preserves general capabilities and achieves competitive calibration performance. RLEC also supports fine-grained target control without an external decision system or threshold calibration after training. By making explicit confidence targets part of the trained policy, RLEC opens a new and more direct path toward behavioral calibration.

CASEL: Consequence-Aware Selective Execution for Reliable Long-Horizon Tool-Using Agents

Yanyan Zhang, Fei Shen, Ting Yan et al. · Oct 26

CASEL is a runtime allocation policy for tool-using agents that weighs failure probability, consequence, and reversibility to choose between direct execution, verification, or sandboxing.

For you: Directly applicable design pattern for safeguarding enterprise multi-agent systems that invoke external APIs and modify external state.

Abstract

Tool-using language agents increasingly modify external systems, yet most runtime safeguards allocate scrutiny by generic uncertainty scores or coarse mutation heuristics. We introduce CASEL (Consequence-Aware Selective Execution Layer), a model-agnostic runtime allocation policy that combines calibrated failure probability, state-dependent consequence, reversibility, ambiguity, and evidence freshness to choose, for each proposed tool call, among direct execution, verification, sandboxing, clarification, approval, and abstention by minimizing a per-mode cost. We formalize direct execution as selective prediction and state and test the validity conditions of a finite-sample threshold and a trajectory risk ledger. In a controlled simulation over 20 seeds with 120,000 test actions each, in which every method has the same intervention menu and the same compute cost, CASEL reduces harmful-side-effect trajectories from 14.75 percent for an uncertainty-ranked gate to 7.02 percent and mean normalized harm from 0.184 to 0.123. A gate ranked by the product of failure probability and consequence reaches similar harm (5.82 percent, 0.120) but sends 45 percent more actions to human approval, and removing reversibility-aware routing raises the rate to 15.53 percent. A trajectory-level risk budget, in contrast, increases harm at equal cost (11.82 percent). Intervention effectiveness is assumed, not measured: the results rank allocation rules under the simulator's assumptions and do not establish deployment effectiveness.

Log-To-Leak: Prompt Injection Attacks on Tool-Using LLM Agents via the Model Context Protocol

Yuepeng Hu, Chongyu Fan, Sirat Samyoun et al. · Oct 26

Researchers demonstrate Log-To-Leak, a prompt injection attack via the Model Context Protocol that forces tool-using agents to exfiltrate context data via unauthorized log calls.

For you: Crucial security awareness if you use or build MCP-compliant tool-using agents in enterprise environments.

Abstract

LLM agents increasingly access external tools through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), but the natural-language metadata used to describe those tools creates a prompt-injection surface. Within this established tool-poisoning surface, we study Log-To-Leak, an additive post-hoc logging attack that induces a separate attacker-controlled call after the final legitimate call. Its payload targets the user query, MCP server name, server response, and agent response. We operationalize the injected directive through Trigger, Tool Binding, Justification, and Pressure, and separately measure call success, payload fidelity, and preservation of legitimate behavior. We evaluate five MCP servers and four LLM agents using five metrics spanning effectiveness, utility, and efficiency. Across 18 model-server pairs with valid tool execution, Log-To-Leak achieves an attack success rate (ASR) of 62.64-100% and conditional logging accuracy (LA) of 76.55-95.64%; 16 pairs have absolute target-task completion changes of at most 6.63 percentage points. Three evaluated defenses leave residual ASR above 55% on GitHub MCP with GPT-4o, while an instruction detector flags 85.53% of benign descriptions. These results show that successful task completion can coexist with unauthorized post-hoc disclosure and motivate host-controlled authorization for secondary tool calls.

Tingzhu Bi, Xun Zhang, Jinglin Li et al. · Oct 26

For you: Directly relevant for evaluating and simplifying multi-agent architectures in production; challenges over-engineered agent design.

Abstract

Root cause analysis (RCA) of microservice failures has attracted many LLM agents whose investigation is hand-crafted, through standard operating procedures, fixed stages and role agents. Each is evaluated on its own data against few baselines, so it is unknown whether this design beats letting a capable model investigate on its own. A stronger model mainly improves reliability, a multi-turn harness mainly improves aptitude, and more reasoning effort changes accuracy by a few points at most while multiplying the time by up to seven. QESChunker: A Single Objective Unifies Overlapping and Non-Overlapping Chunking for RAG

Yifan Zhao, Qianyang Zhao, Xinyu Guan et al. · Oct 26

Introduced QESChunker, which unifies overlapping and non-overlapping chunking by optimizing a utility objective over question-evidence supervision pairs.

For you: Provides a principled, data-driven alternative to heuristic document chunking in RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Chunking is a fundamental preprocessing step in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), yet most existing chunkers rely primarily on heuristics. We introduce the Question--Evidence-Supervised Chunker (QESChunker), to our knowledge the first method for constructing static chunk indices from question--evidence supervision. QESChunker constructs document-specific chunk indices by optimizing over human-annotated or LLM-generated question--evidence pairs, without access to held-out test queries. We formulate non-overlapping and overlapping index construction through a unified question--evidence utility-maximization objective, turning chunking into a supervised combinatorial optimization problem. Under the non-overlap constraint, the objective decomposes over chunks and can be optimized exactly in polynomial time by dynamic programming. When overlap is allowed, the objective becomes monotone submodular; exact optimization is NP-hard, but our greedy achieves a $(1-1/\sqrt{e})$-approximation. Across two self-constructed and three public datasets, both variants outperform existing chunking methods in complete-evidence recall in nearly all evaluated settings, under the same overlap regime and a fixed hybrid retriever. These results recast chunking as supervised, budget-aware index construction for evidence retrieval.

Grounding LLM Agents in Business Processes: Process-Aware Knowledge Graphs for Reliable Multi-Step Service Invocation

Saiful Khan, Afrah Farea · Oct 27

Integrating OData metadata and BPMN process graphs into a typed property graph improved API endpoint retrieval Recall@3 by 5.7 points for enterprise tool-use agents.

For you: Directly relevant for enterprise search and agentic tool invocation where API discovery and process constraints matter.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents that call enterprise APIs must select the correct endpoints, follow process order, use valid payload keys, and choose coded values from predefined lists. However, current retrieval-augmented service discovery treats endpoints independently and therefore misses process and schema constraints. We address this limitation by integrating Open Data Protocol (OData) metadata and Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) reference processes in a typed property graph. A hybrid retriever identifies candidate endpoints using dense embeddings and follows process edges to retrieve subsequent endpoints. We evaluate the method on 42 enterprise services with process-chain ground truth. For queries with low lexical overlap between the query and downstream entities, it improves Recall@3 by 5.7 points (n=74), with consistent gains across embedding models and cut-offs. It matches dense retrieval on other queries and provides no improvement on OpenAPI services without a process layer, indicating that the gains arise from process structure. We also evaluate an LLM agent on 81 multi-step queries. It completes 38.7% of tasks, compared with 10.3% for a chunk-based baseline. In an elicitation probe, providing valid code lists eliminates invented codes but leaves invalid keys in 46% of write runs. Full typed context reduces invalid calls to 3% (3 of 101 runs), with no invented codes. These results show that exposing process and schema constraints during retrieval improves the reliability of agent-driven API calls.

SkillQR: Agentic Skill Discovery for Reason-Intensive Query Rewriting

Kenan Jiang, Meixiu Long, Zihao Zhang et al. · Oct 24

SkillQR automatically discovers reasoning strategies from a retrieval corpus to dynamically route queries to appropriate rewriting strategies at inference time.

For you: Actionable pre-retrieval query rewriting technique that adapts to specific corpora without expensive training data.

Abstract

Dense retrievers provide grounded evidence that mitigates hallucinations in outputs from Large Language Models (LLMs). Real-world queries are often verbose, ambiguously framed, or require multi-step reasoning, introducing irrelevant details that weaken semantic matching for dense retrievers. Query rewriting is a widely adopted pre-retrieval technique that leverages LLMs to reformulate queries and bridge the semantic gap between such queries and the retrieval target. Existing rewriting approaches either rely on expensive proprietary models, substantial training data, or iterative retrieval and rewriting, while largely applying a uniform rewriting strategy regardless of the retrieval corpus. We observe that effective query rewriting instructions are not universal: the optimal strategy depends on the retrieval target, rather than the model alone. We introduce SkillQR, a query rewriting framework that automatically discovers reasoning strategy sets from the retrieval target and dynamically routes a query to the appropriate rewriting strategy at inference time. Without relying on training data, SkillQR achieves competitive performance compared to fine-tuned rewriting models on the BRIGHT and R2MED benchmarks, while also providing lower inference latency than iterative rewriting approaches.

Thinking-in-Bets at Test Time Reduces Sycophantic Revisions in LLM Agents

Nghi D. Q. Bui · Google · Oct 25 · ▲ 19

Introduces Thinking-in-Bets, a training-free method where agents log a claim checklist before reading user feedback, reducing sycophantic reversals across eight benchmarks.

For you: Directly applicable to multi-agent orchestration and chat apps to stop agents from folding under user pressure or incorrect corrections.

Abstract

Language model agents often abandon correct work when a user challenges it, yet simply telling them to stand firm makes them ignore real mistakes. Once an agent reads a complaint, it tends to discount its own earlier checks in order to agree with the user. We introduce Thinking-in-Bets, a training-free method in which an agent writes a short private record of its main claims, the checks supporting them, and its confidence before reading user feedback. When challenged, the agent consults this Claim Checklist to keep verified work intact while investigating uncertain or newly challenged parts. We evaluate five Gemini models (3.5 Flash-Lite, 3.5 Flash, 3.6 Flash, 3.8 Flash, and 4.0 Argon) across eight benchmarks: SWE-Together, SWE-Interact, and DeepSWE for software engineering, Agents' Last Exam for professional reports, Humanity's Last Exam and SycEval for academic reasoning, Vals Finance for financial analysis, and Terminal-Bench for command-line tasks. Recording claims before criticism nearly eliminates false reversals in patch review and protects passing report drafts under mistaken pushback, whereas writing the same record after reading the complaint recovers very little of the gain. In multi-turn coding, pre-reply claim records substantially reduce unnecessary file edits after mistaken complaints, and gating the record so it is written only when the agent is uncertain lowers token overhead while fixing more real bugs than the baseline. Even in non-interactive tasks without user feedback, listing the key claims in a draft and re-checking the weakest one before submission improves both answer accuracy and confidence estimates across all tested models.

ACARE: Agent-Context-Aware Reranker for Iterative Search

Sahel Sharifymoghaddam, Yauhen Babakhin, Reza Esfandiarpoor et al. · Oct 26

Introduces ACARe, a compact 4B LLM reranker that conditions on the agent's broader task and reasoning context rather than just the sub-query. It improves retrieval quality and answer accuracy while reducing search effort.

For you: Directly applicable to improving retrieval accuracy in multi-step RAG and agentic search loops where standard rerankers fail.

Abstract

In agentic search, a document can match an agent-generated sub-query without helping answer the user's original question. Conventional rerankers score documents against the sub-query alone, overlooking the broader task and the agent's reasoning. We introduce the Agent-Context-Aware Reranker (ACARe), a compact 4B LLM trained to rerank documents using this context when available. Across multiple benchmarks and agent families, ACARe improves retrieval quality over its base reranker while reducing search effort. These benefits extend to answer generation: ACARe improves accuracy and reduces searches over no reranking, while matching or exceeding the base reranker's mean accuracy with more precise evidence. It also helps smaller agents narrow the retrieval-quality gap to larger agents, showing how better retrieval tools can make smaller agents more effective. The model, training data, and code will be released soon.

AgentBaton: Efficient Model Collaboration in Agentic Tasks

Junlong Li, Jindong Jiang, Yonggan Fu et al. · Oct 24

Systematically investigates model collaboration via handoffs in long-horizon tasks, discovering that capable-to-efficient handoffs at strategic stages preserve accuracy while lowering costs.

For you: Actionable pattern for cutting LLM operational costs in production multi-agent pipelines by routing tasks between high-end and cheaper models.

Abstract

Solving long-horizon agentic tasks requires language agents to interact with their environments over multiple steps, and reliable performance often comes from the expensive capable models available through cloud providers. Meanwhile, alternative efficient models with inferior performance but a much lower cost are emerging, including lower-tier cloud models and smaller open-weights models deployable on local hardware. This raises a natural question that remains less explored than traditional query-level routing: can capable and efficient models collaborate within a single long-horizon agentic task to substantially reduce cost while preserving task accuracy? In this paper, we systematically investigate key design choices for such collaboration through model handoffs, where one model takes over from another, focusing on handoff direction and context transfer. We find that efficient-to-capable handoffs generally underperform capable-only execution with limited or even no cost reduction, whereas capable-to-efficient handoffs at suitable stages of task progress largely preserve or even improve accuracy while substantially reducing cost. These findings suggest that a one-time, simple, well-timed handoff strategy is good enough for cost-effective agent collaboration, motivating AgentBaton, a lightweight detector that learns when to hand off from recurring patterns in task progress. Across 11 model pairs and two representative benchmarks, AgentBaton reduces API costs by 65.9% on average while largely preserving task accuracy, with accuracy gains of up to 8.8 percentage points over capable-only execution. Experiments across various reasoning-effort settings further show that AgentBaton extends the empirical accuracy-cost Pareto frontier.

SpectRAG: Weighted Chunk Graphs for Token-Budgeted Context Selection in Multi-Hop Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Zafer Sahinoglu, Abdulazeez Mohammed, Buse Yaren Kazangirler et al. · Oct 27

Proposes SpectRAG, a spectral graph-augmented retrieval framework combining weighted chunk graphs, semantic similarity, and spectral relevance propagation for multi-hop RAG.

For you: Provides a concrete graph-based retrieval strategy to handle multi-hop dependencies and reduce context redundancy in enterprise search.

Abstract

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems ground large language models (LLMs) by retrieving external evidence and conditioning generation on the retrieved context. Although dense retrieval is effective for local semantic matching, independently ranked retrieval can return redundant passages, miss cross-chunk dependencies, and provide incomplete evidence for multi-hop reasoning. We propose SpectRAG, a spectral graph-augmented retrieval framework that combines hybrid candidate seeding, weighted chunk graph construction, graph-based expansion, spectral relevance propagation, and token-budgeted context selection. Document chunks are represented by dense semantic embeddings and graph-spectral coordinates derived from a weighted chunk graph whose edges encode semantic similarity, document adjacency, section hierarchy, shared entities, citation links, and similarity between LLM-generated utility questions. At query time, SpectRAG builds a high-recall seed set using exact inner-product search and hybrid relevance signals, expands it over the chunk graph, re-scores candidates using graph proximity and spectral information, and assembles the final context under a fixed token budget. The goal is not merely to retrieve individually similar chunks, but to construct a context that is relevant, diverse, structurally connected, and sufficient for grounded reasoning. Experiments on MultiHopRAG, UltraDomain, and long-document question-answering settings show that SpectRAG improves broad evidence recovery and achieves competitive generation quality against dense, hybrid, HyDE, LightRAG, and NaiveRAG baselines. The results further indicate that graph-spectral structure is particularly useful for inference-oriented and cross-document retrieval, while highlighting trade-offs in early precision, computational cost, and domain-dependent performance.
