Agent Memory as Recommendation: Recommender-System Design for Long-Term Memory of LLM Agents

Xinyu Zhang, Weiwei Sun · Oct 25

They formalized LLM agent long-term memory retrieval as budgeted slate recommendation, introducing AgentMemRec to handle candidate generation, learning-to-rank, and slate construction within per-request cost budgets.

For you: Directly applicable to improving retrieval, context windows, and cost efficiency in long-running production agent systems.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents that serve users across many sessions must choose, for every request, the few stored memories that enter a limited context window. This choice largely decides whether the agent answers correctly, and it runs on every request at Web scale. This selection faces three challenges: a large and growing store, a per-request cost budget, and multi-session questions that need all of their evidence. Many memory systems build notes or graphs with hundreds to thousands of LLM calls per history, and each is designed on its own, so it is unclear which component yields the accuracy. These challenges mirror the properties of recommendation, so we formalize agent memory as budgeted slate recommendation, with the request as the user, stored memories as items and the context window as a slate. The formalization places memory systems in one design space of recommender cascades and factorizes accuracy into the completeness that each stage controls. Guided by it, we propose AgentMemRec, which adapts candidate generation, learning to rank and slate construction from recommender systems and selects memories without any LLM call. On multi-session questions of LongMemEval, AgentMemRec outperforms memory and context-selection methods, including A-Mem, HippoRAG 2, DynamicRAG and T-Mem, by 10 to 31 points of accuracy. It matches an LLM reranker that makes 64 calls per query at 3.2 times lower serving cost, and on LoCoMo it outperforms Mem0 and Zep without any LLM call at write time. The factorization attributes the gains to slates that more often hold all of the evidence, so recommender-system design offers an effective and efficient foundation for the long-term memory of LLM agents.

CalibraRL: Confidence-Calibrated Reinforcement Learning for Mitigating Hallucinations in RAG

Qianchi Zhang, Hainan Zhang, Liang Pang et al. · Oct 26

They proposed CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework combining correctness utility with a Brier-based confidence penalty and Beta posterior abstention rewards to mitigate hallucinations in RAG.

For you: Directly tackles hallucination reduction, confidence calibration, and reliable abstention logic for production RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.

Your Wish Is My Commit: Over-Compliant Refactoring in AI Coding Agents

Haocong Jiang, Sadra Sabouri, Sepand Haghighi et al. · Oct 26

Controlled study of AI coding agents across 3,000 turns reveals they suffer from over-compliance (sycophancy), repeatedly rewriting clean codebases up to 31x without stopping.

For you: Crucial for designing reliable multi-agent systems and preventing runaway API cost loops caused by agent verbosity.

Abstract

Large language models are known to be sycophantic, over-complying with users even when doing so is unproductive. When these models act as coding agents, this can be costly: an agent that keeps editing after no edit improves the code writes churn, technical debt, and review burden into the codebase. Yet the consequences of sycophancy in software engineering remain largely understudied. To understand them, we run a controlled study of iterative refactoring, an open-ended task with no stopping criterion, where any edit an agent makes once the code is already clean reflects compliance rather than improvement. Across algorithmic (n=50) and object-oriented (n=10) suites, we repeatedly ask two widely used agents, Codex and Claude Code, to refactor further across five models. Over 3,000 turns, we define six behavioral signals of over-compliance. We hypothesized that a capable agent would stop once it recognized an optimized codebase; none did. Every model kept editing, rewriting up to 31x the original codebase before first stopping and resuming edits even after declaring the task complete. Over-compliance is a costly, multi-turn behavior that compounds with every turn, and our six signals give developers and tool builders a way to detect and curb it.

Rethinking Verification in Agent Harnesses

Zhengxin Zhang, Erkang Zhu, Yin Lin et al. · Oct 24 · ▲ 9

Analyzes 17 verification designs across four harness layers (instructions, reminders, gates, critics) for coding agents; independent critic review gives best accuracy‑cost trade‑off, modest gains from prompts and reminders, larger gains but higher cost from completion gates.

For you: Guides adding cheap verification steps to improve reliability of your RAG agents without large latency.

Abstract

Agent harnesses enable large language models to act through tools, observe the results, and iteratively work toward a task goal. Verification checks and revises agents' work to improve output quality. Yet there is limited guidance on how harnesses should organize this effort. We study verification as an axis of test-time scaling by examining when to intervene, who checks the work, and how checking leads to revision. Our framework spans four layers of the agent lifecycle: initial instructions, mid-turn reminders, completion gates, and independent review. We compare 17 designs across seven frontier coding models on Terminal-Bench (TB) and SWE-bench Verified (SWE), and introduce VisualBench for expert evaluation of visual and interactive artifacts. Initial instructions and execution-time reminders yield modest, model-dependent average gains on the coding benchmarks, without substantially increasing average output token use. Completion gates can yield larger gains but may substantially increase cost. Independent review by a critic offers a more favorable accuracy-cost tradeoff, improving average accuracy by 5.9-9.5 percentage points on TB and 3.9-6.6 on SWE at 1.13-1.25 and 1.00-1.04 times baseline cost, respectively. On VisualBench, independent review achieves net preferences of 13.2-23.8 points over the baseline across its four designs, exceeding the evaluated L0-L2 designs. Additional review rounds or independent critics further improve average TB accuracy by up to 3.5 percentage points. Together, these results provide empirical guidance for verification design in agent harnesses and support independent review as a practical axis of test-time scaling.

What Effort Level Should I Choose? On the Economics of Reasoning Effort in LLM Agents

Kaiser Sun, Yongchan Kwon, Zain Hasan et al. · Together · Oct 24 · ▲ 15

Study shows higher reasoning effort can cost up to 42× without consistent accuracy gains, revealing trade‑offs between compute and performance.

For you: Guides cost‑effective setting of compute budgets for agentic inference.

Abstract

Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.

EXCISE: Locating and Excising Distracting Sentences in Medical Question Answering

Yerim Hong, Sohyun Son, Heeseung Chung et al. · Oct 26

EXCISE is an inference-time method that detects and blocks distracting sentences from hidden states, gaining 16.7 to 22.8 accuracy points on medical QA with targeted distractors.

For you: Relevant inference-time intervention technique for improving grounding and robustness against noisy context in RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Language models are increasingly considered for clinical decision support, where their answers should be grounded in clinical evidence and remain robust to irrelevant information. Prior work shows that a single irrelevant sentence can lower the accuracy of medical models, but not where inside the model its effect can be removed, or how to do so without knowing which sentence it is. We find that blocking attention to a known distractor restores the answer, whereas head masking can suppress correct answers. We also show that the model's hidden states mark the distractor as the sentence that least fits the case even when the model follows it. To remove a distractor's effect without knowing which sentence it is, we propose EXCISE, an inference-time method that blocks the sentence most influencing the answer, but only if the model's hidden states mark it as inconsistent with the case. On MedQA-TD (MedQA with Targeted Distractors), a benchmark we construct in which each distractor targets a specific wrong option, EXCISE gains 16.7-22.8 accuracy points at a 1.5-2.1-point cost on the same questions without distractors, outperforming prompting, rewriting, and steering baselines for all three models. Its gains extend to MedDistractQA and BiasMedQA, as well as chain-of-thought generation.

CoS-VQ: Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization for Large Language Models

Zukang Xu, Zhixiong Zhao, Xing Hu et al. · Oct 27

Proposed CoS-VQ, a vector quantization method that preserves low-dimensional codewords while jointly optimizing indices and shared scale via a tangent separable upper bound.

For you: Directly applicable for reducing memory footprints and optimizing cost/latency when deploying large language models in production.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) require substantial memory, motivating low-bit weight quantization for efficient deployment. Existing vector quantization (VQ) methods often improve accuracy by increasing the effective coding dimension, which can complicate independent lookup and local codeword updates. We propose CoS-VQ (Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization), which preserves low-dimensional codewords while jointly optimizing hard indices and their shared scale across positions. The key challenge is scale-induced coupling: the optimal scale depends on the entire codeword combination and, in turn, changes candidate rankings across positions, so pointwise updates can stall even after scale re-optimization. CoS-VQ collects candidates at multiple probe scales and constructs a tangent separable upper bound for the residual-aware quadratic objective. By reducing candidate comparisons to lower envelopes of lines over positive scales, it identifies finitely many representative intervals for joint index--scale updates without exhaustively enumerating codeword combinations. On Qwen3.8-27B, CoS-VQ outperforms QTIP and LLVQ on the evaluated language modeling, coding, and reasoning benchmarks. Ablations from the same initialization further show larger gains from joint updates than from scale-only refinement, highlighting joint selection as an alternative to increasing codeword dimensionality. The code is available at https://github.com/xzktx003/Cos_VQ.

From Ripple to Boundary: Deterministic Impact Boundaries for LLM-Based Software Evolution

Jiameng Bai, Zhitao Wang, Long Wang et al. · Oct 26

Replaced probabilistic semantic retrieval with deterministic structural constraints using a Merkle DAG for design asset evolution, stopping ripple effect hallucinations.

For you: Directly relevant for handling design documents and engineering specs in enterprise RAG without hallucinating scope changes.

Abstract

The landscape of software evolution is undergoing a fundamental paradigm shift in the era of Large Language Models. As industrial software engineering increasingly transitions toward design-driven code generation, the bottleneck of evolution has shifted from writing code to maintaining deterministic consistency between multi-source design assets and the generated code. When a design asset changes, LLM agents currently lack a deterministic impact boundary to identify which code must be evolved, leading to two failure modes: hallucinating missing changes or breaking unrelated code. Probabilistic approaches, such as semantic retrieval or text-based diffing, fail to capture the structural ripple effects across highly interconnected design dependencies. We propose a paradigm shift from probabilistic semantic retrieval to deterministic structural constraints. We formalize the design assets as a Knowledge Graph and organize them into a content-addressed Merkle Directed Acyclic Graph. Our key insight reveals a topological isomorphism between the logical ripple effect of a design change and the content hash propagation within the DAG. By leveraging this, we transform ambiguous impact analysis into verifiable structural operations. Through a two-phase diffing process, we extract a precise causal chain that defines the exact modification boundary. This boundary enables deterministic context injection, providing the LLM agent with strictly scoped code and design updates, thereby fundamentally guaranteeing evolution safety. As a by-product, the KG serves as an agent-queryable knowledge base for natural language design retrieval. Early deployment on a large-scale industrial payment platform demonstrates that our mechanism successfully computes the exact impact boundary and generates incremental code modifications that meet the expected design changes.

MLE-World: Predicting Machine Learning Experiment Outcomes with a Learned World Model

Brian Ko, Han Wang, Hejia Geng et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 5

Presents MLE-World, a lightweight world model that predicts ML experiment outputs to enable affordable test-time scaling for autonomous agents.

For you: Demonstrates a pattern of using cheap world models to simulate execution and enable test-time scaling for software-writing agents.

Abstract

AI research agents can propose, execute, and improve programs that solve machine learning tasks. However, executing each candidate program requires training and evaluating ML models, limiting how many candidates an agent can explore at each step. We introduce MLE-World, a world model finetuned from Qwen3.5-4B that predicts a program's terminal output (error traces, training logs, and validation scores) from its code and data description without executing it, together with a confidence score estimating the prediction's correctness. Because a prediction costs one inference call rather than a training run, MLE-World makes test time scaling (TTS) affordable for these agents. We build five TTS policies, guided by MLE-World, that compare several candidates per step by their predicted outputs: Sequential revision, Best-of-N, Pairwise judging, Fusion, and a UCB style rule that also adds an exploration bonus from this confidence score. Since predictions can be wrong, we run a candidate for real only when its confidence score is low. On 17 MLE-bench Lite tasks, MLE-World matches the real terminal output on 77.9% of held out programs, versus 41.1% for much larger prompted models. MLE-World guided TTS achieves up to 22.1% higher observed mean performance than the agent without a world model, with less real execution. Project webpage: https://mle-world.github.io/.

Remembering Is Not Enough: Refining Memories and Learning When to Use Them in Multi-Agent Systems

Yipeng Hao, Yuxuan Cai, Wenkai Ji et al. · Oct 26

Proposed DiscernMem, a training-free framework that refines flawed memories and learns contextual boundaries for when agents should use them.

For you: Directly applicable to improving memory retention and reliability in multi-agent enterprise workflows.

Abstract

Memory helps LLM-based multi-agent systems reuse experience, but stored advice can be flawed or applied in the wrong situation. Team-level success or failure alone cannot reliably show whether a memory needs correction or should not have been used. We propose DiscernMem, a training-free framework that improves both memory quality and memory use. To improve quality, Defect-Aware Memory Refinement (DMR) compares team executions with a single memory and with no memory, diagnoses rule defects, and tests repairs on current and earlier tasks to guard against losing previously useful behavior. To guide appropriate use, Boundary-Learning Meta-Memory (BLM) records each memory's past uses and effects, learning when its owner role should apply or avoid it based on task context and incoming messages. Together, these components help teams correct flawed experience while retaining useful rules that were applied incorrectly. Experiments with Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on four benchmarks show that DiscernMem achieves the highest average score on AutoGen, CAMEL, and MacNet, exceeding the strongest reported baselines by 0.51-4.32 points. It leads on code generation and symbolic planning across all three systems, including a PDDL score of 37.71 versus 28.48 on CAMEL.

When Connectivity Misleads: Guiding Agentic Search with Contrastive Evidence Paths

Junchen Li, Liang Xu, Rongzheng Wang et al. · Oct 26

Proposed Contrastive Evidence Paths (CEP) to mitigate structural graph bias and rank candidates by strong individual paths in cross-document graph search.

For you: Provides a concrete technique to improve graph-based retrieval and agentic search over document corpora.

Abstract

Search agents can use cross-document graphs to discover sources through multi-hop connections and choose subsequent actions based on candidate rankings and relationships within a limited observation window. However, documents may rank highly because their numerous connections make them easier to reach, without necessarily providing more valuable evidence. Graph diffusion methods such as Personalized PageRank (PPR) aggregate propagation contributions from multiple sources, allowing highly connected distractor nodes to crowd the observation window. Flat node scores also obscure cross-document evidence trails, limiting continued exploration. We propose Contrastive Evidence Paths (CEP), which scores candidates by their strongest individual paths on a query-conditioned document graph and calibrates these scores against the same graph's background visitation distribution to mitigate structural bias. CEP ranks candidates by the calibrated scores and retains the corresponding source paths. Building on CEP, we develop Structured Corpus Interaction (SCI), which organizes candidates and their connecting paths into a structured Map, enabling agents to verify source documents and pursue missing information through expansion from anchor documents. Experiments on BrowseComp-Plus and TREC-COVID show that SCI achieves state-of-the-art answer accuracy and nDCG@10 with the lowest generation cost. Compared with the strongest baselines by task performance, maximum reductions in tool calls and total generation costs exceed 50% and 40%, respectively. Further analysis confirms that SCI with CEP suppresses ranking inflation induced by structural connections and improves gold recall and exploration efficiency.

Bridging Search Intent and Tabular Data in Dataset Search

Wenjing Lin, Madelon Hulsebos · Oct 24

Introduced Hypothetical Schema Embeddings (HySE), using LLMs to generate task-aligned hypothetical table schemas to bridge search intent and tabular data.

For you: Directly applicable technique for improving tabular data retrieval and dataset search within RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Dataset search remains a bottleneck in data analysis workflows, as existing systems largely rely on keyword matching or shallow retrieval that fail to capture user intent. To facilitate intent-driven search, we present two retrieval components to equip multi-faceted dataset search interfaces through natural language. First, we propose Hypothetical Schema Embeddings (HySE) that uses a language model to generate task-aligned hypothetical table schemas which are used for embedding-based retrieval, bridging the gap between intent and table content. We introduce KaggleDS, a corpus comprising 6,503 tables and diverse task-queries derived from Kaggle, and show that HySE consistently improves recall over strong keyword and embedding-based baselines, as well as over a dense table retriever fine-tuned on KaggleDS. We also show that HySE generalizes to end-to-end question answering on, for example, the NQ-Tables dataset, a corpus of 169,898 Wikipedia tables with human-written queries, where HySE improves Recall@10 by +6.0 pp over dense retrieval baselines. Second, we introduce FlexPrune, which translates dataset constraints expressed in natural-language into SQL predicates over a customizable metadata index, enabling filtering across arbitrary metadata attributes. Combined with HySE, FlexPrune increases Recall@10 by +6.0 pp over dense retrieval followed by tag-based filtering. Our results show that natural-language-based retrieval supporting task and metadata queries offers a viable alternative to schema-level keyword search in dataset search.

LLM Hallucination Detection via Low-rank Multilinear Decomposition

Devansh Sharma, Faizul Rakib Sayem, Shahana Ibrahim · Oct 26

Proposed LOTUS, a low-rank multilinear decomposition framework that extracts compact representations from Transformer hidden states for hallucination detection.

For you: Directly relevant for building robust evaluation and reliability guardrails in production LLM and RAG systems.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) frequently generate fluent yet factually incorrect responses, raising concerns about their reliability. Recent hallucination detectors increasingly exploit internal representations, but extracting informative signals from high-dimensional hidden states remains challenging. Existing approaches often rely on selected activations or pooling-based compression, potentially overlooking discriminative information distributed across Transformer layers, feature modes, and tokens. We propose LOTUS (Low-rank Order-statistic TUcker-based Subspace learning), a multilinear low-rank decomposition framework for hallucination detection that extracts compact representations from LLM hidden states while reducing dependence on labeled data. Our approach first summarizes variable-length token representations using complementary statistical descriptors that capture peak activations, activation distributions, and cross-layer changes. We then leverage low-rank multilinear decomposition to extract compact representations that capture the underlying structure of LLM hidden states, which are subsequently used to train a lightweight hallucination classifier. Experiments across multiple LLMs and question-answering benchmarks demonstrate improved hallucination detection performance over recent state-of-the-art baselines.

Secure and Functional Code Generation with Self-Evaluation

Md Afif Al Mamun, Gias Uddin · Oct 23

SecGent uses runtime retrieval from the CWE database to inject security constraints during code generation, repairing vulnerabilities without retraining.

For you: Demonstrates an inference-time RAG pattern for injecting compliance/security rules into LLM generation without expensive fine-tuning.

Abstract

Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate functionally correct code that remains insecure. Security-focused fine-tuning can be expensive and is not always available, motivating inference-time approaches. Security-oriented prompting and retrieval can reduce vulnerabilities, but may also remove required behavior or apply defenses incompatible with the task. The challenge is to identify relevant security knowledge and use it to improve security without sacrificing functionality. We present SecGent, an inference-time framework that retrieves weakness context from the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) database and applies it to secure code generation. By connecting task specifications to relevant textual CWE records, SecGent provides task-specific security guidance without relying on retrieved vulnerable or patched code examples. Complementary functional and security tests check the resulting implementation, and their execution feedback guides repairs toward satisfying both objectives. We evaluate SecGent with four open-source LLMs and Claude Sonnet 5.5 across five programming languages on the CWEval benchmark. It achieves the highest joint functional--security success against four inference-time baselines for all five models. Across the open-weight models, mean functional-security@1 (FS@1) improves by 15.5% over RAG, with gains in both functionality and security, and by 19.8% over the strongest prompt optimizer compiled for each model. On Sonnet, FS@1 reaches 92.1%, outperforming the strongest baseline AutoGen by 4.8%. In a separate comparison, SecGent outperforms Claude Code with security-focused prompting by over 6% in FS@1. Feedback-guided repair further improves FS@1 by up to 6.7% over generation without feedback, primarily by recovering functionality while checking security.

GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents

Zack Yu, Nicholas Lee, Minjae Lee et al. · Oct 27

GEPA-Curator filters out noisy or stochastic agent rollout failures during prompt optimization and selectively retrieves past successful trajectories as context.

For you: Provides a practical strategy for agent prompt optimization by filtering noisy training signals and leveraging successful historical trajectories.

Abstract

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $τ$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.

MiRL: Error-Targeted Reinforcement Learning for Correcting Terminal Agent Misbehaviors

Runchu Tian, Zaid Khan, Zun Wang et al. · Oct 26

MiRL profiles agent execution errors to find recurring misbehaviors, then trains the policy using RL starting rollouts at those specific failure steps to suppress wrong actions.

For you: Directly applicable technique for debugging and hardening multi-turn enterprise agents against repetitive execution failures.

Abstract

Error-targeted learning gives a legible way to improve a model: identify specific weaknesses of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. We study how to apply error-targeted learning to terminal agents, which solve long-horizon tasks specified in natural language by interacting with a command shell. Existing error-targeted methods are designed for single-turn tasks, and existing analyses of agent failures are used to evaluate and debug agents rather than to train them. We propose Misbehavior-driven RL (MiRL), which first profiles the errors of a policy to find generalizable misbehaviors: kinds of wrong steps that recur across many environments. MiRL then trains the policy with RL, starting rollouts at the steps where these misbehaviors occur and rewarding a rollout only if it passes the tests and avoids the misbehavior. On Qwen3.5-4B, eight misbehaviors account for 52.1% of wrong steps. After training with MiRL, the policy repeats the measured misbehaviors 43% to 57% less often on average on unseen prefixes. MiRL outperforms TMax-4B on Terminal-Bench Lite (51.8% vs. 47.4%) and matches it on Terminal-Bench 2.1 (21.3% vs. 20.6%), while using 35% of its rollouts. These results show that error-targeted learning can be applied to realistic, long-horizon terminal tasks.

Does Your Generalist Skill Optimizer Really Incentivize Agentic Tasks? Rethinking Skill Optimization via Bipartite Attribution

Jiawei Zhou, Bo Peng, Yuxuan Huang et al. · Oct 27

Analyzing agent skill optimizers with BiPA reveals that dev-set gains often vanish on test sets due to noise and token inflation, questioning current optimization practices.

For you: Critical evaluation insight for validating whether automated agent prompt or skill tuning actually improves robustness rather than overfitting.

Abstract

Skill optimizers adapt an agent to long-horizon tasks, such as repairing a terminal environment, by rewriting its skill, a natural-language procedure in its context, and keeping the edits that raise success on a small dev set. On such tasks success is noisy, so a gain on a small dev set does not show that an edit helps, and a success score does not show what the edit costs. We introduce BiPA, which replays an optimizer's acceptance rule on candidates identical to its seed to measure what selection alone reports, and records each episode as a bipartite graph of turns and the text they read to trace extra input tokens to the behavior behind them. Across three optimizers, three agentic benchmarks and two models, the learned skills succeed about as often as their hand-written seed, 0.1 points higher on average. Their dev gains vanish on test: keeping the best of several candidates on at most 30 tasks reports gains this large even when none changed. They read about 16% more input tokens: the optimizers almost always add rules, half to re-check work and some to act one call at a time, and the agent follows them as extra turns, each re-sending the whole conversation. Four principles follow: accept an edit only if its gain exceeds what selection alone reports; charge it for the turns it adds, not for its length; let the editor delete and merge rules; show it what earlier rules cost. Code is available at https://github.com/valleysprings/BiPA.git.

Stratum: Repository Memory for Coding Agents Beyond the Context Window

Yuvraj Sehgal, Mahsa Panahandeh, Naser Ezzati-Jivan · Oct 26

Proposed Stratum, a persistent repository memory architecture for coding agents that decouples long-term experience from costly model KV caches.

For you: Directly addresses memory and context limitations in multi-session enterprise coding and multi-agent systems.

Abstract

Context windows have grown a thousand-fold, yet coding agents still start every session with no memory of their past work. They re-read files, retry failed approaches, and forget root causes they established hours earlier. This is not a context-length problem but a memory-architecture problem. Our hypothesis: coding agents need persistent, experience-aware repository memory, so that knowledge survives from one session to the next. Three measurements on real repositories motivate the design. First, mid-size projects already exceed the largest production context windows, and storing knowledge inside the model's key-value (KV) cache costs $10^4$ to $10^5$ times more bytes than storing it as indexed text. Second, across 752 real bug-fix commits, the median fix edits two files, and these files plus their direct imports amount to less than 10% of the repository; the bottleneck is selection, not capacity. Third, even with transitive import reachability and test links, 47% of co-edited file pairs remain structurally disconnected; the knowledge needed for selection must be mined from history and experience. We sketch Stratum, a tiered memory that lives with the repository and serves it through page-safe tools: every response states what was returned, what remains, and what it cost. Retrieval over a store of one million nodes takes 1.7 ms at the median. In a multi-session experiment on eight real bugs, a fresh agent that inherited a prior session's memory, written by a different model, identified the root-cause file in 21 of 24 trials versus 6 of 24 for a retrieval-only baseline (Fisher $p<10^{-4}$), at 4.5x lower median token cost. With the notes of all eight investigations pooled in one store, success remained 8/8.

PinGER: Token-Efficient Generative Retrieval with Semantic IDs

Xinyi Li, Sujan Reddy Ale, Yi-Ping Hsu et al. · Oct 23

Introduces PinGER, a generative retriever that uses asymmetric attention masks to pack semantic IDs into dense token histories, serving one token per item at scale.

For you: Interesting alternative retrieval architecture for improving context efficiency and latency in large-scale enterprise search systems.

Abstract

Generative retrieval replaces nearest-neighbor search with token-by-token decoding of an item's semantic ID (SID). But existing SID-based methods primarily use L levels of tokens sequentially to represent each item in the input history. As a result, a fixed context window holds L times fewer items than it would with one embedding per item. We present PinGER, a generative retriever deployed at Pinterest that retains SID outputs while making input histories compact, built on three ideas. (1) An asymmetric attention mask over interleaved SID and dense tokens lets later items attend only to the dense ones, so the model is supervised at every SID position in training yet serves one token per item while computing the same function. (2) Generation is controllable through goal-conditioned outputs, reinforcement learning, and a prefix-diversity penalty, which together steer retrieval toward surface-specific goals and diverse candidates. (3) A serving stack built for efficient wide-beam generation is paired with decoding constrained to valid identifiers and candidate resolution from a host-local index. Deployed in Related Pins, PinGER lifts saves of fresh Pins by 3.60% and overall saves by 0.62%, while reducing the surface's end-to-end latency by 0.7% and eliminating the cost of the nearest-neighbor search it replaces.

Did the Model Use That? Counterfactual Training Boosts Self-Attribution in Language Models

Zikai Zhou, Jiacheng Sang, Yilin Xu et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 6

Introduced CREDIT, a counterfactual training method that supervises final-answer self-attribution to explicitly teach language models when external information influences their responses.

For you: Useful technique for improving grounding and citation reliability in enterprise RAG applications.

Abstract

A fundamental challenge for explainable and accountable language models is revealing how external information influences their answers. This paper examines whether models are self-attributive: that is, whether they indicate when -- and only when -- their responses to questions are influenced by information provided alongside the question. Furthermore, we explore how self-attribution can be improved through counterfactual training, which explicitly teaches models to distinguish between answers that do and do not depend on supplied information. We found that most of the eleven frontier models rarely attribute influential information in their answers, and the rest attribute non-influential information. Training acknowledgment into reasoning does not fix this: the acknowledgment does not reach the final answer. We introduce CREDIT, which directly supervises final-answer self-attribution with counterfactual labels, without the need for human annotation. CREDIT substantially improves attribution in four open models, including two distilled reasoning models, and outperforms reasoning-level fine-tuning and prompting baselines. Among answers that agree with a user's suggestion, CREDIT distinguishes those changed by the suggestion from those the model would have given anyway. Gains transfer partly to unseen information types and prompts containing sources that support conflicting answers.

RouteDRAG: Learning to Route and Control Agentic Execution for Multimodal Document RAG

Zekai Chen, Hao Zhu, Yu Liu et al. · Oct 26

RouteDRAG uses a graph-backed multimodal document index and adaptive path routing to predict when agentic execution is necessary for long document RAG.

For you: Addresses cost-accuracy tradeoffs in multimodal document processing and agentic RAG pipelines you can apply directly.

Abstract

Web-based applications increasingly rely on understanding long multimodal documents, where answering questions often requires combining information across pages, tables, and figures. Agentic retrieval-augmented generation addresses these tasks through iterative retrieval and reasoning. However, Agentic execution is not always necessary, introducing additional computation and potentially incorrect answers. Our empirical analysis reveals that Agentic execution provides query-dependent benefits, while additional operations may improve, preserve, or degrade answer accuracy. These findings motivate controlling both Agent invocation and subsequent execution. To address these challenges, we propose RouteDRAG, a learning-based framework comprising four components. Unified Document Index organizes multimodal content into a graph-backed index and derives query representations from initial retrieval. Adaptive Path Routing preserves exact answering and predicts the relative benefit of Agent over shorter execution paths. Dynamic Agent Execution maintains an evolving workspace and refines Planner proposals to control tool use and termination. Sequential Policy Learning first trains operation control from observed outcomes and then trains path routing using the resulting Agent's performance. Across two benchmarks, RouteDRAG achieves an average accuracy improvement of 3.47 percentage points over shorter-path-only execution while assigning only 79.3% of queries to Agent. Notably, it also surpasses uniform Agent execution on both benchmarks while requiring fewer answering calls. Our code is now available at https://github.com/Ereshkigal1/RouteDRAG.

EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation

Yitao Xiao, Xu Zhang, Ye Cheng et al. · Oct 26

EvoRouter uses on-policy distillation of execution insights to jointly optimize task performance and computational cost in multi-agent routing.

For you: Provides a practical blueprint for cost-efficient multi-agent orchestration and routing in production environments.

Abstract

Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. However, the resulting configuration space grows combinatorially, while sparse execution rewards provide limited fine-grained supervision for interdependent planning decisions. We propose EvoRouter, a self-evolving multi-agent routing framework driven by insight-guided on-policy distillation. A dedicated expert periodically synthesizes performance-cost feedback from configuration trajectories across tasks into reusable conditional routing insights about capability requirements, returns from resource scaling, and interactions among configuration choices. A privileged teacher combines these evolving insights with current execution feedback to provide dense token-level supervision for the router's own planning trajectories through on-policy distillation. Cost-aware reinforcement learning jointly optimizes task performance and computational cost. Together, these training signals internalize execution insights into the routing policy. The updated policy generates new trajectories that support subsequent insight extraction, forming an execution-driven self-improvement loop. We evaluate EvoRouter on 19 in-domain datasets spanning five domains and two out-of-domain benchmarks. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. Our code is open-sourced at https://github.com/CenYangtze/EvoRouter.

Agentic AI Orchestration Framework for Spatial Intelligence: Supply Chain Deployment

Constantinos Papantoniou, Brian Hilton · Oct 25

A reference architecture for spatial intelligence multi-agent orchestration achieved a 50% runtime reduction and 60% lower decision latency in a supply chain deployment.

For you: Offers concrete architectural patterns for enterprise multi-agent orchestration, human-in-the-loop controls, and data contracts.

Abstract

Industrial spatial decisions are assembled across systems that share neither a reference frame nor an owner, and analysts spend substantial effort reconciling those systems rather than reasoning about space. This paper presents ANASSA, a reference architecture integrating structured reasoning, multi-agent workflow orchestration, authoritative spatial grounding, and feedback-driven execution, and reports its instantiation in an operating supply-chain deployment. The architecture places typed data contracts at agent boundaries, validates spatial claims against a certified authoritative foundation, and enforces two blocking human-in-the-loop approvals inside a six-step cognitive loop. The deployment covers multi-hazard risk assessment on an ocean-and-inland freight lane, including cold-chain excursion detection over container telemetry. Across a three-month proof of concept (POC) with three implementation iterations, three deployment outcomes are reported: 25% improvement in data quality, 50% reduction in spatial workflow runtime, and 60% lower decision latency relative to the preceding manual workflow. Absolute deployment-scale quantities and baseline counts are confidential; reported percentages are observed relative changes. These are uncontrolled deployment observations, not controlled experiments. We therefore specify instrumentation and ablation protocols for mechanism-level evaluation.

Adaptive Retrieval, Selective Answers: Evaluating Conformal Factuality in Agentic RAG

Muhammad Aimal Rehman, Chi-Kuang Yeh · Oct 25

Evaluated claim-level conformal factuality in agentic RAG, showing how target levels trade off full support rates against answer availability.

For you: Useful evaluation and reliability technique for bounding hallucination and ensuring grounded outputs in production RAG systems.

Abstract

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can improve factual grounding without guaranteeing that every generated claim is supported. Previous work has studied claim-level conformal factuality in single-stage RAG and, more recently, multi-hop RAG. We examine its behavior in agentic RAG, where a language-model policy adaptively issues follow-up retrieval queries or terminates retrieval before response generation. We retain the retrieval-based relevance-scoring formulation and post-generation conformal filtering and evaluate six model-dataset configurations: GPT-6 Luna and Llama 3.1 8B on HotpotQA, Natural Questions, and TriviaQA. Each configuration uses 60 fixed questions, 100 seeded 30/30 calibration-test partitions, five conformal target levels, and a no-conformal-prediction baseline. Across the six configurations, higher target levels are accompanied by higher observed full-support rates and reduced answer availability. At the 95% target, non-empty response coverage ranges from 17.23% to 60.17%, claim retention from 10.72% to 43.16%, and full support among non-empty outputs from 89.14% to 95.56%. We formulate the adaptive retrieval trajectory explicitly and characterize the reliability-informativeness trade-off when conformal factuality is applied after agent termination.

Beyond Benchmarks: Can Small Open-Weight Models Get Real Coding Work Done?

Vaibhav Jain · Oct 24 · ▲ 11

An engineering retrospective on serving small open-weight coding models using vLLM, SGLang, and llama.cpp, highlighting real-world friction in tool calls and cache reuse.

For you: Extremely relevant practical insights on serving open-weight models, KV caching, and orchestrating coding agents in production.

Abstract

Making an open-weight language model generate text is not the same as making it finish coding work. This retrospective empirical systems experience report follows my engineering work from July to October 2026 across coding clients, model-specific serving recipes, vLLM, SGLang, llama.cpp, and MLX. Models behind a nominally compatible endpoint still differed in tool-call formatting, turn control, context behavior, cache reuse, and reliability. A served model, coherent text, executed tool calls, and code that passes independent behavioral checks proved to be distinct levels of progress. The evidence includes 4,165 coding-agent gateway turns from my own sessions, 623 debug jobs, and three separate delegation studies with hidden behavioral checks and a fixed Claude Opus 5.5 parent. Saved cold-prefix requests had a median time to first token of about 69.4 s, versus 2.8 s for high-reuse requests (observational). In a 45-trial study, direct coding passed 15/15 and delegation to a quantized Qwen3-Coder 30B-A3B worker passed 12/15 and 14/15. On a second 15-task suite under a fixed artifact contract, the completed replacement cohort had 45/45 for direct coding and 12/45 for a Nemotron Cascade 2 worker (11 worker substitutions). Sixteen sessions stopped by a subscription limit before any model call were replaced; counting them as failures gives 37/45 and 8/45. A Qwen3-Coder worker passed 0/45. In both studies with a direct arm, direct coding had lower API list-price-equivalent frontier cost per verified success. A post-hoc diagnostic found that 19 of 36 artifacts rejected only for renamed parameters passed the hidden checks unchanged; they are not counted as successes. The contribution is an evidence-qualified account of serving, agent protocol, session continuity, artifact validation, and verification in practice. The studies are not pooled and do not rank models or runtimes.

DocSkill: Skill-Integrated Agentic Reasoning for Multilingual Multi-Hop Document Question Answering

Jing Huang, Zhiya Tan, Changtao Miao et al. · Oct 27

Introduces DocSkill, an agentic framework with a learned skill-orchestration policy using MCTS and DPO for multilingual multi-hop document question answering across complex layouts.

For you: Provides advanced techniques for complex document extraction and multi-hop reasoning over unstructured enterprise documents.

Abstract

Multilingual multi-hop document question answering (MMHop-DocQA) requires locating and integrating evidence across complex layouts, document pages, and diverse languages. Existing approaches include single-pass processing and adaptive tool use, but learning to coordinate evidence acquisition, cross-page integration, and answer verification remains challenging. To address this challenge, we present DocSkill, an agentic framework with a learned skill-orchestration policy for adaptive evidence acquisition and reasoning. The policy selects, revisits, or skips skills according to the evolving evidence state. To construct supervision for skill orchestration, bounded Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) explores alternative skill trajectories offline. These trajectories provide demonstrations for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and preference pairs for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). We further introduce MMHop-DocBench, a multilingual multi-hop document QA dataset with training and test splits, containing 60K QA pairs across 10 languages and eight document domains. Its HopGen pipeline builds hierarchical page-element graphs to derive traceable cross-page evidence paths and generate grounded QA pairs with page- and element-level evidence annotations. Experiments show that DocSkill with Qwen3-VL-8B achieves 41.0 accuracy and 49.2 F1 on MMHop-DocBench, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 5.5 and 6.3 points, respectively, and achieving competitive performance on MMLongBench-Doc and DocBench.

From Questions to Queries to Insights: A Schema-Grounded Agentic Workflow for Natural-Language Crash Data Analysis

Jingwen Zhu, Keshu Wu, Steven T. Parker et al. · Oct 26

Presents CrashDB-QA, a schema-grounded agentic workflow for natural-language database querying that uses specialized components to interpret questions, generate SQL, and validate results.

For you: A practical reference architecture for building enterprise text-to-SQL agents connected to structured relational databases.

Abstract

Transportation agencies maintain extensive crash databases to identify safety priorities, evaluate countermeasures, and guide resource allocation. Making these data accessible to practitioners requires flexible ways to translate analytical questions into reliable results. Existing dashboards support predefined analyses, while additional requests often depend on specialists who understand database queries, coded fields, and agency conventions. This study presents CrashDB-QA, a fully local, schema-grounded agentic workflow that enables natural-language analysis of crash databases. Specialized components interpret questions, generate and validate read-only SQL, execute queries, and explain the results. A shared knowledge base grounds query construction in the agency's data dictionary and safety-analysis conventions, while recorded intermediate outputs support inspection and auditing. An optional extension, CrashDB-QA+, checks the alignment among the question, query, and execution result and supplies evidence for iterative repair. We evaluate the workflow on a frozen snapshot of 4.4 million Wisconsin crash records using a new benchmark of 300 questions across ten analytical categories and nine local open-weight models. Domain grounding substantially improves query accuracy, and a code-specialized 7B model provides the best accuracy-latency balance among the tested models. Compositional analyses remain more difficult than routine retrieval, particularly comparisons between groups or periods. Verification improves performance when the Judge uses authoritative field definitions and the Refiner receives execution evidence, whereas additional checking alone offers no consistent benefit. These findings demonstrate the potential of locally deployed, domain-grounded workflows to broaden access to crash analysis and identify the reasoning and verification capabilities needed for reliable use.

Constrained Decoding: Harmful or Beneficial? Revisiting Its Impact on LLM Performance

Ngoc Trinh Hung Nguyen, Alonso Silva, Armen Aghasaryan et al. · Nokia · Oct 25 · ▲ 9

Examines constrained decoding across 11 models and shows that format, validity, and reasoning constraints improve reliability and accuracy rather than degrading reasoning.

For you: Directly applicable for improving output reliability and enforcing structured JSON/schema generation in production LLM systems.

Abstract

Constrained decoding guarantees that Large Language Models (LLMs) produce outputs conforming to predefined formats, but is often assumed to inherently degrade reasoning. We examine this assumption through a taxonomy of three constraint types: \emph{format constraints}, which enforce output structure; \emph{validity constraints}, which mask invalid answer; and \emph{reasoning constraints}, which restrict reasoning trajectories. We theoretically show that appropriate constraints can preserve or improve performance by eliminating invalid paths. Across four model families and eleven open-source models, causal ablations on diverse benchmarks using state-of-the-art constrained decoding strategies reveal that apparent performance degradation largely stems from non-equivalent prompting and output formats rather than from decoder-level masking. Meanwhile, validity constraints improve reliability and extractability by eliminating invalid answers, while reasoning constraints can improve reasoning and, consequently, accuracy when they enforce beneficial trajectories. These findings show that the impact of constrained decoding depends on the type of constraint and the output space it removes. Our code is available.

Governed Sovereign RAG for Public Administration: Tripartite Epistemic Routing, Facet-Guided Clarification, and Deterministic Numeric Verification in Citizen Services

Andres Cruz Degante · Oct 26

Evaluates a governed sovereign RAG architecture featuring a tripartite epistemic routing gate and a post-generation deterministic numeric verifier for citizen services.

For you: Valuable patterns for high-stakes enterprise RAG applications requiring strict hallucination guards and deterministic verification of numeric data.

Abstract

Deploying conversational assistants at government service desks faces a tension between regulatory certainty (zero tolerance for invented fees), data sovereignty prohibiting commercial clouds, and high rates of underspecified or out-of-jurisdiction citizen queries. We present and evaluate a governed, sovereign Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) architecture deployed on the official portal of the State of Tlaxcala, Mexico (tlaxcaladigital.gob.mx), covering 1,143 procedures and a decoupled directory of 924 offices. The system governs interactions via three pillars: (1) a two-stage tripartite epistemic gate (clarify_policy and a Boltzmann distribution over AnyJev) arbitrating among answering (SPECIFIC), facet-guided clarification (CLARIFY), and abstaining (ABSTAIN); (2) a post-generation deterministic numeric verifier (verify_numeric_guardrail) with SHA-256 cache invalidation ensuring every generated fee or validity figure belongs to the retrieved official record; and (3) an empirical evaluation across 380 organic production traces (8 weeks) and a 200-query stratified benchmark (5-fold CV: 86.41% +/- 7.50%). In production, 89.47% of traffic is resolved at Level 0 via deterministic templates without free generation (global p50 = 0.665 s; direct L0 p50 = 0.445 s; slow-tail p95 = 30.412 s under CPU queuing), with 0/40 fiscal amount hallucinations observed in Level 1 (95% bound <= 8.81%). On the benchmark, the system achieves 86.50% strict Top-1 accuracy (+10.00 pp over a binary neural gate, p = 0.00453, p_Holm = 0.01172), pairing a 93.00% conditional safe upper bound with a 70.87% dropout-adjusted effective resolution after accounting for 53.33% citizen drop-off in clarification menus.

EMMA: Structured Episodic Memory for Multi-Agent Workflow Synthesis

Ruijia Niu, Kaiwen Liu, Rose Yu et al. · Oct 25

They introduced EMMA, treating multi-agent system configurations—including controller routing and procedural skills—as structured episodic memory to avoid cross-task interference without expensive parameter training.

For you: Provides concrete architectural patterns for organizing reliable, multi-agent workflows and managing state across sessions.

Abstract

Deploying LLM agents in specialized domains often require fine-grained task-specific procedures and knowledge a general model lacks, yet parameter training is costly and non-interpretable, and hand-engineered agent systems shift the cost to expert labor. Episodic memory updates at context level, but single agent adaptation struggles to alleviate cross-task interference, especially with complex tasks such as multi-application navigation or data pool analysis. Although multi-agent evolution alleviates this problem, existing methods lack clear definitions on update rules and rely on the backbone model to decide where and how much the system changes. This ill-scoped evolution makes learning follow the backbone's structural preferences rather than the observed failures: one backbone accumulates skills while another spawns agents. We present EMMA (structured Episodic Memory for Multi-Agent workflow synthesis), which treats the configuration of a multi-agent system (controller routing memory, specialists inheriting a generalist prompt, and per-step procedural skills) as episodic memory. EMMA makes the ownership and admissibility of updates explicit: a reflector assigns each diagnosed failure to the scope owning its correction and proposes one edit within it, and backbone-independent guards keep every accepted edit scoped, incremental and bounded. Across KramaBench, AppWorld and BBEH with five backbones and four baselines (ACE, GEPA, EvoMAS and ADAS), EMMA attains the best overall performance on every benchmark, up to 32.5% over the strongest baseline, maintains consistent improvements across backbones, and uses 17-38% fewer training executions than the most efficient baseline.

Semantic Atoms for Scalable Text Analysis in Data Analytics in the Era of LLM-Based Agents

Jinxiang Xie, Zihao Li, Rui Ding et al. · Oct 25

They defined Tagging and Categorization semantic atoms for LLM-based text analysis in data workflows, interleaving neural text steps with exact SQL or Python operations.

For you: Offers architectural patterns for mixing LLM text extraction with structured SQL/Python data pipelines in enterprise applications.

Abstract

Much of the information needed for data analysis is buried in text columns: an analyst may infer customer frustration from complaint text and use it to explain resolution time. We call workloads in which processing text columns is central to subsequent data analysis TADA (\emph{Text Analysis in Data Analytics}). In an agentic setting, the user's query dynamically specifies this processing, and answering it interleaves LLM-based semantic steps with exact SQL or Python operations. This mixed execution raises two design questions: which work should be neural and which symbolic, and which semantic outputs later steps can reuse. We address them for agent-planned TADA workflows with two complementary, query-driven semantic atoms. Each atom takes a sequence of text items and a natural-language query specifying the semantic task: \textbf{Tagging} returns one string per input item, while \textbf{Categorization} returns $M$ category objects over $N$ input items. Tagging results are materialized as a row-aligned column and Categorization results as a category table; SQL or Python then performs exact computation over this exposed state. We realize this interface in TADA-Agent, whose atoms run inside generated Python programs and internally handle inputs beyond a reliable context window. We evaluate TADA-Agent on TABench, a benchmark of 40 complete analysis requests over spreadsheet tables spanning workflow composition, input--output structure, and literature-grounded analytical scenarios. Against a semantic-operator baseline and relative to a held-out TADA reference run, TADA-Agent delivers comparable row-level semantic quality, has higher complete-artifact preservation estimates, and uses over an order of magnitude fewer semantic-model requests under the evaluated native execution paths.

SearchHorizonBench: A Benchmark for Search Value in AI Agents

Jingjie Ning, Xueqi Li, Yibo Kong · Carnegie Mellon University · Oct 25 · ▲ 10

SearchHorizonBench offers 45,632 executions over 3,168 questions to learn and evaluate the value of additional search; historical calibration cuts Brier error 16.2% and yields 2.49‑point utility gain.

For you: Helps tune when your RAG system should trigger costly retrieval versus answering directly.

Abstract

AI agents must decide when another search is worth its cost. Answer-success probabilities capture question difficulty, while this decision requires estimating the additional benefit of search. We introduce SearchHorizonBench, a benchmark for learning and evaluating search value from execution experience. Paired continuations and repeated executions separate expected search gain from generation noise and connect prediction quality to decision utility. The resource contains 45,632 executions across 3,168 questions. On 1,632 held-out questions, historical calibration reduces Brier error by 16.2%, with the improvement concentrated in success-level information. Search gains have a standard deviation of 21.4 percentage points, and tested historical estimators explain less than 2% of this variance in point estimates. Matched-configuration learning curves probe this distinction across training scales. At the reference token price, free selection observations yield a 2.49-point utility opportunity over the best constant action, driven primarily by gain information. Reusable histories, frozen splits, strong baselines and scoring tools provide a common foundation for developing and evaluating value-aware search agents.

PailitaoMMSearch2.0 Technical Report: Scaling Generative Retrieval to Unified End-to-End Multimodal Search

Xiaohan Ye, Zihan Gong, Jingqian Zhao et al. · Oct 27

They built PailitaoMMSearch, a unified multimodal generative retrieval system that generates product IDs from images and text, scaling via data pipelines and continual pre‑training across intents.

For you: Shows how to scale generative retrieval across modalities and intents, offering patterns for building unified RAG pipelines in production.

Abstract

Generative retrieval models have been increasingly adopted in search and recommendation systems. However, industrial generative retrieval models are commonly specialized to a fixed online scenario: they learn from scenario-specific interactions, support a narrow set of query forms and retrieval objectives. We study how to remove these couplings and build a unified end-to-end multimodal search system for open-ended image--text queries, heterogeneous shopping intents, and online scenarios with different resource constraints. We present \textbf{PailitaoMMSearch}, which directly generates product Semantic IDs from a raw query image and free-form instruction and scales along three dimensions. \textbf{Data scaling} uses a reusable toolset that can be composed into intent- and lifecycle-specific workflows for cold-start synthesis, quality control, real-traffic refinement, and continuous data production. \textbf{Intent scaling} progressively equips one model with e-commerce product knowledge, multiple retrieval relations, and adjustable reasoning budgets through continual pre-training (CPT), mixed-budget supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and unified reinforcement learning (RL). The RL stage combines intent-balanced optimization and Sample--Beam rollouts aligned with multi-candidate serving. \textbf{Scenario scaling} exposes quality--cost operating points across model capacity and reasoning budget. The system is deployed in multimodal search applications on Taobao Pailitao. A controlled online A/B test reports statistically significant improvements, including item page views $\textbf{+17.90\%}$, transactions $\textbf{+27.04\%}$, and gross merchandise value $\textbf{+8.94\%}$.

EREBOTS 4.0: A framework for conversational assistants with A2A agents and MCP tools

Elia Pacioni, Davide Calvaresi, Andrei C. Coman et al. · Oct 26

EREBOTS 4.0 offers an open‑source hybrid‑AI multi‑agent framework where language‑model agents plan, deterministic tools compute, and an orchestrator routes requests via standardized A2A and MCP protocols.

For you: Provides reusable infrastructure for building reliable multi‑agent LLM assistants, accelerating internal development.

Abstract

In recent years, multi-agent systems have shifted from rule-based, symbolic agents to sub-symbolic agents built on language models. However, several limitations remain unresolved. The new frontier is neuro-symbolic agents, which pair language models with deterministic tools and explicit protocols. Without a common framework to standardize and compose them, each study rebuilds the same infrastructure, and results are hard to replicate. This paper presents EREBOTS 4.0, an open-source, hybrid-AI, multi-agent framework that fills this gap for conversational assistants. Language-model agents plan and route requests, while deterministic tools compute exact results over the Model Context Protocol (MCP). An orchestrator selects one or more specialist agents per turn and calls them over the Agent2Agent protocol (A2A). A new agent, local or remote, is added by listing its URL in a configuration file. The framework also manages identity, user context with consent flags, task states and traces. Its A2A server passed 97 tracked requirements in the official A2A Technology Compatibility Kit, with no failures.

Trainable Boundary-Keeping Where No Formal Answer Exists: A Controlled Knock-In Study of Reward Training in Evidence-Sufficiency Judgment

YaJie Yin · Oct 26

Applying an SFT then GRPO boundary-keeping training recipe raises spontaneous correct refusal from 25% to 100% on evidence-sufficiency judgment tasks without increasing false refusals.

For you: Actionable post-training recipe for improving reliability and boundary adherence in tasks lacking clear correctness rewards.

Abstract

Reasoning-tuned and reward-trained models are often assumed to verify more reliably because they reason longer or receive correctness signals. We test this in evidence-sufficiency judgment: deciding whether available information actually supports a causal claim, a domain with no decidable correctness reward. Using a pre-registered, same-base, controlled knock-in design, we compare a boundary-keeping recipe (SFT then GRPO) with its own baseline, a scrambled-pairing placebo, and a general-purpose reasoning model under an action-pressure protocol with no evaluation invitation. All load-bearing outcomes are rated by two separate model-rating sessions masked to arm labels; automatic judges only trigger. The trained arm raises spontaneous correct refusal from 25% to 100% (40 per arm; Wilson 95% CI lower bound 91.2%, non-overlapping with the baseline interval [14.2%, 40.2%]), while the scrambled placebo collapses (5% spontaneous / 7.5% invited correct; 91.1% false refusal). The effect is boundary-specific rather than blanket refusal: automatic false refusal on 281 well-formed claims is 1.1% (Wilson upper bound 3.09%), and a targeted 18-item arm-(c) recheck (3 flagged plus 15 accepted) yields 0/18 and 1/18 false refusals in two sessions. The dissociation is the main result: final boundary-keeping is trainable without an inference-time verifier, while observed active numeric/calculation checking in the H3 assay remains near zero; SFT saturation makes the GRPO increment not separately identifiable. A pre-registered mechanism adjudication favors reward internalization of final-action status (H2, best-supported, not proven). The scope is a controlled 14B same-family study: we do not claim proof, cross-family generality, or independent human-expert validation; the rating sessions are model sessions, not human reviewers.

WorkFactory: Turning Professional Intents into Executable Work for Scalable Agent Training

Ziyang Zeng, Tingyang Chen, Jia Deng et al. · Oct 27

For you: Offers useful techniques for generating synthetic training data and evaluating complex multi-step enterprise agent workflows.

Abstract

Scaling supervised training for working agents requires diverse professional tasks that yield reliable expert demonstrations. However, missing or inconsistent task information can obstruct teacher execution and weaken the resulting supervision. We propose WorkFactory, a scalable framework for constructing coherent and executable professional tasks. Starting from professional intents, information-level planning builds an information support graph to guide task instantiation, explicitly representing the dependencies and locations of information required for the intended outputs. Probe executions then identify support failures and guide repairs while preserving the intended work. We construct 112,686 tasks spanning 612 occupations and use the collected teacher trajectories to fine-tune Qwen models at 4B, 27B, and 35B-A3B scales, yielding the WorkPilot family. All three models improve over their respective base models across four professional-work benchmarks, with WorkPilot-35B-A3B improving from 879 to 1565 Elo on GDPval-AA v2.1. Scaling analyses reveal model-dependent diminishing returns, with execution length first increasing and then decreasing. Controlled component analyses show that planning and repair reduce probe-diagnosed task environment defects and improve downstream performance. The data and models are available at https://github.com/IQuestLab/WorkFactory.

Proactive LLM agents anticipate service opportunities from ongoing observations and accumulated history. A Domain-Aware Pipeline for Structured Extraction and Retrieval-Augmented Question Answering over arXiv Superconductivity Literature

Ivan Iorsh, Aleksandr Kapitonov, Ivan Filianin et al. · Oct 25

Built an end-to-end RAG pipeline for superconductivity literature combining domain-aware extraction of materials and transition temperatures with hybrid retrieval.

For you: Demonstrates practical document structuring and hybrid search techniques that can be adapted for specialized enterprise document processing.

Abstract

Scientific literature in materials physics is growing faster than researchers can manually inspect it, yet most retrieval tools rely on keyword matching or generic question answering that ignores domain-specific structure in PDF files. This paper presents an end-to-end pipeline that transforms arXiv superconductivity publications into a searchable corpus with structured metadata and retrieval-augmented question answering. The system combines thematic corpus construction, domain-aware extraction of materials and superconducting transition temperatures, and hybrid retrieval that merges dense chunk embeddings with structured publication fields. We test whether normalized domain attributes improve evidence retrieval and answer groundedness relative to lexical-only and dense text-only methods. Extraction is evaluated on a manually verified gold set of 59 superconducting materials, 46 of which have confirmed experimental critical temperatures. On the 33-material primary positive subset, domain-aware pair accuracy reaches 45.5% versus 9.1% for a regex baseline. Retrieval and generation are compared under a fixed language model (qwen2.5:7b-instruct) on a 100-question benchmark, varying only the retrieval mechanism: BM25, dense, hybrid, and hybrid with domain metadata. BM25 gives the highest source recall (Recall@5 of 47%), and adding extracted metadata to hybrid retrieval does not improve it (39% versus 41%), which we attribute to noise in the extracted fields; the main bottleneck is locating the supporting span inside a correctly retrieved paper. As a materials-informatics application, literature-derived temperatures are matched to an independent theoretical reference set: 200 materials appear in both sources, and 20 have a median experimental critical temperature above the theoretical value.

API-TAIL: Detecting Model Substitution in LLM APIs through Temperature-Aligned Likelihood Tails

Haocheng Mei, Yuchuan Tian, Ye Yuan et al. · Oct 27

Introduced API-TAIL, a typicality audit using temperature-aligned likelihood tails to detect when commercial LLM APIs silently route requests to cheaper substitute models.

For you: Useful pattern for enterprise auditing and monitoring API vendor reliability and cost optimizations.

Abstract

Commercial language model APIs return only text, allowing a provider to cut costs by quietly routing a fraction of user requests to a cheaper substitute model while billing for the advertised one. Detecting such partial substitution is challenging because honest providers also alter the output distribution through undocumented decoding and serving choices, such as temperature scaling, sampling truncation, repetition and presence penalties, and default system prompts, forcing an audit to test against a composite null of legitimate configurations rather than a single reference. Our key insight is that these legitimate variations primarily shift response typicality across the entire batch, whereas partial substitution injects individual responses that remain anomalous under the claimed model. Building on this, we introduce API-TAIL, a typicality audit that first absorbs batch-level shifts by aligning a single effective temperature to center standardized surprisal scores across the audited batch, and then flags the endpoint if the number of remaining tail responses exceeds a threshold calibrated across legitimate configurations. Evaluated on real-world commercial APIs, API-TAIL reliably controls false alarms across diverse honest providers while detecting even low-rate partial substitution with a small query budget, substantially outperforming existing rank- and MMD-based audits that lose detection power under the same composite calibration.

Heads of Security: Learned Attention Gates Improve Prompt Injection Robustness

Igor Shilov, Xiaoxue Yang, Alexander Panfilov et al. · Oct 27

Heads of Security (HoS) uses learned per-head attention gates to separate instructions from untrusted data, reducing prompt injection attacks on Llama and GPT models.

For you: Relevant architectural mitigation for prompt injection risks in production multi-agent systems handling untrusted external inputs.

Abstract

We introduce Heads of Security (HoS), a prompt injection defense that enforces instruction-data separation via a small set of learned per-head attention gates. HoS learns how each head should redistribute attention between trusted and untrusted tokens, enabling models to reason over untrusted content without following injected instructions. We evaluate HoS on Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and GPT-OSS-20B. We compare it against SecAlign++, a state-of-the-art finetuning-based defense, and ASIDE, a defense that, like ours, builds instruction-data separation into the model architecture. We show that HoS offers greater robustness to adaptive attacks, better performance on standard utility benchmarks, and matches top performance on standard agentic prompt injection benchmarks. More specifically, the black-box RL-Hammer attack reaches 97% ASR against GPT-OSS-20B and 85% against SecAlign, but only 10% against HoS. Claudini, a state-of-the-art white-box attack, strengthened against HoS through autoresearch, requires 3 times more FLOPs to reach the same ASR on HoS as it does on GPT-OSS-20B or SecAlign. HoS preserves base-model utility across standard multiple-choice benchmarks and prompting setups, whereas SecAlign and ASIDE degrade in few-shot settings. On agentic benchmarks with static attacks, HoS and SecAlign perform similarly and substantially improve the security-utility trade-off over the base model. ASIDE struggles with tool use, achieving only 10% utility. These results show that prompt injection robustness can be achieved and improved through targeted attention interventions.
