Select-TTT: Query-Conditioned Selective Fast Weight Updates for In-Place Test-Time Training

Yue Wu, Yang Zhang, Weinan Gan et al. · Oct 27

Researchers propose Select-TTT, a query-conditioned fast-weight update rule for In-Place Test-Time Training that scales each context chunk's write strength based on its relevance to the query, read directly from the frozen output head.

For you: Could improve long-context RAG inference by dynamically internalizing relevant context into model weights without retraining.

Abstract

Test-time training (TTT) enables a deployed language model to internalize incoming context on-the-fly into a small set of fast-weight parameters, rather than merely treating it as transient context for attention. In-Place TTT recently made this idea practical for large language models by repurposing the down-projection of standard MLP blocks as fast weights. However, we find that its benefit largely disappears on realistic web long-context question answering. We attribute this to In-Place TTT's uniform write schedule: every chunk is written with equal strength, so irrelevant content dilutes query-relevant information in fast-weight memory. Building on this insight, we propose Select-TTT, a model-agnostic, query-conditioned rule for In-Place TTT that scales each chunk's write strength by its relevance to the user query. This relevance signal requires no supervision and no new parameters: it is read directly from the model's frozen output head, as the probability mass that a chunk's hidden states assign to the query tokens. A temperature-scaled softmax turns these scores into learning-rate multipliers, leaving the chunk-wise, context-parallel computation of In-Place TTT intact. Select-TTT consistently improves long-context question answering on two benchmarks and two backbones. These findings highlight the value of query-conditioned fast weight writes for practical test-time training on long-context question answering, which is also potentially promising for long-horizon agent tasks involving multiple user queries over evolving contexts.

From Interaction to Capability: Task Advancement as State and Supervision for GUI Agents

Ziyang Chen, Qian Li, Chengwei Qin · Oct 27

Task Advancement Memory (TAM) uses verified task progress to update remaining goals and abstract successful operations, raising MobileWorld task success from 12.0% to 28.2%.

For you: Directly applicable architectural pattern for multi-agent orchestration and long-horizon tool use where agents must track state across steps.

Abstract

GUI memory should preserve the results of past actions and learn reusable operations. This is difficult in long-horizon workflows, where agents must use information across applications and useful operations are interleaved with failed attempts. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement as verified task progress, and supports ongoing task execution and learns reusable operations. During execution, Task Advancement State represents how verified task progress changes the remaining goals, allowing the agent to build on earlier achievements across applications. After each task, advancement supervision identifies and abstracts the interactions that produced a verified local result. The learned operations preserve the relation between required inputs, expected outcomes, and verification conditions. This retains locally successful operations from both successful and failed tasks. On MobileWorld GUI-only tasks, Advancement State alone raises success from 12.0% to 28.2%. Full TAM achieves a 17.6% relative gain in task success over the strongest evaluated baseline and reduces lifecycle tokens per solved task by 38.7%. On SPA-Bench, TAM achieves state-of-the-art task success in the single-app English Level 3 setting.

SimplexLoRA: Dynamic Rank Updating via Alternating Minimization with Simplex Projection

Igor Shalygin, Grigorii Davydenko, Andrey Veprikov et al. · Oct 27

SimplexLoRA dynamically adjusts adapter ranks during fine-tuning using simplex projection, matching or outperforming standard LoRA without increasing parameter budget or inference cost.

For you: Relevant technique for optimizing fine-tuning efficiency and custom model performance without added inference overhead in production.

Abstract

The success of large language models in real-world applications relies on parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), which inserts compact, trainable adapters into selected layers. In this paper, we propose SimplexLoRA, a LoRA variant that adaptively adjusts adapter ranks during fine-tuning based on their learned importance. Unlike standard LoRA, which employs a fixed rank across all layers, SimplexLoRA optimizes per-adapter importance weights on the $n$-scaled simplex during a short warm-up and then freezes them, dynamically allocating capacity where it is most beneficial without increasing the overall parameter budget or inference cost. We evaluate SimplexLoRA across 12 language understanding, summarization, and reasoning tasks using DeBERTaV3, BART-large, Llama 3.1 8B, and Qwen3-8B. Our results show that SimplexLoRA consistently matches or outperforms standard LoRA and recent adaptive baselines, with the largest gains at low ranks. Our code is available at https://github.com/brain-lab-research/SimplexLoRA.

CoHARL: Co-Evolving Agent Policies and Executable Harnesses with Reinforcement Learning

Jianwen Chen, Peng Xia, Kaide Zeng et al. · Oct 26

CoHARL co-evolves agent policies and executable harnesses using reinforcement learning, improving task resolution rates on software engineering benchmarks.

For you: Offers architectural insights into co-optimizing tool interfaces and agent policies for better execution reliability.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through executable harnesses that govern tool use and feedback. Optimizing a harness for a fixed policy overlooks how its behavior changes during learning, while training a policy under a fixed harness leaves the interface that generates its training experience unchanged. This motivates adapting the harness throughout policy training. We introduce CoHARL, a population-based reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a shared policy and a population of executable harnesses. The policy learns from rollouts across the population, and a proposer uses its execution traces to revise harness code during training. CoHARL achieves a 27.6% mean resolved rate on SWE-rebench, improving over harness-only search by 6.4 percentage points. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show gains in software engineering and terminal-based tasks. Under matched training budgets, CoHARL also outperforms searching for a harness once before RL and alternating policy updates with revisions to a single active harness.

CIVER: Causal Intervention Verification for Reliable Code Review

Xinyuan Song, Hasibul Haque, Jay Somasundaram et al. · Oct 26

Introduced CIVER, a causal intervention framework that separates genuine code defects from false positives in LLM code reviews.

For you: Useful evaluation and verification pattern for filtering noise in automated agentic code review or document validation workflows.

Abstract

Code review is essential for maintaining software repositories by identifying defects, improving code quality, and supporting reliable software evolution. With the growing adoption of LLM-based coding agents, an increasing number of code contributions and repository changes are generated by these agents. Consequently, LLM-based code review agents are becoming increasingly prevalent to evaluate these contributions and maintain software quality. However, LLM reviewers often generate lengthy reviews containing numerous potential issues, many of which are false positives or lack sufficient evidence. These unreliable reviews can mislead developers and downstream coding agents, motivating the need to distinguish actionable findings from unsupported claims. We propose CIVER, a causal intervention-based framework that identifies the core issues underlying code-review findings and distinguishes genuine defects from false positives. CIVER integrates three modules: a review module assesses findings, an intervention module generates repair-oriented perturbations, and a verifier module scores review reliability. Through joint optimization via expert iteration, the three modules identify genuine defects by responding to targeted repairs while remaining stable under unrelated changes. Extensive experiments on SWE-Review-Bench demonstrate that CIVER correctly identifies 77% of genuine defects through targeted repairs. Cross-project and downstream repair evaluations further demonstrate that CIVER generalizes across repositories and improves coding agents' task success rates, highlighting its effectiveness for reliable automated code review.

Correction or Manipulation? Measuring Epistemic Vigilance in LLM Agents Under User Pressure

Nardine Basta, Venus Haghighi, Dali Kaafar et al. · Oct 26

An empirical study across seven LLMs measures how easily agents concede to user disagreement, distinguishing between corrective feedback and manipulation attempts.

For you: Valuable insights into agent robustness and user interaction patterns when designing customer-facing or assistant-style LLM apps.

Abstract

LLM agents that answer questions and act on behalf of users must decide when to yield to a user who disagrees. Disagreement may correct a genuine error or may be an attempt at manipulation. Prior work shows that models frequently abandon correct answers when challenged by users, yet it remains unclear whether they distinguish correction from manipulation or merely differ in their propensity to concede. We examine this question across seven LLMs from four vendors in two studies, one on the answers agents give and one on the actions they take as customer-support agents whose policy permits account changes only for verified users. Simulated users push toward correct and incorrect answers with graded pressure. Following research on epistemic vigilance, the pressure either appeals to the source, such as claimed authority, or offers content the agent cannot verify, namely a cited source. Drawing on signal detection theory, we measure on a set of benchmark questions how much more readily each agent concedes to correction than to manipulation, separately from its overall propensity to concede. Citations made five of seven models readier to concede and four worse at discriminating, while stronger wording added little. In the agentic study, five models never violated the verification policy, including models that readily abandoned correct answers, so concession on answers did not predict violations of policy. Ministral 3B violated the policy mainly under claimed authority, and Ministral 8B reported changes it had not made. An instruction to revise only on verifiable evidence chiefly shifted propensity to concede rather than improving discrimination. Concession rates alone are therefore misleading, and agents should be evaluated on both their discrimination and the actions they take.

Δτ-Bench: Pushing Agents Toward a Moving Capability

Shuofei Qiao, Xuanguang Pan, Yijun Chen et al. · University College London · Oct 24 · ▲ 26

Introduced Delta-Bench, a 1,588-task benchmark measuring how agents handle dynamically evolving tool ecosystems, showing current models achieve only 42.3% success.

For you: Good evaluation reference for robust agent design against changing API definitions and toolsets in production environments.

Abstract

In this paper, we introduce $Δτ$-Bench, an executable agent benchmark that models the structural evolution of large tool ecosystems. In $Δτ$-Bench, independently evolving services continually reshape how tasks can be accomplished: familiar tools may disappear or be replaced, while more efficient alternatives emerge amid overlapping functionality and noisy documentation. Against this moving capability frontier, we evaluate whether agents can proactively discover efficient tool-use paths and promptly revise their accumulated procedural knowledge, bringing together demands that existing benchmarks fail to examine. $Δτ$-Bench comprises 1,588 validated multi-turn tasks across 42 domains and six successive ecosystem states generated through capability-preserving transformations. Our evaluation shows that even the strongest evaluated model or harness achieves only 42.3% task success, while initial gains from existing skill-learning methods can not consistently translate into better adaptation as the ecosystem evolves. These findings motivate Steward, an ecosystem manager that jointly manages tools and procedural knowledge through incremental skill maintenance and dynamic provisioning during task execution. Experiments show that Steward sustains higher task success across ecosystem changes with lower skill-maintenance overhead, while improving both recovery from disrupted workflows and adoption of more efficient alternatives.

Learning to Remember: Advances in Memory Mechanisms for Large Language Models

Chenye Han, Hongche Yu, Mengyue Wu et al. · Oct 26

Provides a comprehensive survey organizing LLM memory mechanisms into nonparametric, parametric, and agent-evolution families based on cognitive science concepts.

For you: Valuable taxonomy for evaluating long-term memory architectures and context engineering strategies in production agentic apps.

Abstract

Memory has become a central mechanism for extending large language models (LLMs) beyond fixed context windows, stateless interaction, and static parametric knowledge. This survey organizes the literature around three complementary dimensions: the life cycle of memory, the storage substrate, and the system role that memory plays in LLM-based agents, while using cognitive-science-inspired memory concepts as a lightweight conceptual motivation rather than as a separate technical contribution or implementation family. On this basis, we distinguish three mechanism families: nonparametric memory, where information is stored outside model parameters in text, graph, multimodal, or database-like structures; parametric memory, where information is stored or accessed through activations, key-value caches, learned modules, adapter-based parameterizations, or model weights; and memory for agent evolution, where stored experiences support reinforcement learning, continual learning, procedural reuse, context engineering, and agent harness design. Evaluation is treated as a separate cross-cutting component rather than as a memory mechanism. Unlike surveys that focus primarily on retrieval-augmented generation or long-context modeling, this paper emphasizes the full memory life cycle: construction, maintenance, retrieval, consolidation, forgetting, governance, and evaluation. By aligning conceptual motivations, computational memory mechanisms, benchmarks, and deployment concerns, the survey provides a structured roadmap for designing memory-augmented LLM agents that are persistent, adaptive, auditable, and safe to update over time.

ThinkBit: Search Codewords, Recalibrate Scales, Select by Reasoning

Rhea Sanjay Sukthanker, Pashmina Cameron · Microsoft · Oct 25 · ▲ 5

Presents ThinkBit, a strict scalar 2-bit quantization framework for reasoning LLMs that combines layerwise bitwidth allocation, Gumbel-softmax quantization, and scale recalibration.

For you: Relevant for reducing LLM inference costs and memory bandwidth bottlenecks when deploying large reasoning models in production.

Abstract

Reasoning language models generate thousands of tokens per answer, and generation speed is limited by memory bandwidth, so storing weights in two bits instead of bfloat16 can potentially make inference 8$\times$ cheaper. Two-bit methods are usually judged by perplexity and multiple-choice question-answering accuracy, both of which say little about whether a model can still reason through more difficult problems. We present ThinkBit, a strictly scalar two-bit framework built to preserve reasoning, in which every weight is an integer codeword times a bfloat16 scale shared by a group of weights. We first use a sensitivity metric to assign each layer a ternary, 2-bit, or 3-bit tier to allocate bitwidths optimally among layers. This is followed by a layerwise Gumbel-softmax-based scalar quantization algorithm that searches for optimal codewords block by block. Since a layerwise scheme does not capture how quantized layers interact with each other, we recalibrate scales, with the codewords frozen, using a knowledge-distillation-based loss against the teacher model. We then perform a short quantization-aware training (QAT) stage that lets the model mimic the base model's distribution by reassigning codewords. Finally, a paired reasoning test on training problems selects the final checkpoint between the recalibration stage and the QAT stage. On Qwen3-1.7B at matched size, our recalibrated checkpoint scores 46.7 on MATH-500 against 30.0 for EfficientQAT trained on the same data, and 73.0 after QAT on reasoning traces; at 0.52 GB it is smaller than every published 2-bit GGUF of the model yet more accurate on MATH-500. We show that this framework scales and up to 14B, a 3.74 GB model scores 94.8 on MATH-500, against 97.5 for the 29.5 GB bfloat16 model.

Action-Value Calibration via Continuation Sharing for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Yingjian Chen, Lang Zhou, Zixin Zhao et al. · Oct 27

They proposed ConCal, a recursive continuation-value estimator for long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning that matches transition visits to share evidence and reduce local credit assignment error.

For you: Provides methodological improvements for credit assignment in multi-step agent trajectories, useful if training proprietary agent policies.

Abstract

Reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents relies on feedback from sequences of interactions, making credit assignment to individual actions difficult. Existing methods such as GiGPO compare actions at shared states to compute relative advantages, but estimate each action's value from a single sampled continuation. The resulting local credit remains sensitive to decisions made later in the trajectory. To address this problem, we propose ConCal (Continuation Calibration), a recursive continuation-value estimator that incorporates continuation evidence already present in a rollout batch. ConCal matches visits from the same task by their incoming state-action-state transitions, combines shared continuation evidence with each visit's own transition estimate, and propagates calibrated values backward to refine earlier action-value estimates. This reduces dependence on individual sampled continuations and mitigates local credit distortions without additional rollouts. Held-out return diagnostics show lower error in within-group value differences and higher agreement with reference action orderings than estimates based on individual continuations. With Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, ConCal improves success rates over GiGPO by 3.39 and 5.86 percentage points on ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively. Our code is available at https://github.com/Yingjian-Chen/ConCal.

InfraDev: Can Coding Agents Develop AI Infrastructure, and How Can We Tell?

Yangshen Deng, Jiaqi Ruan, Yeqi Huang et al. · Oct 26

InfraDev presents 36 multi‑file pull‑request tasks for coding agents to modify AI infrastructure code across GPUs; hidden probes catch missing requirements. Top model solves 38.9% of tasks, with probes rejecting 32% of apparently passing submissions.

For you: Helps evaluate and improve coding agents that could automate infra changes in your LLM deployment pipelines.

Abstract

Coding agents are beginning to develop the production-grade AI infrastructure that trains and serves large models, yet existing benchmarks cover only single kernels or single-GPU tasks, and grade with tests and prompts that misjudge both correct and incorrect patches. We introduce InfraDev, a benchmark of 36 tasks built from real-world pull requests to Megatron-LM, vLLM, and verl, covering infrastructure development for training, inference, and reinforcement learning. Each task asks an agent to reimplement a real change that spans many files and must run correctly across many GPUs. To grade such changes, we jointly synthesize hidden probes, which enforce the requirements the original tests leave unchecked, and a prompt that states every interface the probes call without revealing the solution. We also design a shared execution environment that reuses dependencies and artifacts across tasks, cutting storage by 52-57% and build time by 13-14% on Megatron-LM and vLLM. Across six frontier models, the best resolves only 38.9% of the tasks; the probes reject 32% of the submissions that pass every official test, and with the original pull request descriptions as prompts, no model resolves a single task. Failure analysis shows that structural and state errors dominate and that agents' own tests rarely check memory, time, or traffic. Turning these findings into an agent skill raises the resolved rate of a frontier model on the Megatron-LM tasks by 10.5%.

Your Policy Is Secretly a Harness: The Decision Geometry of Agent Interfaces

Jian Mu, Ruibo Shen, Yao Shu · HKUSTGZ · Oct 26 · ▲ 7

Trained policies inherit the preferences encoded in their harness; performance equals harness ceiling minus policy mismatch, and changing the harness shifts both terms.

For you: Clarifies how prompt/tool harnesses affect agent reliability, guiding better harness design for production agents.

Abstract

Language model agents are post-trained and deployed through a harness, the prompts, tools, and control logic that present a task to the model and execute its outputs. Harnesses are often revised and judged by how an already trained model performs under them, which assumes that what the model learned under one harness carries over to the next. We ask what a policy learns from its harness and what survives when the harness changes. Under KL-regularized control, we show that the log odds of an optimal policy recover how strongly its harness favors each action over the others, so a trained policy carries the preferences of its harness. Deployed return then equals the ceiling of the harness, the best return it offers, minus the mismatch of the policy, the return lost to its own suboptimal decisions. Training changes only the mismatch, while changing the harness moves both terms. Comparing harnesses with a fixed model therefore mixes what each harness offers with how well that model uses it. In MiniWoB++, post-training makes a model's preferences specific to its training harness. Reversing the order of the observation fields leaves every optimal decision unchanged yet lowers a trained model's success from 85.0% to 19.2%. Conversely, every new harness that a fixed-model comparison rejects but that raises the ceiling outperforms the original agent after further training under it, and ceiling and mismatch measured beforehand predict these gains. For coding-agent harnesses on Terminal-Bench Lite, ceiling minus mismatch estimated with a stronger model as a stand-in for the optimum reproduces the accuracy ranking, while either term alone does not. Code is available at https://github.com/2020-qqtcg/HRP.

Omni-InteractScope: Benchmarking Proactive and Agentic Interaction in Continuous Omnimodal Streams

Yinsong Yan, Ling Wang, Yuxuan Wang et al. · Qwen · Oct 25 · ▲ 14

Omni-InteractScope benchmark contains 1,479 annotated interactions over continuous audiovisual streams; shows current Omni‑LLMs achieve ≤40% on‑time tool invocation and ≤32% joint timing‑tool accuracy.

For you: Provides realistic metrics to gauge latency and tool use reliability for your streaming RAG agents.

Abstract

Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) are increasingly expected to assist users while observing continuous audiovisual streams. However, existing evaluations provide limited insight into how reliably these models connect stream evidence and user needs to timely, sustained interventions. We introduce Omni-InteractScope, a benchmark of 1,479 human-annotated, cross-reviewed interactions with 1,790 temporal targets across eight task groups. It links input modalities, event triggers, intervention types, and user needs, covering event-timed tool invocation and query-free assistance alongside notifications, warnings, state tracking, and continuous responses. Our experimental results on the proposed benchmark reveal three critical limitations of current Omni-LLMs: (1) Timely tool invocation remains unreliable: all 12 evaluated models produce an on-time first response for at most 40\% of tool-invocation targets, and joint timing-and-tool accuracy reaches at most 32.0\%. (2) Query-free reminders remain poorly covered: all evaluated models achieve lower timely reference-target recall on query-free reminders than on explicit questions, measured on their respective target sets. Reminder recall never exceeds 23.0\%. (3) End-to-end guidance coverage remains limited: nine models cover none of the 26 guidance sequences in full, and the remaining three cover only one to three sequences each. Targeted controls further show that tool-invocation scores alone do not establish event grounding and that reminder coverage depends on response history and frequency. These findings motivate developing and evaluating streaming assistants for timely action, initiative under general user needs, and coverage of entire response sequences.

EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems

Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu et al. · Oct 25

EvolveMAS pairs each agent with a MemoryAgent storing structured episodic memory, enabling within‑episode online adaptation and cross‑episode knowledge evolution for decentralized LLM agents.

For you: Offers a memory‑based adaptation architecture to improve reliability of multi‑agent LLM systems in production.

Abstract

Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.

Linear Probes Transfer from Base to Distilled Language Models despite Rotation

Marios Tsatsos · Oct 26

Linear probes trained to detect harmful requests on base models transfer successfully to distilled and instruction-tuned successors despite internal representation rotation, maintaining high AUC.

For you: Useful technique for safety auditing and classifier reuse when upgrading underlying production LLMs.

Abstract

Linear probes, classifiers that read a language model's internal activations, are widely used to detect harmful requests. They are cheap to train and run, and an auditor who has trained one would like to reuse it when the model is replaced by a successor rather than retrain it for every release. We test how well probes can be reused. For four base models (Qwen2.5-Math-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, Qwen2.5-32B) we train a probe on the base model with the XSTest prompt set and apply it, unchanged, to two successors of that base: the DeepSeek-R1 distilled reasoning model and the developer's instruction-tuned chat model. We find that the successors rotate the direction separating unsafe from safe prompts by 17$^\circ$ to 35$^\circ$, yet on XSTest the transferred probe's AUC changes by only $-0.011$ to $+0.027$, and on an independent OR-Bench evaluation, never used for training, by $-0.015$ to $+0.053$. On XSTest, whose safe prompts deliberately contain alarming words, the probes beat a classifier that sees only the prompt text by 0.15 to 0.20 AUC. On the OR-Bench prompts that margin shrinks to 0.03 to 0.20, and the probes flag 29\% to 42\% of prompts that sound harmful but are benign, so part of what they read is the appearance of harm rather than harm itself. A probe trained on a base model can thus be reused, in these families, after distillation and after instruction tuning; how much it reads beyond textual cues depends on the test set.

NumTrace: Auditing the Number Provenance of Financial Analyst Agents

Tianlei Zhu, Matt White, Xue Liu et al. · Oct 25

Introduced NumTrace, a benchmark with 540 questions across SEC filings to audit financial analyst agent numerical claims, checking source provenance and financial semantics.

For you: Useful evaluation methodology for validating numerical extraction and grounding in complex document processing pipelines.

Abstract

A numerical claim generated by a financial analyst agent can match a number in a company filing but refer to the wrong entity, concept, period, unit or scope. A check based on reference answers can mark such an answer as wrong but does not explain why it fails. In this paper, we present NumTrace, a new benchmark that audits the provenance of each numerical claim made by financial analyst agents: its source and financial semantics. NumTrace currently has 540 questions over 2,163 SEC EDGAR documents. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first numerical benchmark about who holds a company's shares (Form 13F) and what its executives are owed (Item 402(i)). On a frozen snapshot of these documents, we build the reference answers according to accounting rules. NumTrace then gives each claim three separate verdicts: value, binding and support. By retaining separate value and binding verdicts, NumTrace detects right number, wrong account (RNWA) errors: a claim has an RNWA error when it passes the value check but fails the binding check. The RNWA rate quantifies the gap between value-only and joint value-and-binding pass rates. Finally, we audit three agents, Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. RNWA errors make up 67.5-81.8% of each agent's failing claims. In particular, on 13F co-filing group questions, about 91% of claims pass the value check, but fewer than half pass the binding check.

Created a multi-level evaluation framework across household and scheduling tasks with asymmetric information to test whether LLMs can effectively utilize theory of mind inferences in execution.

Abstract

Effective collaboration requires language models to understand what others know and believe, and to act accordingly. Models that assume others share their information may omit necessary communication and undermine coordination. Many theory-of-mind (ToM) benchmarks assess mental-state inference through story questions, but correct answers do not establish that models can use these judgments in subsequent tasks. We introduce a multi-level evaluation built on existing, verifiable tasks. Scaling Laws for Tool-Use Agents via Controllable Environment Synthesis

Andrew Szot, Samira Abnar, Omar Attia et al. · Oct 27

Proposed the SynthAPI pipeline to systematically vary tool, task, and database diversity, deriving scaling laws that predict tool-use agent generalization from environment composition.

For you: Offers practical insights into synthetic data composition and environment design for training robust tool-calling agents.

Abstract

We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.

TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information

Chanwoo Park, Jaywoong Jeong, Zachary Wojtowicz et al. · Oct 27

Introduced TeamGym, an environment with distributed pivotal information across real-world corpora, revealing that multi-agent systems suffer a 22.9 percentage point drop in success due to communication gaps.

For you: Highlights communication failure modes in multi-agent orchestration that are crucial when building team-based agent architectures.

Abstract

Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Yet AI agents are still primarily trained and benchmarked in isolation. We introduce TeamGym, an environment for evaluating and training language model-based agents on team decision-making problems constructed from real-world legal, finance, medical, and software engineering corpora. TeamGym introduces a method to identify a minimal set of facts, each element of which is necessary and all of which are collectively sufficient for a correct final decision, which we call pivotal information. This enables TeamGym to both control the distribution of pivotal information and track how well agents identify and integrate pivotal information when making team decisions. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). We also document that participants do not effectively retrieve decision-relevant information on their own: A team's success rate is lowered by a further 19.9 points when participants must identify and extract pivotal information from source material rather than receiving it directly (a retrieval gap). To help remedy these deficits, we use TeamGym to train policies through reinforcement learning on team decision loss. Across two policy-training experiments, we find that training improves final-decision quality and induces emergent coordination in participation, speaker selection, and communication, even though the reward evaluates only the final decision.

LiveSearch: Questions as Executable Verifier Code for a Live Benchmark of Professional-Source Financial Search Agents

Sinuo Wang, Wentao Zhao, Jiacheng Lu et al. · Oct 27

Introduced LiveSearch, a benchmark storing questions as executable verifier code against professional financial data services, evaluated via automated daily jobs to prevent data leakage and staleness.

For you: Useful methodology for building robust evaluation suites for agents that rely on live external tools and APIs.

Abstract

In finance, the answer that matters is usually the current one: prices, rates, and disclosures hold only as of a given day, so retrieving up-to-date facts is a core capability of a financial agent. Yet most financial benchmarks overlook timeliness. They fix each answer at annotation time, so the gold goes stale as markets move and, once leaked into training data, rewards recall rather than search and analysis. We introduce LiveSearch, a live benchmark that stores each question as executable verifier code: a typed program of calls to a professional financial data service, followed by a declarative extractor. The question is frozen, while its gold answer is determined by executing the program against professional financial data at evaluation time, and a daily job re-executes it with no human or language model in the loop. A memorized answer is thus likely outdated, and a model must reach the current one through tools and analysis. Construction is equally automatic: a language model only verbalizes programs enumerated from real tool responses and never chooses an answer. Under general web search, six contemporary tool-calling models reach 70.9% to 76.7% answer accuracy. Over three consecutive days, 70% of served questions change their gold value, and even under tolerance-aware matching, a literally reused earlier snapshot fails on a majority of questions within a single day. Per-question tracking uncovers dynamic shifts in correctness masked by aggregate accuracy.

Channel Selection for Mixture-of-Experts Decoding Needs No Calibration Data

Geunhyoung Kim, Sung Ju Hwang · KAIST AI · Oct 26 · ▲ 21

Shows that Mixture-of-Experts channel selection can use simple top-k routing instead of calibrated thresholds without needing offline data.

For you: Practical optimization for running MoE LLMs efficiently during interactive decoding by removing calibration requirements.

Abstract

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.

ETAMem: Decoupling and Aligning Heterogeneous Memory for Long-Term Conversational Agents

Xinying Qian, Ying Zhang, Yu Zhao et al. · Oct 27

Introduced ETAMem, a multi-view memory framework that decouples conversational information into Event, Affect, and Time views with entropy-based retrieval.

For you: Provides concrete architectural patterns for managing long-term conversational memory in production agent systems.

Abstract

Long-term memory enables Large Language Models (LLMs) to maintain coherent and personalized interactions across extended conversations. However, existing memory systems face two key challenges: (1) Information Entanglement, where heterogeneous information is mixed within coarse-grained memory units; and (2) Organizational Mismatch, where memory structures based primarily on semantic similarity may overlook other associations. To address these challenges, we propose ETAMem, a multi-view memory framework that decouples heterogeneous conversational information and aligns memory representations and organization with its distinct characteristics. Specifically, heterogeneous information parsing extracts atomic memory units and identifies affective information. View-specific memory structuring then organizes these units into three complementary hierarchical views: Event, Affect, and Time. ETAMem incrementally maintains memory structures with adaptively calibrated thresholds. For retrieval, entropy-based retrieval allocates query-specific budgets across views and performs hierarchical search within each view. Experiments on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S demonstrate that ETAMem outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving relative accuracy improvements of 12.46% and 7.96%, respectively, while maintaining competitive token efficiency. Further analyses demonstrate the complementary benefits of different memory views.

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has shown strong empirical gains in improving the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) on tasks such as mathematics and programming. However, recent studies have shown that RLVR rollouts often collapse onto a narrow set of reasoning trajectories, limiting the explored reasoning space. Learning When to Commit: Schedule Distillation for Fast Diffusion Language Models

Aidar Myrzakhan, Zhiqiang Shen · Oct 26

Proposed dSpark, a schedule distillation framework for diffusion language models that prioritizes informative token commitments to achieve up to 10x speedups.

For you: Interesting approach to inference acceleration, though specific to diffusion-based LLMs rather than standard autoregressive models.

Abstract

Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) can generate multiple tokens in parallel, but often require many decoding rounds to complete a response. Efficient generation depends not only on which tokens are easy to predict, but also on how committing to them helps predict the remaining positions. A highly predictable token may provide little useful context, while revealing a less predictable token can help several other positions become predictable sooner. We propose dSpark, a new schedule distillation-based framework for learning when to commit tokens for faster diffusion language generation. To achieve this, we construct unmasking schedules that prioritize tokens whose reveal makes other positions easier to predict, reducing uncertainty earlier in decoding. We then distill these schedules into the model to enable faster parallel sampling, with a KL-divergence regularizer to help preserve generation quality. We extensively evaluate our approach across mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks. On GSM8K, our method achieves remarkable 10.12$\times$ and 5.12$\times$ speedups over the various baselines on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct, respectively, while maintaining superior task performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/VILA-Lab/dSpark.

EnvSched: Environment-Aware Scheduling for Rollout-Efficient Agent Reinforcement Learning

Sihan Yang, Weichen Bai, Ruichuan An et al. · Oct 27

EnvSched uses online scheduling over dual-relation graphs to prioritize task instances and environments that maximize informative reward differences for agent RL.

For you: Useful data management approach for training and evaluating reinforcement learning agents across complex enterprise environments.

Abstract

As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. Under finite rollout budgets, this raises an important question: can data management at the environment level make agentic reinforcement learning (RL) more efficient by organizing, evaluating, and scheduling task instances together with the environments that determine their tools, states, transitions, and verification? To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. It also improves downstream performance across model sizes at the same training budget.

Abstract

Multimodal transformer models have driven unprecedented performance breakthroughs in modern document understanding. However, the inherent black-box nature of these models remains a major obstacle to their safe deployment in high-stakes sectors. Despite significant progress in explainability for vision and language tasks, explainable AI (XAI) research tailored to multimodal document understanding has received little attention. To address this gap, we present \ourframework{}, the first comprehensive explainability framework that adapts existing XAI methods and evaluation metrics to the multimodal (text, layout, and image) document understanding context, while introducing efficient multi-target adaptations of both methods and metrics for domain-specific tasks such as semantic entity recognition. From Self-Confidence to Statistical Accuracy: Scaling Human-in-the-Loop Evaluation with Direct and LLM-Assisted Auditing

Harine Choi, Chenchen Kuai, Zihao Li et al. · Oct 26

Presents a unified statistical framework for human-in-the-loop and LLM-as-a-judge evaluation using confidence-stratified sampling and error-corrected estimators to optimize evaluation costs.

For you: Provides practical statistical sampling methods for scaling production LLM evaluation and cutting auditing costs.

Abstract

For large language models (LLMs) and multimodal large language models (MLLMs), model-reported self-confidence is often miscalibrated, yet it may still be useful for efficient evaluation. At the same time, LLM-as-a-judge offers a scalable alternative to direct human auditing, but its practical benefit depends on calibration quality and inference cost. This paper develops a unified statistical framework for both evaluation routes under alternative sampling strategies. For direct auditing, confidence-stratified sampling is used to recover population accuracy from partially verified outputs without requiring calibrated confidence. Finite-sample uncertainty is characterized, and the analysis shows how highly uneven confidence strata affect audit efficiency and label allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, the judge is modeled through sensitivity and false-positive rate, yielding an error-corrected estimator with calibration uncertainty. The two routes are then compared through human-label and total-cost efficiency boundaries. Experiments on MMLU-Pro and MMMU-Pro show that miscalibrated self-confidence can still support statistically valid stratified auditing. Under highly imbalanced confidence distributions, proportional allocation substantially reduces estimation error relative to equal-per-stratum allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, only a small subset of target--verifier pairs satisfies the idealized human-label efficiency condition, and automated inference costs can further limit potential savings. These findings clarify the statistical and economic conditions under which confidence-guided auditing and automated verification are useful.

Hierarchical Skill Evolution for Coding Agent

Jun Gao, Yun Peng, Changhai Zhou et al. · Oct 26

Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE) progressively distills reusable procedural coding skills from trajectories by aggregating evidence from task to repository levels via an external writer-evaluator loop.

For you: Interesting pattern for distilling and reusing experience across multi-turn agent workflows and coding assistants.

Abstract

Skills provide agents with reusable experience to guide future decisions without modifying the underlying model. However, coding trajectories are often entangled with task- and repository-specific contexts, making it challenging to distill procedural skills that generalize across diverse coding environments. To address this challenge, we present Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE), which progressively distills procedural knowledge from coding trajectories through hierarchical evidence aggregation. HSE first extracts task-level evidence from individual trajectories, then aggregates recurring evidence within the same repository to form repository-specific skill candidates, and finally consolidates consistent patterns across repositories into transferable skills across repositories. The evolution is driven by an external writer-evaluator loop that generates and assesses skills while keeping the underlying coding model frozen. The loop externally updates the writer and evaluator policies to improve subsequent skill generation and assessment. This hierarchy progressively filters task-specific details while preserving procedures that generalize across diverse coding environments. Successful trajectories yield reusable guidance for future tasks, while failed trajectories reveal recurring failure modes that guide avoidance and recovery. We evaluate HSE on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE, using GLM-5.2 across Pi and Claude Code harnesses and GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 with Pi. Across both benchmarks, 10 skills evolved solely from SWE-Gym improve coding performance across all evaluated settings, demonstrating transfer across benchmarks, coding models, and agent harnesses. We further evolve the skill library from target-benchmark trajectories without verifier feedback, relying solely on the learned evaluator for candidate assessment and achieving additional gains through benchmark-tailored skills.

BenchMeUp: Building Software Engineering Benchmarks from Natural-Language Specifications

Islem Bouzenia, Michael Pradel · Oct 27

BenchMeUp automatically builds software engineering benchmarks from natural language specifications using parallel LLM agents, generating 198 valid execution instances across four tasks.

For you: Useful methodology for automatically generating domain-specific evaluation benchmarks and test suites for production code-generation agents.

Abstract

Software-engineering research relies on benchmarks that evaluate new techniques on a set of problem instances. Unfortunately, manually constructing benchmarks is labor-intensive, as researchers must find suitable instances, prepare execution environments, establish expected outcomes, and validate correctness. This paper presents BenchMeUp, an approach for automatically creating software-engineering benchmarks from natural-language specifications. Given a specification defining the desired instances, data sources, and evaluation procedures, BenchMeUp generates search rules and a construction plan, invokes parallel LLM agents that identify candidate instances and construct executable instances, validates the results in fresh execution environments, and packages the instances behind a unified command-line interface. We evaluate the approach on four tasks: program repair, issue reproduction, compiler autotuning, and fixing static-analysis warnings. Targeting 50 instances per task (200 in total), BenchMeUp constructs 198 instances, of which 196 (99\%) are successfully packaged. Search and construction take 36 minutes and \$0.79 per instance, on average. Evaluating eight existing techniques on the generated benchmarks, we find that 189 of 196 packaged instances (96\%) support end-to-end evaluation, allowing users to study the effectiveness of the techniques. Checking every constructed instance against its source, the specification, and its expected outcome, we find that 180 of 198 (91\%) meet all criteria, compared with 10 of 200 instances delivered by Codex (general-purpose coding agent). Overall, we show that LLM-based agents can automate benchmark construction across diverse software-engineering tasks and produce executable instances that existing techniques can use, while validity beyond execution still needs to be checked.

ADTGuard: Adaptive Depth-Time Safeguard for Streaming Inference in Large Language Models

Chuming Wei, Churui Yang, Zihan Zhang et al. · Oct 26

ADTGuard introduces an adaptive depth-time safeguard that checks each token before streaming release, using causal depth routing to catch harmful content early.

For you: Valuable pattern for implementing real-time output moderation and safety guardrails during LLM token streaming.

Abstract

Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate fluent responses to diverse user requests, yet streaming may expose users to harmful content before generation is complete. Existing guardrails face two limitations in this setting. First, post-hoc moderation at response or chunk boundaries delays intervention, risking harmful exposure when content is released before verification. Second, even with token-level detection, coarse response-level supervision does not specify when harmful content begins, while fixed-layer readouts may miss relevant safety signals at other depths. To address these limitations, we introduce ADTGuard, which checks each token before release, progressively refines token-level training targets, and adapts layer selection to the current prefix. ADTGuard models risk across depth and time through Depth-Time Safety Dynamics (DTSD), which integrates safety evidence across layers at each token and uses it to guide risk accumulation over successive tokens. To adapt the choice of observation layers to the current prefix, we propose the Causal Depth Router (CDR). With only response-level labels available, we further develop Risk-Onset Target Refinement (RTR) to progressively construct token-level training targets for both components. Through extensive experiments across multiple safety benchmarks, we demonstrate that ADTGuard consistently achieves strong streaming detection performance, matching or surpassing state-of-the-art safeguards while requiring only 16M trainable parameters and maintaining competitive per-token latency.

Agents & orchestration70% match

Rare, Relevant, and Still Wrong: How Memory Fails LLM Group Decisions

Anouar Imel, Imad Charradi, Abderrahman Sghir et al. · Oct 27 · ▲ 5

CrossProfile evaluates how LLM multi-agent discussion memory compression fails under relevance and rarest-first heuristics, showing accuracy drops from decoys and poor balance of retained evidence.

For you: Directly relevant to designing multi-agent conversation history compression, synthesis nodes, and context window memory management.

Abstract

When an LLM group's discussion is compressed into a short memory for a final decision maker, what should the memory keep? Two natural heuristics keep the rarest claims or the claims relevant to the decision criteria. On hidden-profile tasks both look attractive, because how widely a fact is held is confounded with whether it helps. We introduce CrossProfile, a diagnostic benchmark of 60 templates with automatically verified answer keys, in which the ownership of supporting, decoy, and irrelevant facts is crossed while their text is held fixed (480 tasks). Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and two DeepSeek models, both heuristics fail in predictable places. Selecting by relevance readmits decoys, because decoys are on topic: even a perfect relevance filter loses 23 to 31 points to rarest-first selection in the classic hidden-profile configuration. Rarest-first selection is no better than random selection on average and wins only where the rare facts happen to be the useful ones. In both cases accuracy follows the balance of evidence the memory keeps, not the policy that kept it. The decision model adds a third failure, a form of social proof: it gives extra weight to facts attributed to many agents. Removing the attribution tags, or reassigning them at random with line order fixed, raises accuracy in the classic configuration by 13 to 24 points. In natural discussions the evidence is lost before any memory acts: the complete transcript yields lower accuracy than no notes at all.

MAREval: A Multi-Agent Framework for Evaluating Natural Language Recommendation Explanations

Reza Yousefi Maragheh, Jayesh Uddhav Kudase, Aysenur Inan et al. · Oct 26

MAREval orchestrates planner, moderator, and arbitrator agents with Chain‑of‑Debate prompting and Monte‑Carlo sampling to produce robust, human‑aligned evaluation of recommendation explanations.

For you: Provides a structured multi‑agent evaluation pipeline that can be adapted for LLM app testing.

Abstract

Evaluating natural language explanations in recommender systems is essential for fostering user trust, transparency, and engagement. However, existing evaluation approaches like human evaluations, while accurate, are resource-intensive and impractical at the scale required by modern recommendation platforms. Also, automated methods using single-agent LLMs suffer from prompt sensitivity and inconsistent outputs. To address these challenges, we propose MAREval, a structured multi-agent framework for evaluating recommendation explanations using large language models. MAREval orchestrates (i) a planner agent that uses a novel Chain of Debate (CoD) prompting strategy to coordinate agent roles and enforce logically consistent evaluation plans; (ii) a moderator agent that regulates discussions by mitigating prompt drift; and (iii) an arbitrator agent that aggregates outputs from multiple evaluation rounds and (iv) a Monte Carlo sampling method, improving robustness and alignment with human judgment. We conduct comprehensive evaluations on both public (TopicalChat) and proprietary recommendation datasets, demonstrating that MAREval outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. Comprehensive experiments on a public benchmark and a proprietary e-commerce dataset show that MAREval improves alignment with human judgments over strong singleand multi-agent baselines. Stability analyses indicate substantially lower variability across repeated trials. In a large human-annotation gate, MAREval meets production quality thresholds where prior evaluators fall short, and online A/B testing demonstrates statistically significant improvements in engagement and revenue metrics. These results establish MAREval as a scalable and reliable solution for human-aligned evaluation of recommendation explanations in real-world systems

When All Rollouts Fail: Policy-Compatible Joint Decoding for Hard-Problem Reasoning

Zehao Wang, Hanzhu Chen, Runquan Gui et al. · Oct 27

The authors propose PICO, a policy-compatible joint decoding method for reinforcement learning on hard reasoning problems that blends expert trajectories with the student's token probabilities.

For you: Interesting post-training technique for reasoning models, though more relevant if you fine-tune open weights rather than build app infrastructure.

Abstract

Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) derives its learning signal from binary rewards via on-policy exploration and becomes a powerful paradigm for improving the reasoning of large language models. Yet it faces a learning cliff on problems beyond model's current ability: when every rollout fails, the samples reveal nothing about a correct solution, and group-relative estimators (e.g., GRPO, RLOO) collapse the update to zero advantage. Expert trajectories offer a remedy, but they are incompatible with the current policy and often lie in the student's low-probability regions, so the surviving updates demand large parameter shifts and decay into surface-level imitation rather than transferable reasoning. We argue that a useful learning signal on hard problems must be both verifiably correct and sufficiently compatible with the current policy. We propose Policy-Compatible Reinforcement Learning (PICO), which performs distribution-constrained joint decoding: expert data guides decoding at the token level while preserving the student's relative token probabilities, and a dynamic top-k mechanism confines the sampling distribution under a finite divergence budget, yielding trajectories that are verified and policy-compatible by construction. These trajectories are optimized jointly with on-policy rollouts under a mixed-behavior objective that mitigates their distribution mismatch via importance sampling. Experiments across reasoning diverse datasets and agentic benchmarks demonstrate that PICO consistently outperforms standard RLVR and prior off-policy baselines.

FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents

Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang et al. · Oct 26

FOCUS uses selective branching and active learning for critic training to improve value estimation in long-horizon language model agents.

For you: Advanced reinforcement learning for long-horizon agents, though mostly applicable if you train or fine-tune custom agent policies.

Abstract

Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($λ$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).

Collective Emergent Alignment: Measuring and Amplifying Interaction-Induced Reasoning Alignment in Multi-Agent LLMs

Boxuan Wang, Zhuoyun Li, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn et al. · Oct 25

Studies interaction-induced reasoning alignment in multi-agent LLM systems, showing positive gains on multi-hop reasoning and proposing a fact-level communication framework.

For you: Offers architectural insights for designing multi-agent communication protocols and reducing token overhead in enterprise multi-agent setups.

Abstract

Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems can exhibit emergent behaviors through interaction, yet whether a similar phenomenon exists for reasoning alignment remains largely unexplored. We study whether agent interaction produces additional reference-grounded reasoning alignment beyond matched individual self-refinement, and introduce REMAP (Reference-Entailment-Matrix for Agentic Process), a framework that quantifies this effect through Semantic Coverage and interaction-specific alignment gains. Across diverse model families and two multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, we observe broadly positive interaction gains, particularly on reasoning content that depends on integrating evidence distributed across agents. These gains are consistently associated with independently measured peer-information recovery, while further analysis reveals composition-dependent heterogeneity effects and asymmetric information transfer across agent capability levels. Motivated by these findings, we further introduce a fact-level multi-agent communication framework that replaces full reasoning-trace exchange with compact, gap-aware, and selectively routed information. Our experiments show that compact fact exchange improves reasoning alignment and task performance while reducing communication overhead, whereas more selective communication exposes a trade-off between acquiring distributed information, retaining local content, and communication efficiency. Overall, our results show that collective emergent alignment can be quantified, linked to information-transfer patterns, and used to guide more effective multi-agent communication.

Skill^{infty}: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Skill Optimization

Wangcheng Tao, Zhanglu Yan, Han Wu et al. · National University of Singapore · Oct 25 · ▲ 9

Proposes Skillinfty for recursive self-improvement in language agents, using a meta agent to optimize task skills and iteratively rewrite its own meta-skill optimization procedure.

For you: Offers interesting ideas for advanced agent architectures and self-improving workflows, though complex to implement in standard enterprise settings.

Abstract

Recursive self-improvement (RSI), in which a system also improves the way it improves, is a long-standing aspiration on the path to AGI. Language agents increasingly store procedural knowledge in reusable skills. Optimizing skills offers a direct way to achieve RSI, because the optimization procedure itself can be encoded as a skill. Existing skill optimizers, however, follow a hand-designed procedure. Even methods that evolve a meta-skill confine it to a fixed pipeline and relearn it for each task. We present Skill$^{\infty}$, which makes skill optimization recursively self-improving: a meta agent follows a meta-skill to optimize task skills and optimizes the meta-skill itself using the same procedure. The optimization is agent-led. The meta agent diagnoses execution traces, proposes candidates, requests independent reviews, and allocates the search budget. The meta-skill defines these steps, allowing self-improvement to rewrite them. This meta-skill is also decoupled from task skills, refined on multiple skill optimization tasks and then reused to optimize task skills across diverse domains. As in deployment, skills at both levels are standard packages loaded through native skill discovery. On 83 SkillsBench tasks, Skill$^{\infty}$ reaches a pass rate of 60.7%, compared with 56.4% for human-curated skills and 54.5% for SkillEvolver.

Agent-Conditioned Routing: Diverse Reasoning Paths Within One Frozen Model

Ananth Kalyanasundaram, Ankush Kadu, Athira Gopal et al. · Oct 27

Introduces Agent-Conditioned Routing (ACR), assigning persistent expert-eligibility masks to parallel trajectories in a frozen MoE model to generate diverse reasoning paths.

For you: Provides a method to elicit diverse reasoning paths from a single frozen model without extra fine-tuning or model copies.

Abstract

Generating several solutions can expose alternative methods, but additional trajectories often paraphrase one dominant approach. We investigate whether the conditional capacity of a frozen sparse mixture-of-experts (MoE) model can provide such diversity from one problem prompt, without user-assigned roles, prescribed methods, fine-tuning, or model copies. Agent-Conditioned Routing (ACR) assigns $m$ parallel trajectories persistent, untrained shared/private expert-eligibility masks while preserving the learned, input-dependent token router. On a screened 126-question multi-mode mathematics and physics benchmark, with model judges reading the reported derivations in full, we count the number of problems yielding at least two distinct primary methods among soft-correct solutions in four trajectories. ACR reaches approximately twice as many such problems as the strongest temperature setting under GLM 5.3 (37.3 versus 18.7), and 1.67 times as many under GPT-5.6 Sol (45.00 versus 27.00), at matched output budgets. A blinded four-expert audit on a stratified sample of 30 problems also finds more multi-method solutions with ACR on both backbones, with lower correctness. Matched-eligibility ablations show modest multi-method count advantages for ACR, while token-resampled masks attain higher correctness. An informed sequential prompt elicits more diversity still, using previous solutions and explicit requests for alternative methods. ACR adds about 160 KiB of mask state. In a three-problem microbenchmark capped at 1,024 tokens, its latency and peak VRAM usage remain within 3% of batched temperature sampling.

Heard Enough to Act: Early Tool Calling in Spoken Dialogue Systems

Yizhou Peng, Chen Chen, Donghang Wu et al. · Oct 25

They constructed a synthetic spoken-dialogue corpus with earliest-actionable-point supervision and trained a model for causal, chunk-level tool calling and partial transcript generation.

For you: Interesting techniques for reducing latency via early tool invocation, though specifically tailored for real-time spoken dialogue systems.

Abstract

Latency-sensitive spoken agents must know not only which tool to call, but also when they have heard enough to act. Waiting for the turn to complete delays execution even when the request is already actionable. However, neither existing spoken agents nor spoken dialogue corpora define the earliest point at which a request becomes actionable. We introduce a synthetic spoken-dialogue pipeline that provides earliest-actionable-point supervision, marking when the observed speech grounds a tool and its required arguments. Through controlled task generation, speech synthesis and alignment, and gated annotation, we construct 26,694 dialogues totaling 786 hours across 48 domains, with disjoint training and test argument values. We additionally create an audio rendering of $τ^2$-bench for external evaluation. We train an omni-modal language model on this corpus, enabling causal, chunk-level decisions and partial-transcript generation for downstream call completion. The model achieves tool-state macro-F1 scores of 0.48 and 0.47 on held-out dialogues and the external benchmark, respectively. For correct calls, the estimated mean lead over a turn-end cascade, using the same formatter, is 1.8-2.0 seconds in-domain and 1.1 seconds on $τ^2$-bench. These results demonstrate a data-driven path toward spoken agents that act while listening. We will open-source the pipeline and the training scripts.

Abstract

However, we find that existing coding agents converge to local optima on real-world, expert-written kernels (e.g., FlashInfer). We introduce Evolving Agent Teams, a multi-agent framework that escapes such local optima. It features (1) a central supervisor that diagnoses each local optimum and restructures a team of agents; and (2) a persistent file system that comprises a learning document, an evolution history, and per-team artifacts, grounding restructuring with accumulated experience. Code is available at https://github.com/caoshiyi/EAT.

Instant Software Factories

Wenxin Jiang, Mikola Lysenko · Oct 26

Instant Software Factory uses a swarm of cron‑job agents coordinated via a shared GitHub board to turn coding agents into repeatable production pipelines; observed 15‑x merge rate increase and agents filed 95% of new issues.

For you: Provides a low‑overhead pattern for scaling agent‑driven code updates in your cloud services.

Abstract

Coding agents can quickly build prototypes, but a long tail of fixes, optimizations, tests, and releases separates a prototype from a product. A software factory handles this ongoing work through a repeatable process. We ask whether this work needs dedicated orchestration and how such factories should be judged. Given a tested repository and a coding agent, an instant software factory uses only two coordination inputs, a swarm of cron-job agents with standing objectives and a shared communication board, such as GitHub, where agents coordinate through the traces they leave. We developed a bootstrap prompt designed to set up both inputs from one round of human input, with governance as a separable layer. We distilled it from two manually configured industrial factories, observed for 15 and 17 days, and have not yet evaluated it. In the GitHub-coordinated CLI factory, daily merges rose 15x during agent rollout, an observed association. Its agents also filed 95% of new issues and authored 40 of 58 PRs that changed CI workflows, and at least 5.6% of issues marked as fixed were later found to have incorrect fixes. Unlike an agent scored on a fixed benchmark, a factory thus changes its own workload, evaluator, and measured progress. We formulate factory development as multi-objective optimization under this endogenous feedback. Over five months, we plan to evolve both factories, test the bootstrap on new repositories, and test five falsifiable hypotheses.

AutoRSE: Evaluating LLM Agents as Recommendation Engineers for Self-Evolving Recommender Systems

Rui Tian, Yi Fang, Yejin Hua et al. · Oct 27

AutoRSE provides a benchmark suite for LLM agents improving recommender systems, showing ~16% average gain mainly from engineering tweaks.

For you: Offers a concrete framework to evaluate and improve reliability of your own LLM agents.

Abstract

Recommender Systems Engineer Agents (RSE-Agents), which use LLM-based agents to autonomously improve recommendation algorithms, are emerging as a promising paradigm. However, their capabilities and limitations remain poorly understood due to the lack of a unified, standardized evaluation framework and systematic trajectory-level analysis. To address this gap, we introduce AutoRSE, an evaluation framework spanning six representative industrial-grade recommendation tasks, and conduct a large-scale empirical study of three frontier LLMs across three widely used agent harnesses. Our results show that RSE-Agents consistently enhance the performance of backbones on five of the six tasks, with an average relative improvement of 16.1%, indicating that they can already autonomously improve recommendation performance. However, trajectory-level analysis also reveals that current RSE-Agents function primarily as engineering optimizers rather than algorithmic improvers: their gains are largely driven by hyperparameter tuning, training strategies, and model ensembling, while genuine algorithmic innovations are rare. Since the largest gains in recommender systems often stem from algorithmic advances, this behavior underscores a fundamental limitation of current RSE-Agents. To probe this limitation, we perform idea-injection experiments and show that agents equipped with innovative ideas from frontier research or human experts achieve substantially larger improvements than those relying on self-generated ideas. These results suggest that idea generation is a central bottleneck of current RSE-Agents, highlights a key direction for the future recommendation system research. Our implementation is publicly available on GitHub: https://github.com/Ray-Tiann/AutoRSE.

TANDEM: Balancing Centralized and Decentralized Critics to Address the Information Gap in Complex Multi-Agent Environments

Alireza Saleh Abadi, Leen-Kiat Soh, Adam Eck et al. · Oct 27

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution.

For you: Matches: Agents & orchestration, Evaluation & reliability, Reasoning & post-training

Abstract

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution. The policies are optimized to rely on information they will not have when they act, an information gap between training and execution. It widens under partial observability, observation noise, and openness, where the set of agents and tasks present changes during an episode. Existing methods transfer centralized guidance in one direction only, removing or distilling the critic once, so the policies never rehearse acting without the critic while they can still be corrected. We propose TANDEM, which keeps one centralized critic and reads it two ways at each training step: a centralized read from the global state, and a decentralized read restricted to the information each agent will have at execution. A parameter-free gate compares the two reads for each agent and trains the policy on the decentralized read where they agree, reinstating the centralized read only where the local view is misleading. A shared target pulls the decentralized read toward the centralized one. We formalize the setting as a task- and agent-open decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (TAO-Dec-POMDP) and evaluate TANDEM on three cooperative domains, each isolating one form of the information gap: discovery for partial observability, sampling for observation noise, and openness in wildfire suppression. We compare against six CTDE baselines and ablate the gate and the shared target; the supplementary material holds the full variant sweep, diagnostics, and learning curves. We measure return at execution, once the centralized critic is removed. TANDEM is the best method on eight of the nine setups and never significantly worse than any baseline.
