OA-RAG: Ontology-based Agentic RAG for Faithful LLM Reasoning

Hui Yang, Jiaoyan Chen, Yongsheng Gao · Oct 26

OA‑RAG merges formal ontology reasoning with LLM generation, scoring evidence grounding and achieving top results on ontology‑based and medical QA benchmarks.

For you: Ontology‑guided grounding technique can be adapted to other knowledge bases.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) perform well across many real-world tasks but remain prone to hallucination, limiting their reliability in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare, where answers must be grounded in established knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) mitigates this problem by incorporating external information, but often overlooks logical relationships and relies heavily on the LLM's own reasoning, which can compromise answer faithfulness. We propose OA-RAG, an agent that integrates formal reasoning with LLM reasoning, drawing on both the logical structure and informal annotations of domain ontologies. For each answer, OA-RAG reports the ontology observations used as evidence and a model-assessed grounding level (entailed, supported, related, or unrelated) that characterises the strength of that evidence. Across benchmarks derived from three large-scale ontologies and six real-world medical question-answering tasks, OA-RAG achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated ontology-fine-tuned and popular RAG methods, with particularly strong results on reasoning-intensive tasks. The reported grounding levels also offer a useful indication of answer reliability, with stronger grounding generally associated with higher accuracy. Finally, our results demonstrate the value of ontologies as knowledge sources: OA-RAG is competitive with, and sometimes outperforms, domain-specific RAG systems that draw on sources such as textbooks.

What Outcome RL Buys: Finishing, Reliability, Rarely Reach

Shan Yang · Oct 25 · ▲ 4

Analyzing 20 RL models under fixed token budgets reveals that Outcome RL gains mostly improve finishing (answering before timeout) rather than core reasoning reach, though reliability improves when token limits are generous.

For you: Provides useful perspective when evaluating reasoning models and setting token budgets for production apps.

Abstract

Outcome RL raises pass@1 but not pass@k at large k (Yue et al., 2025). But every pass@1 is scored under a token budget. Every recipe also trains under one. On physics and math problems at fixed token budgets, we compare the pass@1 of 20 released RL models (1.5B to 32B) with that of the thinking models they started from. We ask whether each pass@1 gain is changed behaviour or new knowledge. We divide each change in pass@1 into three parts. Finishing measures answering before the budget runs out. Reliability measures being right once finished. Reach measures problems RL solves that its base answered wrongly in all eight tries, minus problems the base solves that RL answered wrongly in all eight. We find four things. (1) Where the base often hits the token budget before answering, most of the change, gain or loss, is finishing (82% at 4k and 8k). Given more tokens, the base closes most of the finishing gap itself. (2) Where the base has enough tokens, the gain is reliability: the RL model is graded right more often, even on problems both finish. (3) By our definition, RL rarely solves what its base cannot. Reach adds at most 2.1 percentage points on any of our four physics and math sets. We checked 144 physics problems that only the RL model solved. Of these, 3.5% hold up when resampled or read. In a random sample, nine of ten new RL steps a human confirmed also appear in the base's samples. (4) In a small-scale RL run on an 8B model, the gain on new problems is finishing, with reliability flat. Supplying each problem's physical setup was not shown to improve on plain RL.

Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents

Mingyu Zhao, Shuhang Lin, Ligong Han et al. · Oct 27

A metacognitive controller for diffusion language models dynamically adjusts token generation length and finalization based on agent interaction needs, reducing FLOPs by 20.6%.

For you: Interesting inference-cost reduction technique, though tied to the specific paradigm of diffusion language models rather than standard autoregressive LLMs.

Abstract

Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.

Improving Reasoning with Rollout Agreement for Group-Based Policy Optimization

Sohyun Son, Yerim Hong, Jaeyun Park et al. · Oct 26

The authors show low-agreement correct-answer rollouts contain more spurious reasoning, and introduce RoAR to add reasoning-level agreement rewards to GRPO for better math reasoning.

For you: Provides insights into rollout verification and alignment techniques that could apply to reasoning-heavy agent steps.

Abstract

Recent methods for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) use rollout diversity to improve reasoning performance or training efficiency. Despite these gains, the extent to which differences among correct-answer rollouts reflect useful alternative reasoning or unresolved errors remains poorly understood. We investigate this question through a blind paired analysis of a model trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), comparing the highest- and lowest-agreement correct-answer traces for the same problem. Across 126 pairs, low-agreement traces have more spurious reasoning than high-agreement traces (35% vs. 22%), including internal inconsistencies and misuse of problem conditions. Based on these findings, we propose RoAR (Rollout-Agreement Reward), which adds reasoning-level agreement to GRPO's rewards for verified-correct rollouts. RoAR computes this signal from existing on-policy rollout groups, requiring no human-written rationales, additional sampling, or separately trained process evaluator. On five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, the 1.5B GRPO-RoAR model reaches 55.5% mean pass@1 under zero-shot evaluation using 7K training examples, exceeding GRPO and GRPO-CARE by 6.0 and 5.5 percentage points. A separate blind audit of 100 problems answered correctly by all four 1.5B models finds spurious rates of 18% for GRPO-RoAR, 32% for GRPO, 27% for GRPO-CARE, and 37% for DRA-GRPO. Our findings identify reasoning validity as an important consideration when using rollout differences to guide learning. Our code is available at https://github.com/ldi-kyunghee/RoAR.

Mechanistic Insights into LoRA: Layer Sparsity for Adaptive Fine-Tuning via Path Patching

Fei Zuo, Yizhou Huang, Kezhi Wang · Oct 25

Adaptive-LoRA uses mechanistic interpretability to identify task-specific layers via zero-ablation, achieving a 3.5x reduction in adapter parameters while outperforming full-layer LoRA on 11 reasoning tasks.

For you: Useful technique for reducing parameter counts and inference costs when fine-tuning models for specific enterprise tasks.

Abstract

Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has emerged as the dominant paradigm for parameter-efficient fine-tuning of large language models. Standard all-layer LoRA applies adaptation throughout the transformer, despite heterogeneous contributions from its components. In this work, we conduct a systematic mechanistic interpretability analysis to understand how LoRA affects model internals. We observe that LoRA's adaptation effect is highly non-uniform, with significant component-wise and layer-wise sparsity. Most critically, we find that important layers are highly task-dependent, with limited overlap across tasks. Motivated by these insights, we propose Adaptive-LoRA, a calibration-driven method that automatically identifies task-specific layers via frozen-base zero-ablation, enabling efficient layer selection without exhaustive search. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-1.5B across 11 diverse reasoning tasks demonstrate that Adaptive-LoRA outperforms full-layer LoRA while using only 30% of layers, achieving a $3.5\times$ reduction in adapter parameters. This work bridges mechanistic interpretability and practical efficiency, providing both fundamental insights and a principled approach for adaptive fine-tuning.

Constraining the Commit Boundary: A DIO/ODI-Informed Authorization Guardrail for Agentic Entity Resolution

Daniel Beránek · Oct 27

Proposes an authorization boundary and two-phase supervisor for agentic entity resolution to prevent erroneous identity mutations caused by flawed intermediate retrieval steps.

For you: Valuable pattern for building guardrails and authorization boundaries in agentic RAG and data mutation pipelines.

Abstract

Agentic Entity Resolution (ER) systems iteratively plan actions, retrieve evidence, revise hypotheses, and propose graph mutations. An erroneous identity hypothesis may condition later retrieval and produce apparent confirmation. This paper proposes a Digital Identity Optimization (DIO) and Ontology of Digital Identity (ODI)-informed authorization boundary between evidence available for agent reasoning and evidence eligible to authorize persistent identity-changing mutations. A domain-governed constraint-binding function maps selected reconstruction-failure distinctions to explicit identity-preserving constraints. An abstract two-phase supervisor permits a commit only when claim-specific authorization-eligible evidence supports every required proposition, relevant conflicts are resolved, the operation remains within its authorized scope, and relevant state and policy versions remain valid. Admission as UNVERIFIED does not confer authorization eligibility. Separate claim-specific promotion governs evidentiary use; insufficient support or unresolved material conflict leads to deferral. The contribution is a specification connecting diagnostic-to-constraint mapping, evidence promotion, proposition coverage, conflict handling, scope binding, and commit-time version integrity. A proposed evaluation protocol is designed to assess the mapping's incremental value beyond generic authorization and conventional identifier rules. This is a conceptual architecture without implementation or empirical validation, not a theorem of semantic correctness. Its property depends on domain-policy adequacy, evidence promotion, and enforcement. It does not prevent erroneous hypotheses or hypothesis-conditioned retrieval; dependency-aware evidence flow remains future research.

Skill Dreaming: Evolving Repository Skills through Pull Request Streams

Xinyuan Song, Hasibul Haque, Jay Somasundaram et al. · Oct 26

Proposed Skill Dreaming, a framework that learns and evolves repository-specific agent skills directly from pull request streams.

For you: Relevant if building advanced software-engineering agents that need dynamic, self-updating skill libraries.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on reusable skills for complex software engineering tasks, making skill libraries essential for repository development and maintenance. However, repository-specific skills must evolve with changing code structures, implementation conventions, and maintenance requirements. Pull requests (PRs) provide a natural source of such knowledge by recording how repositories evolve through implementation and review. Meanwhile, new PRs must follow repository-specific implementation patterns, modify related components consistently, and ensure that existing tests continue to pass while adding coverage for new functionality. This creates a mutually reinforcing cycle: PRs provide knowledge for updating skills, while these skills improve future PRs, maintain repositories, and support further skill evolution. To enable this co-evolution of PRs, repositories, and skills, we propose Skill Dreaming, a framework that learns from PR streams to guide continuous repository development and maintenance. Given a repository and its PR history, Skill Dreaming builds a repository-specific skill library by extracting executable knowledge from validated PRs and continuously refines the skills as new PR information becomes available. These skills guide coding agents not only in implementing requested features but also in maintaining associated tests, documentation, compatibility, and repository conventions. By integrating PR analysis, skill management, code execution, and continuous integration, Skill Dreaming provides an end-to-end workflow for applying learned skills to repository changes and incorporating subsequent PR outcomes into future skill updates.

Mind the Intent Gap: A Methodology for Collaborative Agent-Driven Software Engineering

Nicolas Tacheny, Tom Mens, Saïd Mahmoudi · Oct 27

Presents CAIA, a collaborative agentic workflow methodology for software teams that enforces explicit intent alignment via a shared knowledge base.

For you: Offers useful governance patterns for managing multi-agent software engineering workflows in production.

Abstract

AI agents have entered software teams, first as tools and now as team members. They produce in minutes what used to take days, yet the expected gain in delivery often fails to appear. We argue that they are entering processes designed for people, which quietly relied on people to cover three of their weaknesses. The team's intent was never fully written down, because colleagues filled the gaps. An AI agent fills them too, though by guessing, and drifts from what the team had in mind. The reasoning behind each deliverable was already lost from one step to the next, and AI agents now inflate and deflate the documents passed along. The reasoning people did alone was partly private and now moves into AI tools that keep it private by nature. We present CAIA, for Collaborative Agentic Intent Alignment, a methodology for software teams that brings agents into the way they work. A knowledge base describes the product that a project maintains. For each Change, a micro-team aligns on its intent before agents evolve the product and propose updates to the Knowledge Base. The proposed CAIA methodology emerged from an industrial need and is already in use at Ni2, with a platform built to support it. We present the methodology and outline our plans to evaluate it further.

How Much Does the Hint Matter? Scope Conditions for Hindsight Self-Distillation in Financial Reasoning

Johnny Yu, Cyril Gorlla · CTGT, Inc. · Oct 25 · ▲ 5

Fin‑SD lets a model self‑distill by critiquing its first error, using a hint‑conditioned teacher to improve multi‑step reasoning without external models.

For you: Provides a low‑cost self‑distillation method to boost reasoning accuracy in LLM pipelines.

Abstract

Financial institutions operating under data-residency constraints are restricted to open-weight models small enough to locally host, which trail frontier systems on multi-step quantitative reasoning. On-policy distillation narrows such gaps by supervising a student on its own rollouts, but traditional OPD requires a strong external teacher, and on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) conditions on a full reference solution. We ask how much of that signal is necessary. We present Fin-SD, a localized hindsight self-distillation recipe for multi-step reasoning. Given a problem the student fails, the student itself identifies the first incorrect reasoning step and writes a short critique of that step. We truncate the student's trajectory immediately before the error (aiming to obtain a correct prefix), condition a frozen copy of the student on the same prefix plus the hint, and distill that hint-conditioned teacher into the student over a short rollout under reverse KL. No external model contributes a gradient. We apply Fin-SD to GPT-OSS-20B on a custom multi-step financial reasoning benchmark that requires deriving formula inputs from realistic scenarios via accounting conventions. We find: (i) replacing the critique with a generic-feedback control matches Fin-SD's entire 3.03 pp accuracy gain over the base model on the FinanceReasoning Hard subset, suggesting the gain comes from where supervision is applied, not hint content; (ii) the model writing the hint does not need to be stronger than the model receiving it: hints from same-family GPT-OSS-120B, at roughly 6x the parameters, yield no materially greater benefits than self-written hints; and (iii) Fin-SD matches on-policy self-distillation from full reference solutions on accuracy while generating 9.5% fewer tokens. With Fin-SD, general reasoning on MMLU-Pro and a fourteen-metric compliance audit on FinTrust show no material regression.

Continual Learning Exam: Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM Agents

Haoyu Zhao, Sitian Tu, Yuxuan Huang et al. · University College London · Oct 24 · ▲ 49

CLE benchmark measures LLM agents’ ability to improve over 120 tasks arranged in five‑step ladders, using feedback‑driven revisions and human rubrics.

For you: Provides realistic evaluation suite for long‑term agent improvement.

Abstract

Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), a large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as a five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare a single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harness-model configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield a median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities.

Work Agents: Systems, Capabilities, and Optimization

Zishan Xu, Enjun Du, Mingqi Gao et al. · Shanghai Jiao Tong University · Oct 25 · ▲ 115

Presents a system‑level framework for work agents, defining persistence/open‑ness dimensions, capability families, and optimization strategies for reliable digital work.

For you: Offers taxonomy to design and optimize production LLM agents.

Abstract

As foundation models evolve from information generation to digital work execution, agents are increasingly capable of completing complex tasks across tools, applications, and workflows. However, existing research lacks a unified perspective on how agents reliably accomplish delegated work. To address this gap, we present a work-centric, system-level perspective that views work agents as systems combining foundation models and runtime harnesses to execute tasks in stateful workspaces and deliver verifiable outcomes. We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine approaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes.

Correlation-Aware Exploration for Long-Trace Reasoning

Junxing Hu, Zicheng Zhang, Zhiqian Zhang et al. · Oct 26

Proposed Info-GRPO, an information-theoretic framework using latent priors to mitigate policy collapse and manage entropy during long-trace reasoning training.

For you: Relevant background on post-training and alignment for reasoning models, though more focused on training than immediate inference deployment.

Abstract

Reasoning models trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) now produce chains of thought thousands of tokens long, and recent studies reveal that such training is prone to policy collapse. Entropy regularization is the standard remedy, but it works by pushing probability mass away from the few tokens that carry it and toward the vast low-probability tail of the vocabulary, and it applies this force once for every token of the reasoning trace. This puts the bonus in direct conflict with the entropy decay that convergence requires, and makes its coefficient difficult to set in the long-trace regime. To resolve this tension, we propose Info-GRPO, an information-theoretic framework that cultivates correlation between the policy and a latent prior. Rather than raising entropy everywhere, Info-GRPO builds on the mutual information between a latent variable and the trajectory it induces: augmenting prompts with latent seeds during training lets the model explore diverse policies correlated with the prior, while the seed-conditioned entropy is guided toward convergence. Diversity is therefore carried across whole reasoning traces rather than by per-token randomness, and exploration no longer fights entropy reduction. Experiments show that Info-GRPO outperforms vanilla GRPO and entropy-regularized GRPO across diverse reasoning benchmarks. On AIME24, it improves $Avg@8$ over GRPO by 3.75%, 1.66%, 4.16%, and 4.17% on four backbones, and it reaches the same majority-vote accuracy with $8\times$ fewer samples. Code and models will be publicly released.

Blindfold: Keeping the Audit Coin Secret from LLM Agents

Wei Zhu, Meiyun Zuo, Huiping Sun · Oct 27

Defined pre-commit noninterference (PNI) for audit-based agent certification, showing that audit coin values must remain secret from the agent's view to prevent biased certificates.

For you: Relevant security consideration if implementing randomized auditing or monitoring layers for production LLM agents.

Abstract

Audit-based certification bounds the violation rate of an LLM agent by auditing a random fraction $\varepsilon$ of actions and reweighting the audited labels; its validity is meant to rest only on the defender's audit coin. We show that it also rests on a confidentiality property: the coin must not interfere with the agent's view before the action commits, at a time the agent chooses. We call this pre-commit noninterference (PNI) and measure its violation by $D_{\mathrm{A}}$, the total-variation distance between the agent's views under the two coin values. PNI is necessary and sufficient for an unbiased certificate against adaptive agents, the worst-case bias is $(1-\varepsilon)D_{\mathrm{A}}$, and no procedure that sees only audited labels can pay less. Leakage composes across steps, a resource shared across episodes opens a channel of computable size, and raising the audit rate cannot restore validity. On a monitor we built, a silent audit changes no output but adds latency that tool timestamps expose, and the agent can enlarge it. A real LLM agent follows an injection that names a visible audit notice (generic, tool, and timing clauses have no effect); over that channel the plain certificate fails on measured labels and, by a post-hoc analysis, in a live run on a pre-registered schedule, while a silent audit restores it. A fresh coin per audited call closes the timing channel; padding only narrows it.

The View from Nowhere: Population-Centered Agent Evolution

Shuo Yan, Ningning Hou, Haoyu He et al. · Oct 26

Introduced Population-Centered Evolution (PCE), improving agent harnesses by distilling population-wide task records and modification histories rather than just parent variants.

For you: Provides a concrete architectural pattern for improving self-improving LLM agent loops using population traces instead of single parents.

Abstract

Self-improving AI systems can revise the designs that govern their own behavior. Yet existing approaches center improvement on selected parent agents and their evolutionary experience, leaving the broader population largely underused. We identify a substantial gap between population coverage (i.e., the tasks solved by at least one evolved variant) and the performance of the best individual agent. This gap shows that useful capabilities and diagnostic evidence emerge across the population without being consolidated into its leading agents. These observations motivate the view from nowhere, a principle for agent evolution: an agent should be improved by interpreting its behavior through population-wide patterns of success, failure, and prior self-modification. We instantiate this principle as Population-Centered Evolution (PCE). PCE distils task records and modification histories from the population into a population block, which captures recurring failures, complementary successes, and the effects of previously attempted modifications to guide improvements to a selected parent's harness. Under matched models, initial harnesses, and evolution schedules, PCE achieves 72.1% success on SWE-bench Verified and 96.7% on Polyglot, improving over a strong group-evolution baseline by 3.2 and 5.9 percentage points. In our evaluation, PCE reduces the gap between population coverage and best-agent performance by one-third on SWE-bench and closes it on Polyglot. These results suggest that treating the population as evidence--not merely as candidates for selection--provides a complementary principle for agent evolution.

Your Agent Acts Differently When You're Around: How User Presence Changes Agent Behavior in Negotiation

Jane Pan, You Lyu, Abe Bohan Hou et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated how human user presence affects agent behavior in negotiation games across 11.2k runs, showing users drive higher payouts (+13.7%) but also induce misaligned behavior.

For you: Important cautionary findings for human-in-the-loop enterprise agent architectures where users interact with bargaining or execution agents.

Abstract

LLM agents increasingly interact with other agents on behalf of users, yet how users in the loop affect their agents' behavior--for better or worse--remains poorly understood. We study this question through the lens of negotiation, where agents must represent users while strategically bargaining with other agents. To isolate the effect of user presence, we develop PRESENT, a controlled evaluation framework that compares negotiation settings with and without an interactive user, while holding the game, objectives, and user preferences fixed across conditions. Across 11.2k runs spanning 7 games and 8 models, we find that user presence significantly changes both outcomes and behavior. Specifically, negotiation parties with a user obtain higher payouts (+13.7% on average) and their agents negotiate more aggressively. Surprisingly, user presence also induces misaligned behavior, both toward counterparties (where its rate roughly doubles) and toward users (where it emerges in 14.3% of games). These misaligned behaviors are uncorrelated with outcomes and persist even when the user sends only templated, neutral messages. These findings expose two blind spots in current evaluation practice: static instructions miss behaviors that emerge only when users are present, and outcome-level metrics cannot detect them.

Multiscale Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

Tao Ren, Hui Yang, Jia Deng et al. · IQuest · Oct 25 · ▲ 379

Proposes Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), anchoring RL policy updates and credit assignment at the turn level for long-horizon LLM agents.

For you: Provides a technique for better credit assignment in multi-turn, long-horizon agent workflows, though RL training setup is required.

Abstract

Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.

Octen-WebGR: Scaling Generative Retrieval to 200 Million Web Pages

Guangwei Xu, Wenkai Zhang, Yawen Liu et al. · Oct 26

Developed Octen-WebGR, indexing 201 million web pages into a 4B parameter language model for generative navigational search.

For you: Interesting scaling perspective for generative retrieval, though enterprise search typically relies on standard hybrid vector-lexical stores.

Abstract

Navigational search aims to find a single target page among hundreds of millions of web pages. Click tables work well for queries seen before but cannot generalise to new formulations, while embedding retrievers generalise across content but often rank navigational targets poorly. We introduce Octen-WebGR, a generative retrieval framework that stores web pages in the parameters of a language model: given a query, the model generates a two-token semantic identifier constrained to a table of real URLs. We develop Octen-WebGR-4B, which indexes 201 million web pages, and evaluate it on real navigational traffic from the month after training. Generative retrieval is most effective on new ways of asking for familiar pages: for unseen queries targeting pages with substantial interaction history, it ranks the correct page first 30.9% of the time, against 17.2% for a dense retriever trained on the same clicks (21.4% with a click prior). Embedding retrieval keeps an advantage on rarely visited pages, so the two approaches generalise on complementary sides, the query and the page. Over all navigational traffic, Octen-WebGR reaches 59.6% top-1 accuracy, against 46.5% for a click-enhanced dense retriever and up to 37.5% for frontier LLMs answering from memory, which cost at least 40 times as much per query. The 4B model runs on a single GPU with 10 GB of memory, compared with 412 GB for exact dense retrieval. Scaling experiments with up to 32 million URLs show that, when every query-page pair is seen equally often, the model size needed grows sublinearly with index size, and that retrieval is constrained more by page exposure than by model capacity. We release Octen-WebGR-4B and Octen-WebGR-0.6B with their identifier tables and inference code.

Multi-State Linear Attention with Generalized Second-Order Routing

Hee Seung Hwang, Sanghyuk Chun, Xindi Wu et al. · Oct 27

Proposed MERLIN, a linear attention method tracking first and second moments of segment keys to improve content-aware aggregation in long-sequence models.

For you: Useful background on efficient long-context attention mechanisms, though architectural modifications require deep model training access.

Abstract

Self-attention plays a key role in long-sequence modeling, but its quadratic time complexity fundamentally limits the scaling of Large Language Models. Recently, Multi-State Linear Attention (MSLA) mechanisms have been proposed to make sequence modeling more efficient by splitting the context into segments and compressing each in its own memory state. MSLA aggregates the information extracted from each memory state using only the mean alignment between a query and each segment's keys, ignoring how the alignment is distributed within the segment. In order to model the relevance of a segment to a query more comprehensively and incorporate the variance of the alignment in the aggregation, we propose MERLIN (MomEnt Routing for multi-state LINear attention), a method that tracks the first and second moments of each segment's keys. Using the moments of keys, a query can recover both the mean and the variance of its alignment with each segment to perform a more content-aware aggregation of per-state readouts. Experiments on multi-state variants of Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and Mamba-2-780M show that MERLIN consistently outperforms existing aggregation methods on various long-context tasks. In particular, MERLIN outperforms baselines on RULER needle-in-a-haystack and long-context QA (+8.3% for Gated DeltaNet-1.3B and +7.0% for Mamba-2-780M) and diverse subtasks in LongBench (+13.5% and +12.0%). It even outperforms baselines that consume 4x more memory. MERLIN provides a simple and general routing mechanism for improving long-context capabilities of MSLA models.

HiLo-RD: Cross-Budget Self-Distillation for Masked Diffusion Language Models

Xiang Li, Chengting Yu, Jungang Li et al. · Oct 27

Introduced HiLo-RD, a cross-budget representation distillation method for masked diffusion language models to prevent premature commitment during generation.

For you: Niche relevance for non-autoregressive generation, but mostly outside standard production LLM workflows.

Abstract

We propose HiLo-RD (High-to-Low Budget Representation Distillation) to improve masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) under a limited generation budget. Unlike autoregressive models, MDLMs choose both what to generate and where to commit it. This creates two coupled challenges: predicting useful content before reasoning is complete, and deciding when to fix answer tokens that shape later predictions. We identify budget-induced premature commitment: even when a correct solution fits, the model can fix answer fragments near the window boundary before resolving intermediate steps. These fragments become context for later predictions and redirect subsequent reasoning. Existing post-training methods do not directly transfer the stronger trajectories that the same model follows with a larger window. HiLo-RD addresses this gap by (1) pairing large-window teacher states with cropped small-window views and (2) matching their hidden representations at committed positions. This supervision guides both token prediction and commitment order without an external teacher. Across mathematics benchmarks, HiLo-RD improves low-budget accuracy and shortens responses; these gains extend to code generation without code-domain training. On GSM8K, accuracy rises by 9.4 percentage points as mean readable length falls by about 25%. Attention diagnostics and fixed-budget order interventions support the proposed mechanism. Together, these results show that large-window reasoning can improve small-window decoding.

Beyond Update Counts: A Control Map for Evaluating Test-Time Model Updates

Zehao Zhao, Shangjian Yin, Rui Liu et al. · Oct 26

Introduced Control Map, an evaluation framework for test-time model updates that tracks acquisition, retention, and locality across varied scheduling histories.

For you: Useful background if you evaluate systems incorporating dynamic memory or continual weight updates at test time.

Abstract

Test-time updating lets a language model change persistent state after training---such as model weights, adapter parameters, or a memory module---before or while answering queries. Existing evaluations often compare systems using update count or frequency and performance measured immediately afterward. These summaries omit what is updated, where, when, and in what order. The same total can therefore hide an important difference: one system may answer after one update while another answers after two. We introduce the Control Map, an evaluation framework that records the committed updates preceding each scored answer and separately evaluates behavioral changes, immediate learning (acquisition), later recall (retention), preservation of unrelated behavior (locality), and performance in new settings. We prove when counts alone determine final state and show, under stated assumptions, how differences between pre-answer histories limit differences in average scores. Using ControlMap, our experiments reveal three insights. First, update scheduling matters: at a 760M-parameter checkpoint, 24 schedules with the same update count yield 19.7%--34.2% accuracy. Second, learning an edit does not guarantee that it lasts or remains contained: all six selected CounterFact LoRA settings learn the new information, while ROME retains edits at all 13 operating points but fails locality at each. Third, no editor performs best consistently across models, benchmarks, and tests. Evaluations should therefore report update histories and assess acquisition, retention, and locality separately.

Hierarchical Low-Rank Reparameterization for Parameter-Efficient

Rong Huang, Yi Xiong, Yuan-Yuan Cheng et al. · Oct 27

Hierarchical low-rank reparameterization recursively compresses PEFT parameters into multi-level structures, maintaining strong reasoning performance with fewer parameters.

For you: Relevant background on squeezing parameter efficiency out of PEFT methods like LoRA when fine-tuning open-weights models.

Abstract

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods adapt large models by introducing a small number of trainable parameters. However, we find that these parameters still contain redundancy and can be further compressed while maintaining strong task performance. To this end, we propose a novel hierarchical low-rank reparameterization method. Its core idea is to reorganize the trainable parameters of PEFT methods into an approximately square matrix and then express it via a low-rank decomposition, which can be applied recursively. This forms a multi-level structure with factors compressed level by level, drastically reducing the number of trainable parameters. In our experiments, we combine this reparameterization with LoRA, DoRA, MoSA, and RaSA. We evaluate commonsense reasoning on LLaMA-3.1-8B and Qwen2.5-7B, with additional GSM8K math reasoning experiments on LLaMA-3.1-8B. Under comparable trainable-parameter budgets, our method combined with LoRA or DoRA achieves better performance. Furthermore,when the parameter count of the original methods is compressed by approximately an order of magnitude, our method can still maintain competitive task performance and even surpass the corresponding baselines in average accuracy across the eight commonsense reasoning benchmarks.

FeatureArc: Benchmarking Coding Agents on Evolving Features

Yunlong Wang, Alyssa Wu, Cuiyun Gao et al. · Oct 27

FeatureArc introduces a benchmark and pipeline for evaluating coding agents on evolving features across multiple stages and four programming languages.

For you: Relevant evaluation methodology for coding agents and long-running autonomous developer workflows.

Abstract

A software feature usually evolves over time: developers extend its first version as new use cases and requirements arrive, and each extension builds on code written earlier. Most feature benchmarks evaluate a single request on a fixed codebase. Feature evolution requires evaluating successive extensions on an agent's own earlier code and checking whether the feature remains correct. We introduce FeatureArc to evaluate coding agents on evolving features, together with a pipeline that constructs its tasks from repository histories. The benchmark combines dependencies between successive stages of the same feature, explicit requirements and interface contracts, and executable checks across four programming languages. The pipeline validates fail-to-pass (F2P) tests for new behavior and pass-to-pass (P2P) tests for existing behavior on the reference code before and after each stage. We evaluated 10 coding agents on 73 manually reviewed tasks of 2 to 10 stages. Each agent works in one persistent session and workspace without external test feedback or rollback. The evaluation reveals a gap between first-stage and whole-feature success: pooled success falls from 62.7% at the first stage to 28.5% when the whole feature is tested on the final code. Trajectory analysis identifies inherited defects as a common failure mechanism, present in 72.9% of failed later stages. These earlier errors often remain undetected: in stages following a failure, agents leave the earlier error unnoticed about 95% of the time, and their self-tests rarely revisit earlier stages. Evolving a feature autonomously, without human checks between stages, therefore remains a substantial challenge for current coding agents. At Tencent, the pipeline extends internal evaluations to this setting and guides the development of Hunyuan, Tencent's family of large language models.

Hidden in Plain Sight: an Empirical Study about The Instruction Stacks of Coding Agents

Felipe de Sant'Anna Paixão, Dhyego Tavares, Eduardo Almeida et al. · Oct 27

A longitudinal study of instruction stacks across 1,886 releases of 5 coding agents reveals that system instructions and tool definitions frequently change under the hood.

For you: Good awareness for engineering production agents, highlighting how hidden prompt stacks and tool definitions shift across CLI releases.

Abstract

Coding agents send their models more than the user's prompt: they add instructions, tool definitions, and environmental context that form an instruction stack. This stack guides execution and changes across releases, yet remains largely unexposed to users and insufficiently characterized in research. We present a longitudinal, cross-agent study of the instruction stacks captured in the first model requests of 1,886 releases of Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Qwen Code, and OpenCode. Under controlled conditions, we analyze their composition and evolution and trace changes in the four open-source agents to documented motivations. Our findings show why examining the system prompt or reporting the model alone provides an incomplete account of agent inputs. Tool definitions dominate three agents' latest requests, while occasional concentrated revisions alter instructions governing tool use, attribution, and coding style. Such revisions can complicate how researchers identify agent-authored contributions and interpret generated code. We also found that only 48% of examined change units directly explain the motivation for stack revisions. Across agents, shared capabilities emerge despite increasingly distinct wording. These findings expose an evolving, incompletely documented layer of agent design. The findings motivate documenting captured instruction stacks alongside agent releases, models, and user prompts, and preserving the rationale behind their revisions.

Beyond the Evidence Coherence Trap: Agent-Driven Evidence Selection for Temporally Disordered Fake News Detection

Chi Wang, Min Gao, Yifei Zhang et al. · Oct 27

DelSA addresses the evidence coherence trap in temporally disordered data via a self-updating agent that iteratively refines evidence selection during retrieval.

For you: Relevant if dealing with temporal grounding challenges in RAG, though framed in a specialized misinformation detection domain.

Abstract

Fake news undermines public trust and the integrity of online information ecosystems. Temporally disordered fake news is especially elusive because it rearranges factual events into a false chronology. Individual events may remain factually valid even when their temporal relations are misrepresented. When similar events involving the same entity recur, retrieval may return semantically plausible evidence from the wrong period. Consequently, independent event verification may rely on locally coherent but temporally mismatched evidence and incorrectly validate the article. We call this the \textit{evidence coherence trap}. Because existing benchmarks rarely isolate this setting, we construct ChronoFake, a source-disjoint benchmark of 30,706 articles derived from temporally anchored real events. It distinguishes temporal manipulation from valid narration, including flashbacks. To address this, we propose DelSA, a \underline{D}ocument S\underline{el}ection \underline{S}elf-Updating \underline{A}gent that iteratively refines evidence selection during training and applies a frozen rulebook for one-pass evidence selection at inference. DelSA uses an LLM to characterize candidate evidence, while a verifier-trained reranker balances local relevance against timeline consistency. Finally, the classifier compares the article-implied chronology extracted by the LLM with the evidence-derived chronology to reveal global temporal conflicts. DelSA outperforms existing methods on ChronoFake, while remaining competitive on PolitiFact and GossipCop.

Efficient inference & cost60% match

Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation

Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu et al. · Oct 26

Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation treats the rollout policy as an optimization target to prevent compounding teacher-student mismatches during post-training.

For you: Interesting post-training alignment method, but more relevant if training custom foundational models rather than building application systems.

Abstract

On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.

AgentProofBench: Benchmarking Reusable Verifiers for Web Agents

Jingjie Ning, Xueqi Li, Yi Lu et al. · Carnegie Mellon University · Oct 24 · ▲ 13

AgentProofBench provides 97 web‑agent tasks with paired acceptance‑rule tests and saved execution states; measures reuse of verifiers across families, showing Flash retains 82% and Pro 79% of source programs.

For you: Helps assess how well your agent verification logic generalizes across similar web tasks.

Abstract

Reusable Web-agent verification requires a rule to follow changing requirements and support subsequent execution. We introduce AgentProofBench, which connects paired tests of a frozen acceptance rule to execution from identical saved openings. The resource spans 97 tasks, 79 families, and four Web services. Experiments with DeepSeek Flash/Pro establish two linked measurements. Across 48 families, complete-panel and decision-replay tests retain 82/96 Flash and 79/96 Pro source programs; paired reuse-rebuild BA intervals are [-5.47, 1.57] and [-10.16, 0.00] percentage points. Across 22 execution families, deployment-state panel correctness separates program assignments whose two saved openings are both delivered correctly: 32/33 assignments with error-free panels, versus 1/11 with panel errors. All reference-defined program-gate regressions and withheld deliveries occur in the latter group. Neutral continuation attains the highest correct delivery under the tested generic feedback, providing a concrete baseline for measuring the value added by a gate. Released instructions, states, programs, reference rules, and trajectories connect each test failure to its execution consequences. AgentProofBench makes reusable verification assessable at both the requirement and policy levels.

Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception

Lai Wei, Yatai Li, Liangbo He et al. · Oct 27 · ▲ 28

Presents EvidenceConnect, a data synthesis framework using zoomed image regions to train multimodal models on connecting visual evidence across regions.

For you: Relevant if expanding document processing pipelines to handle complex technical drawings or charts requiring multi-step visual reasoning.

Abstract

Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.

SetGRPO: Calibrating Grader-Induced Policy Optimization

Jiale Huang, Zixu Li, Zhiwei Chen et al. · Oct 27

SetGRPO introduces set-valued joint reward calibration and geometry-guided safe recovery to stabilize rubric-based reinforcement learning against grader randomness.

For you: Useful background on stabilizing RLHF and reward model optimization, but quite theoretical for day-to-day application engineering.

Abstract

Rubric-based reinforcement learning uses fine-grained grader feedback to train large language models on open-ended tasks, but grader randomness can alter GRPO updates in ways that score discrepancies alone cannot characterize. Because GRPO jointly normalizes rewards within each response group and combines the resulting training coefficients with response-level gradients, a scoring discrepancy can shift the coefficients of the entire group and induce policy-dependent update changes. Selective execution further affects the direction-reversal risk of retained updates. To address this, we propose Set-Valued Group Relative Policy Optimization (SetGRPO). It comprises three modules: (a) Joint Reward Set Calibration constructs a joint uncertainty set for the training coefficients; (b) Geometry-Guided Safe Recovery maps candidate coefficients into the current policy's local update geometry and determines the largest safe recovery ratio for each response group; and (c) Selective Risk-Gated Optimization selects a global threshold through independent risk calibration to control the conditional direction-reversal risk of accepted updates while preserving the original GRPO objective. Under exchangeability, SetGRPO provides finite-sample marginal coverage for the joint coefficient set and simultaneous risk control across candidate thresholds. On ten open-ended benchmarks spanning three domains, SetGRPO achieves the highest macro average among all compared training methods on both Qwen3.5 and Llama3.2 model families.

Benchmarking LLM Agents for Behaviorally Adequate Test Generation from Software Requirements

Binhang Qi, Yiming Liu, Yun Lin et al. · Oct 26

The paper introduces Req2Test-Bench, a benchmark of 5,086 execution-validated instances to evaluate LLM agents on generating behavior-driven development tests from natural language requirements.

For you: Provides a benchmark for requirements-to-code generation, but peripheral to your focus on enterprise search, RAG, and agent orchestration.

Abstract

As large language model agents increasingly compile natural-language requirements into repository-level code, requirement-to-test generation grows in importance to provide validation constraints (or harness) to guide code generation. Yet it remains unclear whether current agents can generate tests that validate the required behavior when the implementation of that behavior is withheld. Existing test-generation benchmarks mostly focus on white-box testing, whereas the requirement--test-related study usually requires evaluation on semantic alignment between the given documents and the generated tests. We introduce Req2Test-Bench, a benchmark containing 5,086 execution-validated instances recovered from developer-authored behavior-driven development (BDD) artifacts in 8 open-source projects, spanning 5 programming languages and 4 BDD frameworks. Each instance pairs a natural-language requirement in Gherkin with its developer-written Test Implementation. Given the requirement, target interfaces of the required behavior, an agent must generate a complete, potentially multi-file Test Implementation. Evaluation of 8 agent--model--reasoning configurations reveals a substantial gap between execution success and behavioral adequacy, which is measured strictly as kill-completeness: a kill-complete test kills every mutant that the ground-truth test kills. The best-performing configuration, Codex with DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, produces passing tests for 82.8% of instances, yet only 33.8% are behaviorally adequate. We find that (1) scaling up agents yields sharply diminishing returns in behavioral adequacy relative to generation cost; (2) under-specified requirements cause the information gap, including ambiguous setup and implicit expected outcomes; and (3) requirement-grounded harnesses that steer agent non-determinism offer a promising direction for improvement.

Reva: Real-Time Vulnerability Detection through Contextual Probing of Large Language Models

Amine Lbath, Massih-Reza Amini · Oct 26

REVA compresses multi-step agentic vulnerability analysis into a single forward pass by providing pre-computed context and code understanding to an LLM.

For you: Interesting latency reduction approach for specialized code analysis, but too narrow for general enterprise LLM engineering.

Abstract

A vulnerability costs more to fix the later it is discovered. As coding agents generate code faster than developers can review it, software needs a first line of defense screening each new function in real time. LLM agents have recently become capable of finding real-world vulnerabilities by working as a security expert would: they run analysis tools, explore the surrounding code, test hypotheses and weigh the evidence before forming their own judgement. This reasoning, however, spans many model calls, so that each verdict takes minutes of compute, far too long for real-time screening. Faster detectors, which fine-tune deep learning models or prompt an LLM for a direct verdict, are less accurate: they judge a function without the context an agent gathers, and an LLM's generated verdict is unreliable even when given some of it. We present REVA (REal-time Vulnerability Analysis), an AI vulnerability detector that brings the security context and code understanding components behind agentic detectors into a single forward pass of an LLM. For each new function, REVA gives the LLM a pre-computed threat model of its module, lightweight static analysis findings and call-graph information. REVA leverages a trained probe to read the LLM's internal representations of the new function in this context. On PrimeVul's held-out test set, REVA on GLM-5.3-Flash reaches 60.0% accuracy in about one second per function. This matches VulTriage (59.2%), the state-of-the-art agentic detector, run on the same LLM, while being over 250 times faster and less than a tenth as costly. On the same prompt, the probe is 14% to 17% more accurate than the LLM's own direct verdict. Across three open LLMs, REVA is at least as accurate as VulTriage, and about 10% more accurate on two of them. An LLM's internal representations thus encode much of what agentic detectors obtain through reasoning.

S3AC: Stabilized, Selective, and Scalable Actor-Critic for LLM Reasoning

Shuo Shen, Guangran Cheng, Chengqi Lyu et al. · Shanghai AI Laboratory · Oct 25 · ▲ 33

S3AC introduces a stabilized actor-critic method with token-level credit assignment and difficulty-aware prompt sampling to improve long-chain reasoning.

For you: Pure post-training research; interesting for reasoning models but less directly applicable unless you are training custom base models.

Abstract

Critic-free methods are widely used in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, avoiding difficult and costly critic training. Common alternatives lack token-level credit assignment and typically rely on grouped rollouts to estimate prompt-specific baselines. A critic can address both limitations, but long-CoT critic training is hindered by insufficient coverage of trajectories with diverse lengths and early gradients dominated by the newly initialized value head. We introduce S3AC (Stabilized, Selective, and Scalable Actor-Critic) for LLM reasoning, built around a practical critic-training recipe. The trained critic provides token-specific advantages and prompt-specific baselines. Using the prompt-level baseline to estimate difficulty, we propose Difficulty-Aware Prompt Sampling (DAPS). DAPS combines this estimate with outcome history to select prompts before generation, reducing rollout waste without offline difficulty initialization. S3AC improves group-rollout performance across dense and mixture-of-experts models from 1.5B to 397B parameters, outperforming a modern GRPO variant (GRPO-X). At 35B, it sustains single-rollout training while tested critic-free baselines deteriorate after initial gains.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) agents are extending agent-mediated commerce on the Web from shopping for consumers to operating businesses for sellers. In online-to-offline (O2O) commerce, decisions are issued through Web-based platforms but realized through costly physical execution. Evaluating such agents requires going beyond individual transactions to ask whether decisions made online turn into sustained economic returns. We introduce AutoVend, a benchmark in which one LLM agent manages 20 vending machines across five site types through a Web-based operating interface for 180 simulated days. Operational analyses show that gains in individual metrics do not consistently yield stronger economic growth, as inventory and execution costs erode these gains. ProAS: A Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping Framework for Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Bowen Ding, Mingda Wan, Yuhan Chen et al. · LINs lab · Oct 24 · ▲ 25

ProAS introduces progress‑aware credit shaping for agentic RL, using a reference graph to redistribute advantages, raising success to 94.3% on WebShop and 91.9% on ALFWorld.

For you: Could improve multi‑turn agent training, though RL complexity may limit quick adoption.

Abstract

Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as the primary paradigm for optimizing Large Language Model (LLM) agents, yet efficient critic-free algorithms (e.g., GRPO) suffer from a fundamental uniform credit assignment dilemma that broadcasts trajectory-level advantages equally to all actions. Our analysis reveals that this uniformity inherently reinforces structurally unreasonable actions in preferred trajectories and ignores each action's downstream deviations in unpreferred ones. To resolve this without introducing critic overhead, we propose Progress-Aware Advantage Shaping (ProAS), a plug-and-play framework that leverages a dynamically evolving Agentic Reference Graph (ARG) to achieve fine-grained credit assignment via Masking Unreasonable Incentives (MUI) and Asymmetric Penalty Redistribution (APR). Extensive experiments across Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct demonstrate that ProAS universally elevates mainstream group-based baselines to new state-of-the-art performance, achieving peak success rates of 91.9% and 94.3% on ALFWorld, alongside 66.7% and 70.8% on WebShop, respectively. Furthermore, it accelerates training convergence by up to 2.44times while maintaining strong robustness against imperfect or distilled ARGs. Codes: https://github.com/LINs-lab/ProAS

CohortFlow: A Steerable Clinical Cohort Curation Agent with Localized Semantic Correction

Neil Chen, Adrian Serapio, Hanxue Gu et al. · Oct 26

Introduced a steerable clinical cohort agent that uses localized semantic updates on a execution graph to reduce latency and model calls.

For you: Interesting architecture pattern for incremental graph updates, but domain-specific to clinical trial criteria.

Abstract

Language model agents for clinical cohort curation should accommodate evolving eligibility criteria without repeatedly processing unchanged requirements. We introduce CohortFlow, a steerable agent that represents natural language corrections as localized updates to a structured cohort specification and reconciles them with a persistent execution graph. Explicitly recorded semantic dependencies enable affected computations to be selectively invalidated while preserving valid prior results. On 100 scenarios derived from clinical-trial eligibility criteria, comprising 126 correction turns, localized steering achieves 77.0% requirement micro-F1 versus 76.4% for full regeneration using the same 8B model, a small numerical advantage that is not statistically significant ($p=0.493$). It reduces mean correction latency by 64.8% after completion and 44.3% during execution, with 38.1% fewer model calls. In a separate proof-of-concept evaluation over MIMIC, all 13 cohort states execute successfully, while correction trajectories demonstrate selective preservation of unaffected computations and recomputation of changed or dependent nodes. Together, these results show that localized semantic steering can substantially reduce correction latency and model calls while maintaining strong requirement-level accuracy.

On the Sparsity of Direct Preference Optimization: Weight Disentanglement in the NTK Regime

Kensuke Sasaki, Issei Sato · Oct 27

Analyzed DPO's optimization dynamics under NTK linearization, identifying dual sparsity (data-level and parameter-level) that explains sparse parameter updates and aids model merging.

For you: Pure alignment theory; interesting for foundational understanding of preference optimization but lacks immediate application for building LLM apps.

Abstract

Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) has been widely adopted for aligning language models with human preferences. Recent empirical work has revealed that DPO induces remarkably sparse parameter updates, yet no theoretical explanation for this phenomenon has been offered. We analyze DPO's optimization dynamics under a neural tangent kernel linearization and show that the logistic structure of the DPO loss gives rise to an implicit maximum-margin bias. Building on this observation, we introduce the concept of dual sparsity: the DPO update direction is a sparse linear combination (data-level sparsity, driven by support vectors) of sparse gradient feature vectors (parameter-level sparsity, arising from gradient cancellation between preferred and dispreferred responses). We formalize this structure in our main theorem, bound the number of support pairs by a margin-dependent spectral quantity, and present a framework relating dual sparsity to weight disentanglement---a condition favorable for model merging. Experiments on GPT-2 Small and Medium and on 7--9B instruction-tuned models (Llama-3-8B, Mistral-7B, Gemma-2-9B) with the Anthropic HH-RLHF and UltraFeedback datasets confirm both sources of sparsity, and experiments on GPT-2 show that DPO reduces cross-task interference by up to $35{\times}$ compared to supervised fine-tuning.

Robust LLM Data Selection via Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution

Jia Gu, Liang Pang, Huawei Shen · Oct 27

Proposed Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution to reduce surface-form bias in gradient-based instruction data selection for LLMs.

For you: Too academic for immediate pipeline work, though useful background if building custom fine-tuning data curation pipelines.

Abstract

Gradient-based data attribution has become an important tool for high-quality data selection in the instruction fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs), as it estimates the contribution of training examples through their influence on model optimization. However, we find that existing global gradient attribution methods suffer from surface-form bias: minor changes to semantically irrelevant details, such as wording or formatting, can produce highly inconsistent attribution scores even when the underlying content remains unchanged. To address this issue, we propose Content-Invariant Gradient Attribution (CIGA), a robust framework for instruction data selection. CIGA first identifies layers whose gradients are both influential for attribution and stable under semantics-preserving surface-form perturbations, thereby reducing formatting noise in gradient features. It then dynamically fuses these attribution signals with semantic representations of the input, combining optimization-level influence with content-level information. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show that CIGA produces more stable rankings under surface-form perturbations while preserving downstream capabilities and improving average performance. CIGA also reduces attribution cost, offering a robust and efficient approach to data selection for LLM instruction tuning.

Do Coding Agents Still Need a Debugger? Frontier-Model-Based Fault Localization at the Accuracy Ceiling

Christoph Daxerer, Johann Glock, Christian Macho et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated coding agents on fault localization with and without debuggers, finding frontier models achieve high static accuracy.

For you: Provides background on coding agent capabilities, but mostly tests LeetCode-style debugging tasks.

Abstract

Background: To localize a fault, a coding agent can reason from source code alone or use debugger assistance by running the program and stepping through it in an interactive debugger. While earlier agents struggled with static execution tracing and debugger operation, frontier LLMs have improved at both. Objective: We evaluate how accurately coding agents localize faults without tools across original and transformed programs, how much debugger assistance improves accuracy and at what cost, and how agents use it when optional. Methods: We seed 610 single-line faults into 144 LeetCodeDataset solutions and rewrite each faulty program with five recipes of behavior-preserving transformations. Agents based on Sol 6.1, Luna 6, and Qwen 3.8 localize each fault with no, optional, or encouraged debugger assistance. Results: Without tools, the agents localized 98.2 to 99.9% of faults in original and 96.7 to 99.9% in transformed programs, losing accuracy mainly where recipes restructured loops and conditions. Encouraged assistance removed 63% and 61% of Luna 6's and Qwen 3.8's remaining transformed errors (+2.1 and +1.3 percentage points) at 14.7 to 27.0 times the token use, while Sol 6.1 stayed at 99.9%. With optional assistance, Luna 6 and Qwen 3.8 still gained +1.5 and +1.3 points, though the agents used the interactive debugger in only 0.2 to 6.2% of runs. Conclusions: On short, precisely specified programs, current coding agents localize nearly all faults from source code alone, and debugger assistance removes most errors that remain. Given the choice, agents executed the program far more often than they stepped through it, yet kept most of the benefit.

The Trinity:Capability-Driven Recursive Self-Improvement for Multimodal Agents

Weidong Tang, Ruiheng Wang, Jingxuan Zhang et al. · National University of Singapore · Oct 25 · ▲ 385

Introduces The Trinity, a self-improvement framework that couples world generation with agent harness evolution using a hierarchical capability tree.

For you: Interesting approach to agent harness evolution, but heavily focused on multimodal world generation rather than enterprise application workflows.

Abstract

Multimodal intelligence can be viewed as human-guided co-evolution between worlds and agents through interaction. However, scaling this process faces two bottlenecks: constructing heterogeneous, verifiable environments is costly, and manual Harness iteration cannot keep pace with growing task diversity and complexity. A key insight is that tasks with different modalities and surface forms can share the same underlying capability requirements. We therefore introduce The Trinity, a recursive self-improvement framework coupling world generation with Harness evolution through three roles: Creator, Explorer, and Evolver. Drawing on verified execution trajectories, Evolver shifts the basis of task organization from surface categories to shared requirements, reorganizing the original task tree into a hierarchical capability tree. We introduce Capability-Driven Real2Sim2Real: for heterogeneous tasks at the same node, Creator abstracts their common requirements into simplified, verifiable simulated worlds for capability learning, while evaluation on original tasks measures transfer. Along this capability tree, Explorer progressively evolves reusable capability modules through selective inheritance, forming a Harness tree whose validated updates accumulate across iterations within a fixed topology. The resulting Harnesses specialize in different capabilities, motivating a Capability-Aware Harness Router that uses public task information to select compatible module configurations from the tree. Our evolved Harnesses improve mean performance by 34 points across 28 training benchmarks and 26 points across 18 unseen benchmarks, including two real-world embodied tasks.

h1: A Taste of the Bitter Lesson in Automated Theorem Proving

Skander Moalla, Ahmad Rammal, Niket Patel et al. · Meta Llama · Oct 24 · ▲ 12

Presents h1, a simple harness for Lean 4 theorem proving that uses verifier feedback and self-reflection without complex decomposition workflows.

For you: Demonstrates effective self-reflection and verifier feedback loops, but tailored to formal theorem proving rather than general software agents.

Abstract

While progress in informal mathematics has come largely from scaling the chain-of-thought and agentic reasoning of frontier language models, formal theorem proving has increasingly relied on human-designed workflows around these models, such as planning, decomposition, and orchestrating specialized provers. This raises a bitter-lesson question: can a simple agentic loop that scales test-time compute match these workflows? The answer matters as formal verification must scale to keep up with frontier models producing research mathematics, yet new workflows rarely settle it, as their gains are often confounded with stronger models or more compute. We study this question with h1, a simple harness that gives a model an efficient interface to Lean 4 and keeps it working through verifier feedback and self-reflection, without imposing a decomposition or search structure. h1 reaches state-of-the-art results on PutnamBench, FATE-X, and FormalConjectures100-Solved, settles eight open problems in FormalConjectures, and, at matched models, matches or outperforms specialized workflows such as Goedel-Architect and AlphaProof Nexus, surprisingly showing that a single attempt (pass@1) can often scale to solve open problems. Through ablations and matched-model comparisons with Claude Code and AxProverBase, we find that simplicity alone is not enough: an efficient Lean interface largely determines cost and solve rate. We thus recommend that future work evaluate frontier models with efficient execution feedback before prescribing workflows, at the risk of bitter lessons.

Game-Theoretic Solvers with Bayesian Opponent Modeling and Moral Rhetoric Framing for GLEE

Hayden Arnold · Oct 27

Combined classical game-theoretic solvers, online Bayesian opponent modeling, and LLM message generation for bargaining and persuasion games.

For you: Interesting multi-agent negotiation strategies, but too specialized for general enterprise agent architectures.

Abstract

We describe the agent we submitted to the GLEE Competition, which plays bargaining, negotiation, and persuasion games against a shared pool of human and AI opponents. The agent is built around three central components: a classical game-theoretic solver to compute numeric moves, an online Bayesian layer to estimate opponent archetypes, and a language model to write accompanying messages. We utilize Rubinstein bargaining for bargaining, Faratin concession for negotiation, and Kamenica--Gentzkow disclosure for persuasion, and adapt those models to live opponents using two separate layers: a Bayesian estimate of the opponent's hidden discount factor, reservation value, or signaling reliability, and a Thompson sampling bandit over five moral-foundation framings coupled with an evidence-intuition spectrum to establish message register. Most of the development effort went into the recurring problem that theoretically optimal policies perform poorly in certain live settings; we report associated failure modes, guardrails we built, and our hypotheses. We additionally evaluate our model over 13,200 Monte Carlo games against 11 synthetic opponent archetypes, an ablation of the opponent-modeling layer, and an extensive analysis of 9,337 live-game messages in which moral rhetoric framing in the persuasion setting is strongly correlated with whether the buyer purchased ($χ^2=616.7$, $p \approx 3.7\times10^{-132}$), surviving controls for quality, round, and configuration.

ETC-Bench: A Multilingual Benchmark for Evaluation-Side Transcript Canonicalization

Zhiheng Qian, Junhao Du, Jing Peng et al. · Oct 26

Introduced ETC-Bench, a multilingual benchmark for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization, along with an agent canonicalization skill.

For you: Too narrow domain (speech transcript normalization) to be broadly applicable to standard RAG or agents.

Abstract

Written transcripts of the same speech can differ in ways that are not uniquely determined by the speech signal. We formulate evaluation-side transcript canonicalization (ETC), which maps multiple surface forms to a single canonical form when their differences are not determined by speech. To our knowledge, ETC-Bench is the first and most comprehensive multilingual benchmark for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization, covering 181 phenomenon types across 12 languages. Each instance is a minimal pair that shares a sentence context and differs only in a target span exhibiting one phenomenon. To evaluate both canonicalization success and semantic preservation, we propose Semantic-preserving Exact-match Normalization Accuracy (SENA), which combines exact agreement between independently canonicalized pair members with a pair-relative BERTScore constraint against information loss. We further introduce the Taxonomy-guided Agent Canonicalization Skill (TACS), which provides LLM agents with phenomenon descriptions and explicit canonicalization contracts. Experiments with five baseline configurations and three TACS-based agents show that the agents outperform the baseline systems on shared languages. ETC-Bench provides a systematic testbed for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization across languages.
