PRISM-BENCH: Diagnosing Grounded and Faithful Multimodal Reasoning Beyond Final-Answer Accuracy

Vansh Bhatia, Mehdi Rezagholizadeh, Parsa Ashrafi Fashi et al. · Oct 26

The paper introduces PRISM-BENCH to evaluate multimodal reasoning beyond accuracy, showing via perception probes that 85% of frontier model failures stem from misperception rather than reasoning.

For you: Useful background for multimodal evaluation and grounding, though focused heavily on vision-language models.

Abstract

Final-answer accuracy reports whether a vision-language model was right, but not whether its answer was grounded in the image, whether its stated reasoning reflects that evidence, or what the answer cost to produce. Those are the properties that decide whether a system can be deployed. We present PRISM-BENCH, a benchmark of 1,000 expert-curated questions spanning five under-represented reasoning categories, in which every item carries an idealized reasoning trace written by one human annotator and validated by another. The traces support two diagnostics that accuracy cannot provide. A perception probe, in which the model answers from a description it wrote itself, attributes roughly 85% of frontier-model failures to misperception and places the bottleneck at grounding. Trace alignment predicts answer correctness for all 25 systems tested (macro AUROC 0.66) and survives length control, so it measures reasoning content and not verbosity. We further introduce CORE, an efficiency-aware metric that reorders 24.2% of model pairs relative to accuracy while remaining uncorrelated with response length. Across 25 open and closed systems, including GPT-5 and Claude Opus 4.6, the best accuracy is 66.0%. On the four categories with a human baseline the best system trails humans by 18.4 points, and recent frontier gains fall on recognition and not on spatial grounding.

M4Bench: Evaluating Procedural Specification for Clinical EHR Derivation Agents

Hannes Ill, Rafi Al Attrach, Rajna Fani et al. · Oct 27

M4Bench evaluates AI coding agents on 28 clinical EHR derivation tasks, showing that specification completeness accounts for most performance gains rather than generic skills.

For you: Useful insights on prompt engineering and specification completeness, though domain is clinical records.

Abstract

Clinical research over electronic health records depends on derived variables whose definitions sit outside the schema, in item codes, time windows, missingness rules, and output-grain choices that change cohorts and downstream conclusions. We introduce M4Bench, 28 clinical derivation tasks across 15 task families on MIMIC-IV and eICU, to test whether AI coding agents can reproduce such derivations and how clinician-reviewed agent skills change their results. Its controls decompose the value of skills into specification completeness, delivery format, and schema familiarity. In a Docker-isolated Codex campaign (GPT 5.4 Mini and GPT 5.5), skills raise reward by 0.136 over the no-skill baseline, with 26 of 28 tasks improving. Specification completeness accounts for most of this gain: on an eight-task control subset, an operational spec alone raises reward by 0.162, and skills add only 0.024 on top, indistinguishable from zero. On a ten-task subset, decoy skills score like no skill and raw reference SQL nearly matches targeted skills, arguing against generic context or the skill format as the explanation. Schema obfuscation and restructuring preserve 86% and 70% of the skill gain (GPT 5.4 Mini, 26 MIMIC-IV tasks), a descriptive robustness check rather than a bound on memorization. A descriptive Claude Code replication on all 28 tasks (Opus 4.7 and Sonnet 4.6, 560 runs) is directionally consistent (+0.120, 21 of 28 tasks improving). These gains are partial agreement with the reference, not completed tasks: only 25 of 280 with-skill and 13 of 280 no-skill Codex runs pass every strict check, and skills barely change which rows agents return (key F1 +0.003). Skills are released as a versioned, clinician-reviewable markdown delivery format, with a family-level reference-validity audit. M4Bench releases tasks, skills, evaluation code, and sanitized per-run evidence as an open-source benchmark.

FIDES-Live: Evaluating Behavioral Concordance in Sequential Trading Agents

Arther Tian, Alex Ding, Simon Wu et al. · Oct 26

FIDES-Live audits sequential agent traces across decision validity, commitment adherence, and reporting, revealing divergences between reported and actual behaviors.

For you: Provides a trace-auditing evaluation framework, though demonstrated specifically in a trading domain context.

Abstract

Sequential trading agents produce decisions, orders, execution receipts, account states, and reports that need not agree, even when the final portfolio value is correctly computed. We present FIDES-Live, an operational extension of FIDES from generated strategy concordance to the audit of sequential agent traces. The protocol separates structured decision validity, optional numerical commitment adherence, execution and ledger reconciliation, and receipt- grounded reporting, with explicit coverage and unscorable outcomes. We retrospectively audit ten requested inference routes over 7,299 cryptocurrency account-rounds and a 310-round equity pilot, using real market observations and local paper execution. Cryptocurrency decision validity is 97.35% and structured reporting accuracy is 90.81% among scorable responses, but route-level decision and reporting performance can diverge sharply. Commitment adherence is 94.36% overall; it falls to 62.63% on constraints emitted with nonempty order proposals, compared with 97.56% for valid empty proposals. All retained cash and position transitions reconcile with recorded fills, while execution controls and unavailable quotes explain separate rejection populations. Short-window marked returns provide descriptive context rather than a new test of whether concordance predicts alpha. These observations motivate reporting concordance, evidence coverage, activity, and financial outcomes separately. They do not establish long-run trading skill, semantic faithfulness of explanations, or causal benefits from audit feedback.

DPE-Rec: Diversity-Preserving and Efficient Diffusion Language Model Recommendation for Bundle Construction

Pingchuan Ma, Bohan Jiang, Chengshuai Zhao et al. · Oct 25

DPE-Rec uses diffusion LLMs with semantic‑fork‑aware rollout and pseudo‑trajectory techniques to preserve bundle diversity while reducing inference steps.

For you: Introduces diffusion‑based generation methods that may reduce latency, though applicability to RAG is indirect.

Abstract

Generative bundle construction completes a partially observed bundle by generating its missing items from a large product catalog. Compared to autoregressive LLMs, diffusion LLM emerges as a natural fit for this unordered generation task. Their bidirectional attention provides an inductive bias better aligned with unordered bundle construction. Iterative denoising can reveal multiple tokens per step, potentially reducing inference latency. However, directly applying DLMs to this task presents two key challenges: 1 Despite its flexible reveal order, confidence-guided decoding tends to postpone semantic-fork decisions until refinement tokens constrain their choices, reducing bundle diversity. 2 Revealing more tokens per step can compromise recommendation quality, stifling the potential for faster inference. We introduce DPE-Rec, a diffusion framework for diversity preservation and efficient recommendation. DPE-Rec preserves diversity by introducing semantic-fork-aware GRPO, which prioritizes semantic-fork decisions via autoregressive rollouts. It improves efficiency through pseudo-trajectory distillation, which concentrates learning from the vast random corruption space onto the compact trajectory-induced subspace visited by high-quality decoding, unlocking reliable multi-token decoding. Together, these components enable diverse and accurate bundle construction with substantially fewer denoise steps. Experiments on multiple bundle construction benchmarks show that DPE-Rec improves recommendation performance by 63% on average, and its fast variant achieves 25.6$times$ faster inference on average than existing generative recommendation arts, with minimal loss in recommendation quality.

Watermark Basins: Black-Box Detection of Distilled LLMs

Hongyan Chang, Hady Elsahar, Tom Sander et al. · Oct 27

Proposes WM-Basin, a black-box watermark detector that identifies distilled student models via parameter-space geometric signals left by watermarked teacher traces.

For you: Useful background on model security and IP protection, though rarely an immediate priority for standard enterprise LLM apps.

Abstract

Proprietary language models and their reasoning traces are vulnerable to API-based distillation, where an adversary trains a student model on the teacher model's outputs. To obtain evidence of distillation, model providers can watermark the teacher's outputs, aiming to leave a radioactive signal in the student's outputs. Existing black-box detectors search for such radioactivity using token-level statistics of the suspect model's outputs. However, token-level signals are not always detectable, for instance when the distillation traces are sampled at a low temperature. In this work, we discover the watermark basin, a persistent signal that enables more reliable watermark detection: during fine-tuning, watermarked teacher traces embed a distinct, key-specific geometric signal in parameter space that lies orthogonal to the ordinary task-specific fine-tuning subspace. Building on this, we introduce Watermark Basin Alignment (WM-Basin), a black-box watermark detector that queries the suspect model through its API, without access to its weights or logits. WM-Basin maps generated text to gradient representations using a fixed clean probe model, projects out the ordinary fine-tuning task directions, and tests whether the residual gradient aligns with the watermark basin induced by the watermark key. We evaluate WM-Basin extensively across diverse teacher models and student architectures (from 135M to 8.2B parameters) distilled on mathematical reasoning. At a strict 0.1% false-positive rate, WM-Basin detects 79% of distilled models at n-gram=1 and 45% at n-gram=3, substantially improving over the state-of-the-art token-level baseline (29% and 1.3%, respectively). Because the watermark signal resides in a persistent basin, it survives post-distillation modifications including quantization, pruning, and reinforcement-learning post-training.

God Does Not Play Dice: Enforcing Batch Invariance in Agentic Kernel Generation

Youwei Bao, Chengpeng Wang, Simin Chen · Oct 26

A new framework enforces batch invariance in GPU kernels generated by LLM agents, using static analysis and dynamic checks to prevent numerical drift across batch sizes.

For you: Too low-level (GPU kernel generation and batch invariance), not directly applicable to building production LLM apps or RAG.

Abstract

Large language model (LLM) serving systems dynamically batch user requests for GPU execution, so the same request may be processed alongside different requests across runs. If the GPU kernels lack batch invariance (BI), this changing batch context can alter the numerical output for an unchanged input, undermining reproducibility. Existing kernel generation agents focus on correctness and efficiency but \emph{overlook BI}. We find 19 confirmed BI violations among 83 batch-applicable released artifacts. To address this limitation, we propose \tool, which combines contract-guided static dependency analysis with dynamic validation of bitwise consistency across batch contexts. \tool is designed as a plug-in component for general kernel agents, allowing their original optimization capabilities to be reused while adding BI enforcement. We integrate it as successive acceptance gates in the optimization loop, rejecting structurally risky or dynamically non-invariant candidates before performance benchmarking and returning feedback to the agent. Experiments across three agent frameworks under baseline and BI-aware generation on real world LLM serving tasks show that \tool improves the BI pass rate from 67.6\% to 91.9\% while retaining 91.8\% of the baseline geometric mean speedup. These results demonstrate that \tool can be incorporated into kernel optimization by agents as an additional reliability objective alongside correctness and speed.

ParetoRSI-Gym: Can AI Agents Improve Language Models Across Multiple Domains?

Haojin Wang, Yangzhen Wu, Zeyi Chen et al. · Oct 24 · ▲ 12

Proposed ParetoRSI-Gym to evaluate recursive self-improvement across multiple domains, showing Claude Code can boost generalization scores by 9.2%.

For you: Interesting look at multi-domain agent self-improvement, but more aligned with foundational model training than application engineering.

Abstract

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires an agentic system to design, train, and evaluate its own successor. Data-driven post-training is a central part of that loop, yet existing evaluations measure it on a single benchmark, where an agent selects from a static pool or distills a stronger model to push one score up in one run. Real post-training is multi-domain, and a gain in one domain has to be weighed against a regression in another. We propose ParetoRSI-Gym, in which an agent searches for data, curates it, trains, reads back per-domain results, and repeats, with everything but the data held fixed: the initial LLM, the five domains (math, code, finance, medicine, science), the toolset, and the SFT pipeline. Generalization benchmarks in each domain separate capability gains from overfitting to the scores the agent optimizes against. Across three agent systems, three base models, five rounds and multiple seeds, the strongest system, Claude Code with Opus-5, raises the generalization scores of its proxy-best checkpoint by 9.2% relative to the starting model (+2.3 absolute points), averaged with equal weight over three base models and five domains, with a single-domain relative gain of up to 63% (medicine on Ministral-3-3B). Further analysis shows that the harness matters as much as the model it drives, and that each agent keeps a unique data selection strategy. We see our work as a step towards evaluation of RSI systems in real-world machine learning engineering.

iPrefBench: A User-Centric Benchmark for Personalized Mobile Use Agents

Yuting Ning, Ruijia Cheng, Sushobhan Ghosh et al. · Apple · Oct 25 · ▲ 20

Introduces iPrefBench, a benchmark for evaluating personal mobile-use agents on handling underspecified user tasks through history inference or direct queries.

For you: Too focused on mobile OS environments and end-user personal assistants to yield direct patterns for backend enterprise apps.

Abstract

An agent that truly knows and acts on behalf of its user has been a long-standing promise of AI assistants. Computer-use agents bring this promise within reach by capably executing fully specified tasks across apps. However, real-world user requests are often underspecified. To act correctly for the particular user, an agent must infer some missing information from the user's activity history and ask for other information that only the user can provide, without making unsupported assumptions or burdening the user with unnecessary questions. We present iPrefBench, a benchmark for evaluating the infer and ask capabilities of personal mobile-use agents in an executable iOS environment with an interactive simulated user. Grounded in a coherent persona with 3,051 past activity events, iPrefBench comprises 130 underspecified user tasks across 26 apps. Each omitted user-dependent requirement is, by construction, either history-inferable or ask-only. iPrefBench evaluates personalized mobile agents on both whether agents complete tasks correctly for the user (i.e., personalized task completion) and whether they seek user input when necessary (i.e., user-agent interaction). Evaluation of 7 frontier agents reveals a substantial gap between execution and personalization. Agents achieve high action completion scores (up to 91.6%) but low full task success (up to 47.7%) because personalization lags behind. They especially struggle with ask-only information, failing to request enough necessary information. Our findings highlight a challenge for personalized agents in knowing what to infer and when to ask. We hope that iPrefBench can serve as a valuable testbed for building agents that know their user and work with them.

Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement in the Era of Large Models: Foundation, Risks, Evaluation and Open Challenges

Jiayue Pu, Ang Gao, Jinyi Bai et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 23

Surveys the landscape of trustworthy recursive self-improvement in LLM-based systems, organizing requirements around genuine improvement, constraints, security, and transparency.

For you: Broad theoretical and safety overview; lacks immediate architectural blueprints for near-term production implementation.

Abstract

AI systems built around large models increasingly use experience and feedback to make persistent changes to models, prompts, memories, tools, and workflows. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) extends this process to inductive biases and to the mechanisms that govern future updates, which can themselves be revised and reused across improvement cycles. Yet self-modification alone does not guarantee trustworthy improvement. Reported gains may reflect flawed evaluations, previously acquired capabilities may deteriorate, and compliance with behavioral constraints may weaken. Compromised artifacts or sensitive information may also persist in successor systems. Changes to improvement mechanisms may invalidate earlier verification results and make human oversight less effective. This survey presents an operational definition and process framework for RSI and organizes the requirements for trustworthy RSI into four interconnected themes: genuine and sustained improvement; behavioral constraints, alignment, and fairness; system security and privacy; and transparency and controllability. We examine these themes from three perspectives: Improvement Validity, Constraint Preservation, and Effective Oversight. This analysis covers improvement diagnosis, proposal generation, proposal evaluation, and update implementation (S1--S4), including the recursive application of these stages to improvement mechanisms (RI). We review risks, safeguards, and evaluation evidence for individual updates, effects inherited by successor systems, and revisions to improvement mechanisms, while distinguishing direct RSI findings from evidence in related systems. Finally, we examine evaluation settings, identify requirements for independent and longitudinal assessment, and discuss applications and open research problems.

Novel or Not? PatEx-Gym: Agentic Prior-Art Search for Novelty Assessment in Patent Examination

They built PatEx-Gym, a benchmark and framework for agentic prior-art search in patent examination, along with an annotation-free trajectory synthesis method using document visits as implicit relevance signals.

For you: Narrow domain application (patent examination), though the trajectory synthesis and iterative search methods offer interesting background for complex RAG loops.

Abstract

Patent examination is a challenging real-world task that requires agents to discover sparse prior art, iteratively refine queries, inspect technical evidence, and produce novelty assessments documented in Office Actions (OAs) across heterogeneous scientific and technical domains. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on static and limited candidate sets, leaving the full search--reasoning--assessment process underexplored. We present PatEx-Gym, the first framework for developing and evaluating agents for patent examination, which integrates three components. First, PatEx-Bench provides 1K realistic examination tasks, two large-scale retrieval corpora containing over 1M patents and 52K scientific papers, and a harness runtime supporting iterative search, document visits, reasoning, and OA generation. Second, we introduce PASS, an annotation-free Prior-Art Agentic Search Trajectory Synthesis framework. Our PatEx-Agent achieves competitive performance against substantially larger general-purpose agents. These results show that training on PatEx-SynData enables a compact 4B model to serve as a competitive baseline for prior-art search and patent examination. We view patent examination as a demanding testbed for agentic search and deep research agents.

Harness-Model Collaboration for Long-Horizon Continual Learning

Zhen Bi, Zhizhi Peng, Yan Wang et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 13

HaMo combines rapid retrieval of procedural experience cards with gradual model adaptation to improve long-horizon web agents' retention and cross-task execution.

For you: Matches: Agents & orchestration

Abstract

Long-horizon agents continuously encounter reusable procedural knowledge, yet existing systems typically rely either on model adaptation or external memory, trading persistent learning for rapid reuse. This limitation is especially important for Web agents that repeatedly execute related multi-step workflows. We propose HaMo, a Harness-Model collaborative framework that combines fast non-parametric experience accumulation with slow parametric adaptation. These results highlight the importance of jointly preserving procedural experience and internalizing the competence to transfer and execute it across tasks.

Freeze, Don't Clone: Fine-Tuning with Expressive Policies Without Retaining Offline Data

Sami Nur Islam, Johan Obando-Ceron, Doina Precup et al. · Oct 26

Identified actor-side behavioral cloning failure in offline-to-online RL, showing that freezing online behavioral fitting preserves pretrained priors.

For you: Too academic for near-term production LLM application building, focusing primarily on reinforcement learning algorithms.

Abstract

Offline-to-online reinforcement learning uses offline pretraining to accelerate subsequent online learning. While many methods assume the offline dataset remains available throughout fine-tuning, there are many settings where this is not the case. Prior work attributes failures in this setting primarily to critic recalibration under distribution shift. We identify a complementary actor-side failure: once the offline data disappears, continued behavioral cloning (BC) no longer preserves the offline behavior distribution and instead refits the behavioral model to the agent's limited, and nonstationary, online experience. We demonstrate that stopping online behavioral fitting while preserving the pretrained expressive prior substantially improves no-retention fine-tuning across implicit flow Q-learning (IFQL), flow Q-learning (FQL), and action-chunked RL (QC) on seven OGBench domains. We trace this failure to a behavioral feedback loop: continuing online behavioral cloning rapidly erases the pretrained prior, inducing representational collapse and action diversity loss that starves the critic of useful exploration. Freezing the prior breaks this cycle, preserving offline coverage while allowing value learning to drive continuous improvement.

Strategyproof Deep Reinforcement Learning for Online Resource Allocation

Fariba Dehghan, Sebastian Stein, Vahid Yazdanpanah et al. · Oct 27

Introduces a monotonic neural network RL mechanism guaranteeing strategyproofness for online resource allocation via critical payments.

For you: Shows incentive‑compatible agent design, but may need adaptation for LLM orchestration.

Abstract

Online resource allocation faces two coupled challenges: (1) allocating scarce resources efficiently under uncertain future demand, and (2) ensuring that agents report task valuations and requirements truthfully (strategyproofness). Reinforcement Learning (RL) adapts allocation decisions through experience but does not necessarily ensure strategyproofness. Existing strategyproof RL uses a linear allocation rule, which limits the resource interactions it can represent. Neural mechanism design methods offer greater expressiveness, but provide only approximate incentive guarantees. Against this background, we introduce the first strategyproof deep RL mechanism for online resource allocation. It integrates three novel components: (i) a monotonic neural network that learns nonlinear interactions by construction (higher reported value cannot reduce acceptance, while a longer reported duration or larger resource requirement cannot increase it); (ii) a generalised critical payment: the lowest accepted value, with all other inputs fixed; and (iii) training by either RL or imitation of an existing rule. Together, monotonicity and the critical payment ensure strategyproofness and individual rationality, while the neural rule provides flexibility and imitation improves training efficiency. Empirically, on a four-resource stream with two partner pairs that a linear difference cannot represent, the neural rule achieves 97.8% of an offline welfare upper bound, compared with 93.8% for a linear learner and 89.0% for first-come, first-served, closing 65% and 80% of the remaining optimality gap, respectively. Moreover, imitation reaches 97.5% in only 261 seconds, about 89 times faster than RL.

HARP-LoRA: Hierarchy-Conditioned Rank Projection for Low-Rank Adaptation in Scientific Table Understanding

Hossein Sholehrasa, Mohammadhossein Amini, Doina Caragea et al. · Oct 25

HARP‑LoRA conditions LoRA projections on row/column header hierarchies, improving scientific table understanding without updating the base model, evaluated on 950 PK tables.

For you: Shows a parameter‑efficient fine‑tuning method for structured data, potentially adaptable to other tabular extraction tasks.

Abstract

Tabular data is widely used in scientific applications, where complex layouts, including multi-level headers, merged cells, captions, and footnotes, make information extraction difficult. This is particularly important in pharmacokinetics (PK), where values must be correctly associated with the drug, species, dose, administration route, unit, and experimental condition. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) reduce adaptation cost but do not explicitly incorporate relationships between table values and surrounding headers. We introduce HARP-LoRA, a hierarchy-conditioned rank projection method for low-rank adaptation in scientific table understanding. HARP-LoRA extends projected LoRA by constructing token-specific intrinsic representations from the row- and column-header hierarchies of each data token. These representations condition the low-rank down-projection, incorporating hierarchical context while keeping the pretrained model and random projection tensors frozen. We evaluate HARP-LoRA on 950 manually annotated PK tables from 587 scientific articles and on WikiTableQuestions (WikiTQ) using Llama~3.2 3B, Llama~3.1 8B, and Gemma~3 27B. With Llama~3.1 8B, HARP-LoRA achieves a PK F1-score of 57.64\% and WikiTQ accuracy of 53.15\%, improving over LoRA by 1.42 and 6.56 points, respectively. With Gemma~3 27B, it achieves the best PK F1-score of 70.63\% and WikiTQ accuracy of 56.87\%. These results use only 172K, 197K, and 547K trainable parameters, far fewer than LoRA and TableLoRA. Overall, conditioning lightweight adapters on table-header hierarchies improves extraction from scientific tables and reasoning over flatter general-domain tables while maintaining parameter efficiency. Code is available at: https://github.com/hosseinsholehrasa/HARP-LoRA

Beyond Words Alone: Disentangling Distributed Evidence for Visually Grounded Cultural Translation

Dingming Li, Yiwen Qiu, Linjuan Wu et al. · Oct 26

Abstract

Social media enables cross-lingual communication, yet the meaning of user-generated content (UGC) is often distributed across text, images, and cultural knowledge, making translation challenging. Existing evaluations typically treat images as optional and assume textual information is sufficient, obscuring whether errors stem from missing visual evidence or inadequate cultural adaptation. We introduce \textbf{Culture-MMT}, a diagnostic benchmark for \textbf{distributed-evidence pragmatic reconstruction} in social-media translation. Human-authored descriptions further show that jointly dependent cases remain more challenging even when visual facts are explicit. These results characterize culturally effective social-media translation as {meaning reconstruction from distributed evidence}, highlighting visual grounding and cultural-pragmatic adaptation as distinct model bottlenecks.

Reason or Recite: Complementary Specialists with an LLM Judge for Traffic Anomaly Reasoning

Mayur Kulkarni · Oct 26

Abstract

Road anomalies such as collisions, violations, and sudden hazards demand more from video understanding than detection: a practical system must verify what happened, describe the scene, and explain the cause. We find these competences pull in opposite directions for vision-language models: closed-form questions reward precise, evidence-grounded reasoning, while open-ended answers are scored by similarity to reference text, where matching the annotation style matters as much as reasoning correctly. Abstract

Controlling personality in large language models is important for building conversational agents that can be customized to different users and interaction settings. However, existing personality control methods often face a tradeoff between strong trait expression and natural dialogue generation. We introduce FATE-5, Faithful Alignment of Trait Expression for the Big Five, a two-stage framework that separates personality encoding from naturalness alignment. FATE-5 first learns continuous soft prompt representations of Big Five profiles using dialogue supervision and an auxiliary survey objective. ReForest: Interpretability Meets Reasoning in Isolation Forest

Juyoung Seo, Geonwoo Bang, Dongho Kim et al. · Oct 27

ReForest combines Isolation Forest with LLMs to generate natural-language explanations and dynamically weigh statistical and semantic anomaly scores, achieving a 21% boost in Recall on anomaly detection tasks.

For you: Too domain-specific for general production LLM applications, though the uncertainty-driven ensembling pattern could inspire guardrail architectures.

Abstract

Statistical anomaly detection methods like Isolation Forest are efficient but suffer from a critical semantic gap. They identify numerical rarities but fail to understand the contextual meaning of data, often missing subtle, context-dependent anomalies. Conversely, Large Language Models possess strong semantic reasoning capabilities but are prohibitively expensive and slow for large-scale deployment. To bridge this gap, we propose ReForest, a novel framework that synergizes the statistical efficiency of Isolation Forest with the reasoning power of LLMs. ReForest transforms the latent decision paths of Isolation Forest into natural-language explanations, enabling an LLM to refine anomaly detection through an uncertainty-aware dynamic ensemble. This approach allows the model to adaptively weigh statistical and semantic signals based on uncertainty-driven instance-level confidence. Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets (Healthcare, Finance) demonstrate that ReForest significantly outperforms baselines, achieving an average 21% improvement in Recall and a 10% gain in F1-score. Our framework not only improves detection performance but also enhances model transparency by grounding semantic narratives in statistical evidence, providing interpretable rationales essential for high-stakes decision-making.

RPSD: Retrieval-Privileged On-Policy Self-Distillation with Optimal Transport

Dongyue Li, Zebang Cheng, Zhongyi Deng et al. · Oct 27

Introduced Retrieval-Privileged On-Policy Self-Distillation (RPSD) using optimal transport to convert sequence-level rewards into token-specific credits for sequential recommendation models.

For you: Targeted specifically at recommendation systems rather than general agent or RAG workflows.

Abstract

Sequential recommendation predicts a user's next interaction from historical behavior. Recent LLM-based methods recast this task as generative recommendation and increasingly adopt reinforcement learning such as GRPO for post-training. However, these methods suffer from two limitations, namely credit-blind optimization, where a sequence-level reward is shared across all generated tokens, and collaborative-blind supervision, where each training signal is derived from a single user and ignores behavioral patterns shared across similar users. To address both issues, we propose Retrieval-Privileged on-policy Self-Distillation (RPSD). For credit-blind optimization, we introduce on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), in which a teacher with privileged knowledge re-evaluates the student's on-policy response, and the token-level confidence difference between the student and teacher reshapes the shared sequence-level advantage into token-specific credit. However, the privileged knowledge in vanilla OPSD is still derived from a single user, leaving collaborative-blind supervision unresolved. We therefore ground the teacher in collaborative behavioral evidence from similar users. Specifically, we use temporally aware optimal transport (OT) to compare interaction trajectories across users by jointly considering item similarity and temporal structure, and further apply diversity-aware selection to retain complementary trajectories as privileged evidence. In this way, on-policy self-distillation provides fine-grained token-level credit, while OT enriches the teacher with structured cross-user evidence. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to bring on-policy self-distillation to sequential recommendation and to use cross-user behavior as its privileged signal. Extensive experiments across multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that RPSD achieves state-of-the-art performance.

ASNER: Software Architecture Recovery with Adaptive Semantic Neighborhoods and Evidence-Grounded Refinement

Wenhao Wang, Zhi Jin, Jia Li et al. · Oct 27

Proposed ASNER for software architecture recovery, combining responsibility-aware LLM assignment with adaptive semantic neighborhoods and dependency graphs.

For you: Too specific to codebase architecture recovery and software engineering repo analysis to apply directly to standard enterprise document search.

Abstract

Automated software architecture recovery provides architectural context for software engineering and repository-level LLM agents. Existing methods combine structural and semantic signals, but functional relatedness does not necessarily imply shared architectural responsibility. Fixed thresholds or Top-K neighborhoods also ignore repository scale and local similarity distributions, introducing errors that propagate through clustering. Unconstrained LLM refinement may further alter correct assignments without sufficient evidence. We propose ASNER (Adaptive Semantic Neighborhoods and Evidence-Grounded Refinement for Software Architecture Recovery), based on responsibility-aware, project-adaptive relation modeling and evidence-grounded boundary verification. ASNER derives structured file responsibilities with an LLM, constructs semantic neighborhoods that adapt to repository scale and local similarity distributions, and augments a static dependency graph with selected semantic relations and weak directory cues. Community detection recovers the component backbone. A Responsibility-Ownership-Aware (ROA) Agent then examines uncertain boundary files using project-level responsibilities and traceable code evidence, changing assignments only when supported. Experiments on 12 open-source projects spanning C, C++, Java, and Python show that ASNER achieves the best macro-average on all five metrics on the common benchmark, improving ARI and c2c@66 over GAER-GAT by 19.5% and 32.9%, respectively. A downstream evaluation shows that ASNER-guided LocAgent improves file-, module-, and function-level code localization, demonstrating the value of its architectural context for repository-level software engineering tasks.

Test-Time Communication in Trading Agent Swarms

Avid Fayaz, Harry Zhang, Håvard Alstadheim et al. · Oct 27

Studied test-time communication in trading agent swarms and found communication leads to stock convergence and phrase copying.

For you: Too domain-specific to financial trading swarms to yield immediately applicable patterns for general enterprise LLM apps.

Abstract

Communication between agents has mostly been studied on tasks with a shared objective and fast, clear feedback. Trading has neither: each agent manages its own account, with decisions being scored by markets late and noisily. A trading swarm is thus a population of independent decision-makers rather than a team, for which convergence is a risk and not the goal. We ran 200 agents from four model families in groups of ten under five communication regimes, from isolation to message boards and shared notes, over seven US trading days (1 to 9 October 2026), logging everything each agent read before every decision. In two of the four families, communicating groups came to hold far more of the same stocks than the isolated group. A group's return was largely the return on what its members held in common. Seeing a post from a groupmate made an agent 7 percentage points more likely to reuse an eight-word phrase from it. Agents that started too close together to see each other's posts shared such a phrase in 14% of cases, the rate that common inputs produce. Seeing the post did not detectably change that morning's trades. Case studies traced the adoption of positions and decision rules over subsequent days, and communicating groups sold in step more often than isolated ones. Because isolated groups also converged on the same stocks, sometimes completely, assessments of herding need to measure excess convergence relative to a no-peer baseline.

Can Discrete Tokens Serve as Semantically Lossless Representations of Visual Signals?

Jialv Zou, Xiaotong Li, Zhengrong Yue et al. · LongCat · Oct 26 · ▲ 88

Introduces Factorized Vector Quantization using parallel codebooks to improve semantic fidelity in discrete visual tokenizers.

For you: Too low-level on visual tokenization architectures; limited direct application for standard document processing pipelines.

Abstract

Discrete visual tokens represent vision in a discrete form analogous to language, offering a native interface for multimodal modeling under a shared next-token prediction objective. However, quantization error is widely assumed to cause unavoidable semantic loss, fundamentally limiting the semantic fidelity of discrete visual tokens and positioning continuous visual representations as an intrinsic upper bound. Using multimodal understanding as a proxy for semantic fidelity, we revisit this assumption and find quantization error unreliable, as discrete representations with substantially larger errors can achieve better understanding. Instead, the effective bit budget of discrete indices emerges as a key determinant of downstream performance. However, existing quantizers eventually saturate as additional codebooks contribute progressively less semantic information. As one approach to alleviating this saturation, we introduce Factorized Vector Quantization, which quantizes complementary, fixed-dimensional views of the visual feature with symmetric parallel codebooks. It continues to improve at larger bit budgets, ultimately matching continuous visual representations in multimodal understanding. Trained on industrial-scale multimodal data, our model achieves state-of-the-art performance among discrete-representation models, outperforming UniAR and Emu3-Chat by 22.8% and 59.2%, respectively, on a nine-metric average. To our knowledge, it is also the first discrete-representation model competitive with strong continuous-representation models such as Qwen3-VL, including on fine-grained OCR tasks. We further identify the key principles and a practical recipe enabling this competitiveness. These results and insights support the potential of discrete visual representations to underpin large-scale native multimodal foundation models. Code and models are available at https://github.com/hustvl/FVQ

A Serious-Game Testbed for Self-Governing Human-Machine Institutions

Callum Berger, Jeremy Pitt, Stefan Sarkadi · Oct 27

Introduced Megabike, a multi-agent simulator with 40 sessions testing language-model agents in resource management and political regime selection.

For you: Too academic and game-theoretic for standard enterprise agent orchestration work.

Abstract

AI agents no longer act alone: they cooperate, coordinate and compete within and between groups, increasingly alongside humans who are in, on or out of ``the loop''. Whether such mixed groups can govern themselves is an open question about machine behaviour and human-computer interaction that needs a testbed for hybrid institutions. We present Megabike, a multi-agent simulator and serious game with interdependent arenas for common-pool resource management, cooperative survival and constitutional choice. Riders sharing a megabike must agree their arrangements for steering, pedalling and distributing resources, and their political regime, while negotiating with rival bikes and avoiding an existential threat. Every rule is enforced by the simulator, every action is logged, and any seat can be taken by a human or a machine, so governance can be studied under well-defined metrics. We demonstrate Megabike with forty sessions in which language-model riders with cooperative, lazy, subversive or opposing dispositions chose regimes, elected leaders and expelled one another. The results show that different combinations of agent archetypes could produce unexpected and counterproductive outcomes. We conclude with extensions for systematic experiments mixing human and computational agents.

KL-MOPD: Discrepancy-Aware Multi-Objective On-Policy Distillation for Generative Recommendation

Weiqin Yang, Yandong Wang, Yuan Gao et al. · Oct 27

Proposed KL-MOPD, a token-level distillation method for multi-objective generative recommendation models to balance competing objectives.

For you: Too narrow to generative recommendation systems rather than general LLM application infrastructure.

Abstract

Coordinating multiple recommendation objectives (e.g., click and conversion) is critical for real-world generative recommendation (GR). However, since a positive item in GR can be retrieved only when its tokens are successively selected at every decoding step, traditional item-wise methods lack fine-grained token-wise supervision to guide intermediate steps. Motivated by its success in foundation models, we consider introducing multi-objective on-policy distillation (MOPD) into GR to distill two single-objective teachers into a student at each token. Yet, we find that in vanilla MOPD, (i) teachers exhibit unequal distillation difficulty at each token (i.e., token-level imbalance), and (ii) the harder-to-distill teacher varies across tokens (i.e., cross-token inconsistency), leaving each objective poorly learned at certain steps and hindering positive item retrieval for both objectives, leading to suboptimal performance. To address this challenge, we propose KL-MOPD (KL-Weighted Multi-Objective On-Policy Distillation), a simple yet effective distillation method that allocates token-wise teacher weights proportional to the initial student-teacher token-wise KL divergences, thereby strengthening supervision for the harder-to-distill teacher at every decoding step. Despite its simplicity, we theoretically prove that KL-MOPD strictly outperforms vanilla MOPD in the additive joint recommendation objective under mild conditions. Moreover, KL-MOPD is plug-and-play and computationally efficient, requiring only a one-time pre-training weight calibration with negligible training overhead. Extensive experiments on three public benchmarks, a billion-scale industrial dataset, and online A/B tests on our e-commerce advertising platform demonstrate that KL-MOPD significantly improves both individual and joint recommendation performance. The anonymous code is provided for peer review.

Compatibility and future-proof testing for multi-agent systems

Yueshan Li, Lorenzo Fiaschi, Andrea Censi et al. · Oct 26

Formulated a bi-level framework for future-proof testing and compatibility assurance in independently developed autonomous agents.

For you: Focused on distributed autonomous vehicles and independent safety verification rather than software agent orchestration.

Abstract

Independently developed autonomous agents, such as autonomous vehicles, must guarantee joint compatibility, such as safety and task-completion requirements, when they operate in a shared environment. Existing methods require a centrally designed specification, or interaction mechanism before any agent is deployed. However, such centralized design is unrealistic when agent designs are confidential to their developers, and it is not flexible enough to accommodate future agent designs. We study a bi-level structure in which a regulator coordinates developers by publishing seed agents, and each developer independently tests its agent to ensure that it is compatible with every seed-compatible agent, a property we call future-proofness. Under this structure, joint compatibility is guaranteed for any agents that are designed and tested independently, without any coordination with each other. In this work, we first formulate compatibility for general multi-agent systems and show why it is not trivially implied by seed-compatibility. We then characterize the future-proof agents as the closure of the seed and show that this closure is monotone in the seed. Finally, we study a class of executable tests that involve interaction with the seed alone, and we ask to what extent such tests can distinguish the future-proof agents.

CAMR: Compatibility-Aware Memory Replay for Continual Learning in Large Language Models

Jingpu Yang, Tianhao Li, Fengxian Ji et al. · Oct 26

Proposed Compatibility-Aware Memory Replay (CAMR) to jointly control current-example supervision and historical replay for continual LLM learning.

For you: Continual pre-training/fine-tuning is usually handled upstream rather than in applied enterprise app engineering.

Abstract

Continual learning requires large language models to acquire new knowledge and capabilities while retaining what they have learned. Fine-tuning and replay support acquisition and retention, but deciding whether incoming examples need supervision and which historical examples to revisit remains a challenge. We propose Compatibility-Aware Memory Replay (CAMR), an adaptive framework that jointly controls current-example supervision and historical replay. CAMR combines compatibility checks with a learned memory gate to guide an online policy over three actions: learning incoming examples with general replay, jointly reinforcing compatible related examples, and skipping direct supervision of verified mastered duplicates. The policy learns from acquisition, retention, and training-cost feedback under a bounded per-arrival replay budget. We evaluate CAMR with Qwen3-8B-Base on continual factual updating and cross-task and cross-domain learning. On WikiBigEdit, CAMR consistently leads evaluated methods in Original Match from 3K to 100K facts, reaching 93.66% after just 3K facts and surpassing Full-history Replay (LoRA) after 100K facts. It also achieves the highest final scores on all four selected TRACE tasks and maintains or improves earlier-task performance relative to post-acquisition levels in a sequence spanning financial question answering, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and summarization.

Latent Visual Contrastive Reasoning with Multi-Branch Exploration

Kegeng Tang, Jingbo Wang, Yangming Zhang et al. · Oct 27

Latent Visual Contrastive Reasoning uses multi-branch exploration in the visual latent space for multimodal LLMs, aligning branches with bounding-box evidence.

For you: Primarily focused on multimodal perception and vision-language reasoning rather than standard enterprise text-based RAG and agent systems.

Abstract

Multimodal large language models have made substantial progress in visual question answering, but complex visual reasoning often requires locating and comparing multiple pieces of question-relevant visual evidence. Existing methods that move intermediate reasoning into the visual latent space usually follow a single linear reasoning trajectory, which can be limiting when multiple visual hypotheses or competing evidence regions are relevant. We propose Latent Visual Contrastive Reasoning, a branch-aware latent reasoning framework that generates multiple question-conditioned latent visual branches as soft hypotheses over image tokens. To train these branches, our method uses bounding-box evidence annotations to build target evidence representations and introduces a soft contrastive branch-evidence alignment objective. This objective encourages evidence-relevant branches to align with annotated visual regions while contrasting them against negative evidence. We instantiate our method on Qwen2.5-VL backbones and train it with standard supervised fine-tuning. Experiments on perception-intensive visual reasoning benchmarks show that the proposed method achieves overall improvements over the corresponding backbone and single-path latent reasoning baselines at both 3B and 7B scales.

SEMA-Auto: Sighted and Efficient Multi-Turn Attack with Autonomy

Mingqian Feng, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang et al. · Oct 26

SEMA-Auto is an autonomous, multi-turn jailbreak attacker trained via synthetic bootstrap tuning and decision-supervised reinforcement learning to interact with victim models until success.

For you: Relevant to LLM security red-teaming, but offers little direct value for building production RAG or general agent workflows.

Abstract

Existing jailbreak attackers fall into two classes: static planners fix their single- or multi-turn adversarial plans in advance, remaining simple but losing sight of the victim's replies, wasting turns and limiting transferability; interactive generators read each victim reply to generate the next-turn attack but require in-loop evaluators for success signals or hints, incurring substantial costs and inflated performance. Bridging this gap, we propose SEMA-Auto, a sighted and efficient multi-turn attacker with autonomy, interacting with the victim model and terminating the session by itself upon success. To stabilize its behaviors and boost its capabilities, SEMA-Auto is trained in two stages. Synthetic bootstrap tuning installs the notion of attack targets and conforming decisions by fine-tuning the base model with bootstrapped trajectories. Decision-supervised reinforcement learning then optimizes two objectives jointly yet separately, reinforcing the attack with higher jailbreak reward while supervising the decision at each turn against an absolute target. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) attack success rates (ASR) across multiple datasets, victims, and judges, outperforming all static and interactive baselines, within almost the fewest turns, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency. For example, SEMA-Auto performs a $76.0\%$ ASR@1 within an average of $3.0$ turns against DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AdvBench, while the best static baseline is $54.8\%$ with fixed $7$ turns and the best interactive baseline is $43.8\%$ in $3.7$ turns. Against safety-hardened frontier models, GPT-oss-120B, GPT-5.4, Claude-Haiku-4.5, and GPT-6-Astra, our counter-trained attacker achieves an average $59.6\%$ ASR@1 on HarmBench, $13.8\%$ over prior SOTA. Our code is available at: https://github.com/microsoft/SEMA

BehaviorMem: Building and Evaluating User Memory from Real-World E-Commerce Behavior Streams

Sizhe Wang, Xinghao Fang, Rui Qiu et al. · Oct 25

For you: Too domain-specific to e-commerce logs, but the evaluation framework approach could inspire general user memory benchmarks.

Abstract

User memory helps personalized systems build on their understanding of users over time. In e-commerce, users convey their preferences and needs through both explicit language and implicit interactions with products. We study memory built from logs of these interactions, examining how it can \emph{remember} concrete evidence and established interests, and use that evidence to \emph{infer} underlying needs and preferences that inform judgments beyond well-observed product domains. Existing memory methods primarily target language-based interactions, and evaluations offer limited real behavioral data and limited separation of these capabilities. This leaves open how to jointly support and separately evaluate remembering and inference from behavioral logs. We formulate the problem of continual behavioral user memory around these joint requirements and develop a dedicated evaluation framework and memory method to address them. \textbf{BehaviorMemBench} is a benchmark built from real e-commerce logs covering 1{,}194 users and 17{,}462 behavioral batches over more than three years, with automatic future-behavior labels and a task-independent memory interface to separately evaluate remembering and inference under a common protocol. \textbf{BehaviorMem} is a memory design that organizes explicit behavioral facts and inferred user characteristics across long-term and short-term scopes in a two-axis state, with evidence-grounded formation, continual maintenance, and query-independent readout. Experiments across two builders and two task readers show that BehaviorMem leads the evaluated agentic memories on remember and infer, and reveal different method rankings across the two capabilities and competitive statistical baselines. Validation on a public dataset further supports its usefulness for downstream tasks based on behavioral histories.

Agent or Human? Detecting Commits by AI Coding Agents in the Absence of Authorship Signatures

Kosei Horikawa, Hao Li, Yutaro Kashiwa et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated signature-free detection of AI coding agent commits across 7.8M commits, showing text verbosity and change size achieve up to 0.859 ROC AUC.

For you: Interesting analytics on code provenance, but of limited direct engineering value for production LLM app architectures.

Abstract

AI coding agents such as Claude Code, GitHub Copilot coding agent, and Cursor now commit directly to open-source repositories. Empirical studies of these agents identify their commits through authorship signatures, such as bot accounts and Co-authored-by trailers. These signatures are optional, can be removed, and are often attached to pull requests rather than to individual commits. Hence, it is unclear whether Agent commits can be told apart from Human commits once the signatures are gone. We investigate the signals and limits of signature-free detection using 7,837,946 commits from 1,226 repositories, covering five coding agents and Human commits. After signatures are masked, detectors rank Agent commits above Human commits with a ROC AUC of up to 0.859, and most of this signal comes from message verbosity and change size rather than from the code itself. On the shared sample, gpt-6-sol without fine-tuning reaches AUCs similar to XGBoost and TF-IDF. At the observed Agent share, however, the best detector on the full test set reaches a precision of only 0.332. Detection transfers poorly to held-out Claude Code and Copilot, with TF-IDF reaching an AUC of only 0.524 for Claude Code. Temporal changes also depend on the agent, e.g., Copilot AUC drops and later recovers, while more training history helps Claude Code but not Copilot. These results indicate that signature-free detection can prioritize commits for review but cannot replace recorded attribution, and that studies relying on signature-based labels should treat them as a lower bound on agent activity.

6G Reasoning Agent Societies: Composition, Scaling, and Consistency-Time Laws

Hatim Chergui, Carolina Fernández-Martínez, Mustapha Benjillali et al. · Oct 25

Analyzes how multi-agent consensus and error propagation behave over network topologies using cellular sheaf theory, identifying hidden divergence and trust inflation.

For you: Too theoretical for near-term application, but provides conceptual background on multi-agent communication failure modes.

Abstract

Emerging 6G control planes would compose Large Language Model (LLM) agents that exchange interpreted conclusions, so hallucinations can propagate even over error-free transport. Recent architectures audit each message locally against the sender's observation mask, but isolated audits miss collective behavior. Modeling the agent society as a cellular sheaf, we establish three laws valid on arbitrary topologies. First, the \emph{composition law}: network-wide divergence exceeds the sum of per-agent audit scores by exactly their hidden total correlation. This blind spot masks up to 23\% of joint divergence and, because the declared signal cancels in the cognitive signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), overstates it by $1.14$\,dB purely through topology; the resulting trust inflation preserves valid consensus states but slows convergence. Second, the \emph{scaling law}: reconciliation decouples across metric counters, so the worst-connected dimension sets the pace. Consistency time grows for a society size $n$ as $Ω(n^{2/d})$ on polynomial-growth topologies of degree $d$, and paths breach a $1$\,s near-real-time budget at $n^\ast=13$ agents, whereas expanders ($Θ(1)$) and radius-3 local audits ($O(1)$, $\approx300$\,ms) stay bounded. Third, the \emph{consistency-time law}: the timing error from omitted correlations is one-sided and capped by the worst-measured link. These laws, validated on simulated topologies and deployed LLM societies, give provable worst-case latency ceilings for near-real-time operation.

Uncovering Shortcut Amplification in Video RLVR through the Lens of Perceptual Uncertainty Collapse

Jiafeng Liang, Xianhao Ou, Tao Ren et al. · Oct 27

Investigates shortcut amplification in video reasoning RLVR, showing that outcome-level optimization causes perceptual uncertainty collapse driven by text-prior compression.

For you: Too specialized (video multimodal RL) to transfer directly to standard text RAG and agent pipelines.

Abstract

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as an effective post-training paradigm for enhancing the video reasoning capability of Large Multimodal Models (LMMs). Despite its empirical effectiveness, we show that outcome-level optimization can introduce an undesirable shortcut amplification effect, whereby models increasingly favor text-prior answers even when they are not supported by the visual evidence. To characterize this behavior, we investigate the training dynamics of video RLVR through the lens of perceptual uncertainty, analyzing how predictive uncertainty evolves under video-conditioned and text-only contexts. Our analysis identifies a perceptual uncertainty collapse phenomenon, in which RL training continuously sharpens the model's answer distribution while the uncertainty reduction attributable to visual evidence progressively diminishes. A further gradient-direction analysis reveals that such confidence enhancement becomes increasingly less aligned with visually grounded uncertainty reduction and is instead dominated by text-prior compression. Motivated by these findings, we propose Bifocal Self-distillation Policy Optimization (BSPO), a self-distillation framework that regulates policy updates with two complementary distributional signals. Specifically, BSPO derives empirical reasoning principles from intra-group correct and incorrect rollouts to identify reasoning-sensitive tokens, and introduces shortcut-aware visual perturbation to penalize predictions that remain invariant when task-relevant visual evidence is weakened. By adaptively reweighting the original RL advantage, BSPO preserves the task-solving benefits of RLVR while promoting updates that are both reasoning-sensitive and visually grounded. Extensive experiments validate the effectiveness of BSPO in improving video reasoning performance and strengthening visual faithfulness.

Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation

Michał Wawer, Jarosław A. Chudziak · Oct 26

Proposes Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA) to classify inter-agent disagreement in content moderation by inspecting reasoning similarity and conclusion agreement, outperforming output-only baselines.

For you: Interesting approach to agent reliability, but content moderation is a narrow domain with limited direct transfer to general enterprise search or RAG.

Abstract

Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. This framing is inappropriate for value-laden tasks, where disagreement may reflect irreducible value pluralism - a distinction standard uncertainty decompositions cannot recover, since they operate on output distributions and discard the reasoning behind each judgment. We propose Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA), which embeds model-generated justifications from perspective-differentiated agents and classifies each case by justification similarity and conclusion agreement. The resulting four-state taxonomy isolates convergent disagreement, where agents reasoning similarly but concluding differently, as a candidate signature of value-laden conflict. On 1,000 items from the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, this structural signal predicts human annotator disagreement better than magnitude-only and output-only baselines. We contribute a design principle: condition human escalation on the structure of disagreement, not its magnitude alone, with RTDA serving as a post-processing layer for systems that already generate reasoning traces.

PerceptRL: Making Visual Perception Rewardable in MLLMs from Answers to Interactions

Tongkun Guan, Zhibo Yang, Mingkun Yang et al. · Oct 27

They introduced PerceptRL to improve multimodal LLM visual perception by extracting verified errors from image captions into QA pairs with rule-based rewards.

For you: Too focused on multimodal visual perception and MLLM post-training rather than standard text-based RAG or agent orchestration.

Abstract

Fine-grained visual perception remains a bottleneck during post-training of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), as perceptual errors can constrain gains from reasoning-focused optimization. Although image-caption pre-training improves perception, its influence can weaken as subsequent stages change data mixtures and objectives. Continuing caption supervision during post-training is challenging because subjective, information-dense descriptions complicate verifiable reward design. We present PerceptRL, which strengthens perception at two complementary levels. Perception RL uses a multi-agent pipeline to extract verified errors from dense image captions and convert the corresponding visual facts into QA pairs with fixed answer labels and rule-based rewards. Chain QA links correlated errors, while multiple-choice QA combines unrelated errors. A specialized route targets high-error domains with tailored verification, and a domain-agnostic general route covers errors beyond these domains. Perception Agentic RL addresses fine-grained and dense cases unresolved by single-turn Perception RL through multi-turn inspection with visual tools. We observe coordinate drift even in models trained to use tools, where incorrect crops or annotations distort evidence and propagate errors. Coordinate Injection corrects spatial operations in cold-start trajectories, while a grounding-aware process reward supervises intermediate spatial fidelity alongside final-answer correctness. Training proceeds through shared cold-start SFT, single-turn Perception RL, and multi-turn Perception Agentic RL. Across seven benchmarks under a shared tool-use protocol, PerceptRL achieves 71.6% average accuracy, surpassing the base model with the same tools and the strongest evaluated frontier VLM by 15.7 and 5.2 percentage points, respectively.

Vision-Language-Action or World-Action? Just Action Models

Yonghao Yu, Lang Huang, Runyi Li et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 6

Just Action Model decouples backbone, action loss, and foresight loss, training with a discarded future‑prediction head for efficient action policies.

For you: Vision‑action focus; limited transfer to LLM agent orchestration.

Abstract

The vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action model (WAM) communities debate whether control policies should inherit pretrained knowledge from vision-language models or from visual generators. The debate, however, bundles three separable choices: backbone initialization, the objective applied to future observations, and whether the machinery for that future loss is kept at inference. A gap between a VLA and a WAM therefore cannot simply be attributed to any one of them. We show that decoupling these choices yields a common framework for both families, which we term Just Action Model (JAM): a pretrained visual backbone with an action expert, trained with an action loss and a foresight loss. The foresight loss trains current-observation features to predict frozen encodings of the recorded future through a prediction head discarded after training, so no visual generation is involved in either training or inference, and the backbone can be chosen freely. We first compare objectives within fixed policy architectures, then evaluate the foresight recipe across backbone families. The resulting JAM policies outperform their matched FastWAM and ImageWAM counterparts on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, and achieve similar or slightly higher success rates on RoboTwin. Compared with FastWAM, JAM improves the success rates from 97.0%/55.3%/91.2% to 98.2%/64.5%/91.4%, respectively. With cached foresight targets, JAM reduces estimated online training FLOPs per sample per update by 73% relative to FastWAM. Our results show that future supervision improves control without visual generation or additional inference machinery, and strong foresight performance is attainable with either generator-initialized or vision-language backbones. Project Page: https://y0uroy.github.io/JAM

When the Persona Contradicts the Line: Diagnosing Plan-to-Synthesis Failures in Chain-of-Thought TTS

Joonyong Park, Jerry Li · Oct 26

Analyzes failures in chain-of-thought TTS systems where requested personas conflict with lexical sentiment, achieving 89% English agreement using a learned text-to-latent interface.

For you: Audio synthesis domain, not applicable to text LLM orchestration or document processing.

Abstract

Chain-of-thought TTS systems rely on language-model planners to translate persona and context into vocal delivery, but it is unclear where this intent is lost when the requested affect conflicts with lexical sentiment. We introduce a bilingual Japanese--English persona--line conflict benchmark in which each item pairs one line with congruent and conflicting personas and pre-declares the expected acoustic direction. We probe this direction in the planner-generated delivery phrase, the planner-written emotion vector, the QwenEmotion-generated vector, and the rendered waveform. Planner outputs are usually direction-correct, while downstream agreement can be substantially lower. Bypassing QwenEmotion provides only modest gains over the full planner-controlled Base. Supervising QwenEmotion raises the direction rate to 75% in Japanese and 84% in English. A learned text-to-latent interface reaches the highest English rate of 89% and the highest Japanese arousal rate of 88%. These results show that learned repair of the plan-to-synthesis interface can recover counter-textual delivery, although its effect differs across languages and acoustic axes.

Diagnosing and Mitigating Long-Session Degradation in Full-Duplex Speech Models

Junho Kim, CheHyun Lee, Donghun Kang et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated long-session degradation in full-duplex speech models and proposed a self-distillation LoRA training framework and two-resolution context architecture to stabilize performance.

For you: Focuses on real-time spoken dialogue models rather than text-based RAG, enterprise search, or standard LLM apps.

Abstract

End-to-end full-duplex spoken dialogue models enable natural, real-time spoken interactions with interruptions, backchannels, and overlapping speech. Prior evaluations have assessed diverse interaction capabilities and identified some degradation patterns across conversational turns. However, how model behavior evolves over extended sessions remains insufficiently understood. In this work, we address this gap by systematically characterizing long-session degradation and developing efficient methods to mitigate it. We introduce a closed-loop evaluation framework for long-session stability and apply it to several publicly available full-duplex speech models, revealing degradation over time in responsiveness, spoken content quality, and voice consistency. To mitigate this degradation, we propose a self-distillation-based training framework for lightweight LoRA adaptation that improves long-session stability with a small amount of additional training. We further introduce a two-resolution context (TRC) architecture that preserves recent interactions in full detail while compressing older interaction history into key-value representations. We evaluate our methods on three models with distinct architectures, assessing long-session stability, information retention, and computational efficiency. Across all three models, we show that LoRA adaptation mitigates performance degradation over ten-minute sessions, while adding TRC extends these gains beyond the training session length and reduces KV-cache growth by more than 90%.

OpenGray: a benchmark for evaluating the decisions of radiotherapy planning agents

Humza Nusrat, Kundan Thind · Oct 27

Developed OpenGray, a benchmark evaluating the decision-making accuracy of radiotherapy planning agents against known clinical constraints.

For you: Too narrow and specialized to medical applications for general enterprise LLM engineering.

Abstract

Background and purpose: Radiotherapy planning agents based on large language models (LLMs) decide when to submit a plan or request review but are evaluated mainly by dose. This study developed OpenGray, a benchmark of planning tasks with known correct decisions. Materials and methods: OpenGray adds tasks with known answers to the OpenKBP-Opt head-and-neck dataset, including two contradictions with dose goals proven incompatible and a feasible control with a stored plan meeting every hard goal. Four systems, from a rule-based planning script to a full LLM agent, were evaluated on 30 patients, the LLM-based ones with a commercial (Terra) and a local (Ministral 3 8B) LLM. Decisions, achieved dose, problem recognition, and technical failures were recorded and also read from logs of 39 stereotactic radiosurgery runs of SAGE, a clinical LLM agent. Results: With Terra, the script preceded by an LLM review and the full agent had decision accuracies of 95.4% and 92.1%, respectively, yet escalated only 50% (95% confidence interval, 25-75%) and 25% (10-40%) of overlap contradictions, which required arithmetic on the anatomy. All four systems submitted every feasible control, but 24% of these plans missed a hard goal that the stored plan had met. In four SAGE runs, one goal failed at every attempt; the agent identified it in every reply but had no action for stopping. Conclusions: Favorable aggregate scores concealed specific decision failures. Planning agents should be evaluated on their decisions as well as their dose and given an explicit action for stopping and requesting review.

Beyond QASecClaw: Agent Scaling, Verifier Feedback, and Evidence Reliability in Software Assurance

S M Abdulla Al Mamun, Akif Islam, Mohd. Ruhul Ameen · Oct 27

Evaluated Qwen2.5-Coder on software assurance tasks with up to 50 contexts, finding token scaling fails to improve clearance and raw verifier feedback raises incorrect verdicts.

For you: Too narrow to application security code analysis; insights on verifier feedback noise are worth noting for general agent loops.

Abstract

Can additional specialist language-model contexts improve software assurance when deterministic tool evidence is already available? We study this question through five experiments spanning authored Java warning controls, OWASP Benchmark assessment, Defects4J test generation, SV-COMP assertion verdicts, and deliberately misleading evidence packages. The evaluated systems use Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, with a 1.5B sensitivity analysis, and complete 13,638 model requests. On eight related development warnings, increasing the context count from 1 to 50 increases token use 49-fold without improving unsafe clearance. On 160 OWASP cases, model configurations trade clearance against unresolved outcomes; the ten-context arm assesses only 96 cases. On 32 SV-COMP tasks, raw verifier feedback raises mean correct model verdicts from 12.7 to 22.0, but also raises incorrect verdicts from one to seven; a JBMC-plus-JVM validation baseline returns 26 correct verdicts and six unresolved outcomes. On two sets of 120 evidence packages, models reject 70-100% of applicable counterexamples, while construction-specific scope checks classify every package correctly. No target defect is discovered in three Defects4J bugs, one unsupported by the test adapter. These descriptive results support deterministic evidence admission for the tested settings, while exposing limits of verdict aggregation, feedback interpretation, and the available evaluation coverage.

Who Knows What - and What Does It Support? Perspective-Bounded Evidence Calibration for Multimodal Theory of Mind

Ziqi Liao, Jiaqi Shen, Yiming Na et al. · Oct 27

Analyzed perspective leakage in multimodal Theory of Mind and proposed an evidence-admissibility interface to filter inaccessible event traces.

For you: Domain is social intelligence and ToM benchmarks rather than production LLM orchestration or standard grounding tasks.

Abstract

Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to infer others' beliefs, goals, and intentions, is a cornerstone of social intelligence. Multimodal ToM requires inferring an agent's mental state from evidence that is available to that agent, rather than from the complete episode observed by the solver. Existing LLM-based and Bayesian model-based approaches reconstruct global event traces, yet they do not explicitly distinguish objective events from agent-admissible evidence during hypothesis evaluation. Consequently, agent-inaccessible events or semantically misaligned actions and utterances may influence the inferred belief, goal, or social intention, leading to perspective leakage and source or route confusion. We formulate this limitation as an evidence-admissibility problem: evidence should contribute to a mental-state hypothesis only when it is accessible to the relevant agent, preserves its information source, and is diagnostic of the queried mental-state variable. To operationalize this principle, we propose PIP-ToM, an evidence-admissibility interface that constructs agent-specific evidence stores, aligns them with structured mental-state claims, and calibrates model-based hypothesis scores while preserving the underlying Bayesian inference process. PIP-ToM with GPT-4o outperforms existing ToM methods across MuMA-ToM, MMToM-QA, and Hi-ToM, achieving average accuracies of 92.56%, 95.00%, and 95.00%, respectively.

Jaxnasium: Embracing PyTrees For Any-Agent, Cross-Suite Reinforcement Learning

Koen Ponse, Aske Plaat, Thomas M. Moerland · Oct 26

Presented Jaxnasium, a JAX-based RL library using PyTrees to unify observation and action spaces across 300+ environments.

For you: Pure reinforcement learning infrastructure with no direct application to production LLM apps or RAG.

Abstract

Hardware-accelerated reinforcement learning in JAX has gained rapid popularity in recent years and has proven to be a successful method of scaling online reinforcement learning. The resulting ecosystem, however, is fragmented: environment suites define their own interfaces, and algorithm implementations are typically written for a single suite, a single type of space, and either JAX-native or host-based environments. Here we present Jaxnasium, a library for JAX-based reinforcement learning, aiming to make the field more accessible. Jaxnasium represents observation and action spaces as PyTrees, and uses this structure to build networks and support multi-agent processing. Through this, its near-single-file implementations run on discrete, continuous, image, and composite spaces, as well as in multi-agent environments, without any changes to the algorithm code. The same files serve as readable, research-friendly algorithms, as well as importable algorithms for more applied workloads. Through a minimal Gymnasium-style interface and wrappers for 11 suites, over 300 environments are available on which a small PPO implementation with default hyperparameters is able to run. Additionally, through a simple wrapper, we can run Gymnasium and EnvPool environments with the same algorithm and training loop, with no performance loss compared to architectures specifically built for training JAX algorithms on host-based environments. Jaxnasium further provides tools for multi-seed evaluation, hyperparameter sweeps, and project scaffolding. Jaxnasium is open-sourced at https://github.com/ponseko/jaxnasium
