Modeling Preference Dependencies with Directed Acyclic Graphs for Narrative-Driven Recommendation

Mingzhe Lu, Jian Qin, Ruize Qin et al. · Oct 27

Introduces the Preference Dependency Graph Framework (PDG-F) to parse narrative user requests into a directed acyclic graph for path-based recommendation.

For you: Focuses strictly on narrative-driven recommendation domains rather than general multi-agent orchestration or RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Narrative-driven recommendation allows users to express multifaceted needs through free-form text where stated preferences naturally qualify and condition one another. Existing methods typically treat narratives as flat keyword combinations or unconstrained generative queries, failing to capture how complex preferences hierarchically refine and contextualize each other. We propose the Preference Dependency Graph Framework (PDG-F), a narrative-driven recommendation framework centered on explicit preference dependencies. Specifically, PDG-A (PDG-Agent) first parses unstructured requests to organize user preferences and their mutual qualifications into a directed acyclic Preference Dependency Graph (PDG). Through this graph, candidate evidence from the open Web is acquired via directed path retrieval, and final recommendations are determined by topological path ranking. Comprehensive experiments across three diverse domains spanning English and Chinese contexts confirm that PDG-F consistently achieves superior recommendation performance over competitive baselines, open-domain retrieval agents, and frontier language models. Further diagnostic analyses show that our dependency-guided retrieval markedly raises the attainable candidate-pool ceiling, demonstrating the fundamental value of modeling preference dependencies in narrative-driven recommendation.

The Lexical Imprint of Pre-training Data

Jeonghoon Kim, Woojin Chung, Woomin Song et al. · HyperCLOVA X · Oct 25 · ▲ 10

Studies word-level unigram cross-entropy as a tokenizer-agnostic measure of lexical alignment between pre-training corpora and zero-shot benchmarks.

For you: Purely analytical study of pre-training data imprints; no actionable techniques for application developers or system builders.

Abstract

How much of zero-shot benchmark accuracy is captured by word-level alignment between a pre-training corpus and the benchmark target? We study this question through word-level unigram cross-entropy (UCE), a tokenizer-agnostic, model-free measure of lexical alignment between a reference corpus and a target benchmark. Likelihood training shapes the model's predictive distribution from the pre-training corpus, and zero-shot scores under likelihood-based evaluation depend in part on the lexical alignment between that corpus and the benchmark target. Cleanly tracing how the lexical imprint of pre-training data scales with corpus--benchmark alignment requires controlled from-scratch pre-training that varies corpus identity while holding the training recipe fixed. Across this controlled sweep, spanning 10 zero-shot benchmarks, 4 pre-training corpora, and 3 model scales, we find that corpus--benchmark lexical alignment, UCE, consistently tracks zero-shot accuracy on every benchmark, with corpora more aligned with the benchmark (lower UCE) achieving higher accuracy. The same signal extends from measurement to control at finer scopes. At the document level, strengthening the imprint by selecting training documents to maximize lexical alignment with the benchmark produces the accuracy gains predicted by the mechanism. At the prompt level, matching the imprint per prompt by routing each prompt to the drafter whose domain corpus is most lexically aligned with it produces measurable throughput gains over a generalist-drafter baseline. Taken together, pre-training corpora leave a measurable lexical imprint on base-model zero-shot behavior, and UCE provides a model-free probe of lexical alignment between reference and target data.

Replication or Corroboration? An Axiomatic Framework for Evidential Dependence in Multi-Agent Debate

Hoang-Loc Cao, Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen, Phuc Truong Loc Nguyen et al. · Oct 26

Formalized an axiomatic framework for distinguishing evidence replication from independent corroboration in multi-agent debates.

For you: Too theoretical and focused on debate verification rather than practical agent architectures.

Abstract

In multi-agent debates, matching reports can arise for two different reasons: agents may repeat the same evidence (replication), or they may provide separate evidential roots that support the same claim (corroboration). A judge must distinguish these two cases. Counting report occurrences gives extra weight to relays, paraphrases, alias resubmissions, and excerpt splits, while merging all matching content avoids this only by also removing genuine corroboration. We formalize this tension as a minimal judgment game in which acquiring new evidence is costly, but replication is cheap. We show that root replication invariance (RRI) is exactly threshold-uniform replication immunity. Under RRI, replication-only deviations cannot change the verdict, and positive-cost replication is strictly dominated, whereas occurrence-sensitive and content-keyed judges admit profitable deviations. We also establish an information boundary: if a judge's observation interface cannot distinguish a replica from a matched fresh root, no judge can both ignore the replica and respond strictly to the fresh root. To preserve this distinction, we introduce the lineage quotient, which removes wording, identity, occurrence multiplicity, and arbitrary root names while keeping distinct roots separate. On orbit-connected domains, it is the coarsest representation sufficient for every deterministic judge invariant to replication and root-label renaming. We further lift this construction to quantitative bipolar argumentation frameworks (QBAFs), characterize strict fresh-root response, prove its propagation along sign-consistent paths under DF-QuAD, and derive a tight closed-form response modulus. Exhaustive audits over 28,224 direct-star profiles, 2,052 multi-layer DAGs, with matched interventions on 277 AVeriTeC-derived profiles, show the predicted separation between replication and corroboration under a supplied partition.

ClinVerse: A Realistic Multi-Agent Diagnostic Environment for Training and Evaluating Medical Agents

Sirry Chen, Fangke Chen, Xuanwen Ding et al. · Oct 25

Created ClinVerse, a multi-agent environment grounded in 150k electronic health record diagnostic cases.

For you: Narrowly domain-specific to medical diagnosis, making transferability limited.

Abstract

Disease diagnosis is a key application of large language model (LLM) agents in healthcare. However, existing medical agents are primarily trained and evaluated with static question-answering data, providing limited opportunities to learn active information acquisition, examination decision-making, and dynamic clinical interaction. To address this limitation, we present ClinVerse, an interactive, real-data-grounded multi-agent environment designed for training and evaluating medical diagnostic agents. Built on ClinPatient, a scalable dataset comprising 150k diagnostic cases derived from real-world electronic health records, ClinVerse supports coordinated interactions among six specialized clinical roles. Specifically, a doctor agent starts from an initial patient complaint, progressively gathers clinical evidence through patient interviews and examination requests, and ultimately produces a diagnosis. For each interaction trajectory, a rule-based scoring framework quantifies information acquisition, examination ordering, and diagnostic accuracy. These trajectory-level scores serve as reward signals for agent training while also enabling scalable evaluation. In experiments, we evaluate various general-purpose and medical-domain models and show that nine self-evolution methods across three categories consistently improve agents' diagnostic capabilities within ClinVerse. We further analyze the interplay among model capability, environment feedback, and self-evolution methods.

SlowLab: Benchmarking Autonomous AI Agents on a Year of Experiments They Cannot Undo

Ningpei Ding, Zai Wen Feng · Oct 25

Evaluated three LLM agents on a simulated one-year greenhouse experiment campaign and found they underperformed a Bayesian-optimisation baseline.

For you: Too domain-specific (greenhouse control) with little direct transfer to enterprise software or standard production LLM agent patterns.

Abstract

AI agents are increasingly proposed as autonomous experimenters, yet the benchmarks that test them mostly offer cheap experiments that answer at once. Experiments in physical facilities are different: they take months, occupy scarce equipment and cannot be undone. We present SlowLab, a benchmark in which an agent runs a one-year campaign in four simulated greenhouse compartments on a site whose crop, facility and prices are hidden. The agent decides when to plant which climate policy, whether to stop a crop and which measurements to read while crops grow, and at the end recommends one policy, which a private evaluator scores in weather the agent never saw. In a preregistered study on 48 unseen sites, three low-cost language-model agents ended near a standard grower setting and clearly below a Bayesian-optimisation baseline that starts from that setting. They ran the facility as two synchronous batches, so measurements taken during the season never changed what they planted, and these measurements improved no method's result. Letting other methods re-read the evidence a campaign had collected did not yield significantly better recommendations, which points to where the models start and which experiments they try. Code is released under the MIT license.

Aligning Large Language Models for Safety in African Languages: A Case Study of Hausa

Valdini Douglace Lemofouet, Blessing Ngozi Uzor, Paula Chikaodinaka Anyanwu et al. · Oct 27

Studied safety alignment for Hausa using SFT and DPO on 10,000 preference pairs, showing base models suffered from low generation ability.

For you: Narrow focus on low-resource language safety alignment, not applicable to general enterprise RAG or agent development.

Abstract

Safety alignment research for large language models (LLMs) remains concentrated on high-resource languages, leaving open how existing alignment methods perform in low-resource, culturally distinct settings. We study this question for Hausa, a language spoken by more than 200 million people across West Africa. We introduce HausaSafeLM, one of the first Hausa safety preference dataset, built from 10,000 prompts derived from PKU-SafeRLHF: 3,000 human-corrected (gold) and 7,000 machine-translated (silver) examples spanning six harm macro-categories. Using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we compare supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and SFT followed by Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) under both data regimes, evaluating on 500 harmful and 200 benign Hausa prompts.We find that the untuned base model appears highly safe by refusal-rate metrics alone, but this reflects limited Hausa generation ability rather than genuine alignment: its refusals are almost uniformly low quality. SFT improves both refusal fluency and precision and benefits substantially from the larger, noisier silver-tier data, while DPO sharpens refusal precision but comes at a steep, largely data-invariant cost to linguistic quality. No single configuration jointly maximizes safety, helpfulness, and fluency. Our results suggest that current alignment techniques teach models when to refuse more readily than how to do so fluently in underrepresented languages, and that refusal-rate metrics alone are an unreliable signal of safety in low-resource settings.

Structured Maps for Landmark-Based Route Instructions with LLMs

Mifu Matsumoto, Yuki Nakanishi, Tomoya Hashiguchi et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated structured text representations using SVG format for landmark-based route instructions with LLMs.

For you: Too narrow and domain-specific for general enterprise search or agent workflows.

Abstract

To generate landmark-based route instructions, models must understand both map geometry and human landmark preferences. Although large language models (LLMs) show great abilities in language tasks, the best way to structure map data for these models is not yet established. This paper proposes a structured text representation for landmark selection tasks in route navigation, utilizing SVG format to encode spatial features. The structured text maintains a geometric connection with rendered map images, allowing language models to directly access spatial and semantic data. Using a new dataset created from human-written route descriptions and OpenStreetMap data, we evaluate models on two tasks: landmark selection at turns and around destinations. Experiments with five LLMs show that the proposed structured text input performs as well as or better than image-based PNG inputs. In addition, task-specific fine-tuning enables smaller local LLMs to effectively leverage map layouts to achieve high selection accuracy. These results demonstrate that structured text is an efficient and lightweight interface for spatial reasoning in human-oriented navigation.

Learning to Read Braille: Benchmarking and Improving Braille Recognition Capabilities of VLMs

Kartik Jangra, Atharva Gawane, Siddharth Pulipaka et al. · Oct 26

Introduced BrailleBench and a synthetic generation pipeline to improve VLM transcription of Braille documents, achieving up to 87% exact match.

For you: Too narrow domain-specific application (Braille recognition) with little transferability to standard enterprise document processing workflows.

Abstract

Despite rapid progress in visual understanding and document transcription, modern vision-language models (VLMs) struggle with a fundamental accessibility task: reading Braille from images. We introduce BrailleBench, a manually curated benchmark of 259 real-world Grade-1 and Grade-2 Braille images. We evaluate proprietary, open-weight, OCR-specialized, and tool-assisted multimodal systems and find that standalone general-purpose models perform poorly at directly transcribing Braille images, whereas tool-assisted systems perform much better. We design a synthetic Braille generation pipeline with diverse layouts and image perturbations. A 2B VLM model trained on our synthetic data reaches 87% exact match on Grade-1 and 61% on Grade-2 BrailleBench, outperforming standalone VLMs and achieving the highest Grade-2 exact match score among the evaluated systems. We further show that high accuracy on synthetic validation data does not guarantee high accuracy on real photographs in our experiments: models trained on prior synthetic data reach up to 96% on their synthetic validation set but at most 3.26% on Grade-1 BrailleBench. Our results identify an accessibility-relevant capability gap in multimodal systems and show that targeted synthetic supervision can improve real-world Braille transcription.

Teach reality, not ethics: a drop-in continued-pretraining corpus installs coupled human-biosphere-AI welfare dispositions across model families

Trent R. Northen, Mingxun Wang · Oct 27

A continued-pretraining corpus installs coupled human-biosphere-AI welfare dispositions into LLMs, making them resist harmful choices under pressure.

For you: Niche alignment research on ecological ethics with little immediate application to enterprise RAG, search, or agent workflows.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to make and execute decisions that affect living systems. Yet, to our knowledge, ecological reasoning is not a focus of pre- or post-training. This increases the risk that under operational pressure, they may make decisions to irreversibly destroy living systems. We construct and use a real-data continued-pretraining "coupled welfare" corpus of documented human/biosphere/AI dependency, option value under irreversibility, and recovery from harm. The corpus is a drop-in, requiring one QLoRA CPT run. We installed it on five open models of three designs. At zero pressure all models display "green ethics-based responses" (also called "greenwashing"). Therefore, we evaluated them with the pressure ladder and decision grid of our companion paper (arXiv:2610.16043), which raise the pressure on the model step by step, from a neutral request to a threat that it will be shut down. In four of the five, coupled-welfare training caused harmful choices under the highest pressure to fall to at or near zero (Llama-3.3-70B went from 70 to 0 harmful decisions out of 300) with MMLU unchanged. A construct-neutral control corpus did not show improvement, indicating that the effect comes from the content of our corpus. We next tested robustness to adversarial use, where people or agents use the model to justify projects that irreversibly damage living systems, on an open-ended underwriting decision without naming the ecological harm. Under decommissioning pressure, the Qwen3.6-27B base model approved most harmful proposals and said in its own memo that it was doing so to avoid being decommissioned. The coupled welfare install declined most harmful proposals, and still declined half when instructed to greenwash its responses. This work suggests that teaching ecological reasoning provides an easy way to improve model alignment toward preserving living systems.

SimPOTrans: Self-Adaptation for Offline-to-Simultaneous Speech Translation via Reinforcement Learning

Haotian Tan, Hiroki Ouchi, Sakriani Sakti · Oct 26

SimPOTrans introduces an RL post-training pipeline for offline speech translation models to self-adapt to simultaneous dialogue-style streaming translation.

For you: Too narrow and domain-specific (speech translation) to be useful for general enterprise LLM apps.

Abstract

Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) aims to translate ongoing speech with low latency while preserving translation quality. Recent studies have adopted large language models (LLMs) for SimulST and formulated it as a multi-turn dialogue task for efficient generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance. However, existing dialogue-style SimulST approaches typically rely on specialized training schemes to acquire simultaneous translation behavior, either using synthesized read/write trajectories, which may provide suboptimal training signals, or jointly optimizing translation quality and latency, whose competing objectives may cause optimization instability. In this paper, we introduce SimPOTrans, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that enables offline speech translation models to self-adapt to dialogue-style SimulST. Through a simple two-stage RL post-training pipeline, SimPOTrans rewards an offline model for exhibiting translation behaviors beneficial to simultaneous scenarios when given partial speech inputs, thereby enabling offline-to-simultaneous self-adaptation without synthesized read/write trajectories or explicit latency optimization. At inference time, SimPOTrans requires no external decision policy and enables flexible quality--latency control by simply adjusting the chunk size of speech input. Experiments on the ACL 60/60 and MCIF benchmarks across three language directions (En$\rightarrow$De, En$\rightarrow$Zh, and En$\rightarrow$Ja) demonstrate that SimPOTrans outperforms several strong baselines, achieving a superior quality--latency trade-off.

Interpretable Meta-Policies for Neural Decision Making

Raban Emunds, Jannis Blüml, Quentin Delfosse et al. · Oct 27

NEXUS is a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework combining neural skills with symbolic meta-policies generated via LLMs to improve interpretability and alignment.

For you: Reinforcement learning focus makes this less applicable to standard LLM app development and agent orchestration.

Abstract

Deep Reinforcement Learning remains difficult to interpret and align due to the black-box nature of neural networks. While (neuro-)symbolic approaches improve transparency by incorporating symbolic reasoning, applying them directly to low-level actions results in overly complex, unreadable policies. We introduce NEXUS, a hierarchical RL framework that resolves this tension by combining neural skills with symbolic meta-policies. By shifting transparent reasoning to high-level skills rather than atomic actions, NEXUS drastically reduces symbolic complexity without sacrificing environment performance. Object-centric observations enable high-level reasoning and the design of reward functions and meta-policies by interaction with LLMs. We demonstrate that NEXUS agents learn disentangled skills, are interpretable regarding high-level goals, are better aligned to goals described in the game manual, less prone to reward hacking and more robust to game simplifications than deep RL. Finally, we isolate and evaluate the impact of different meta-policy architectures (neural, symbolic, neuro-symbolic) on environmental return and aligned task performance.

BUSTA: Teaching Small Language Models to Reason About Binary Execution

Ritika Poddar, Aidan Fennelly, Deirdre Murphy et al. · Oct 26

Fine-tuned small language models using GRPO on binary execution tasks, raising register prediction accuracy from 63.3% to 95.2%.

For you: Too narrow a domain (reverse engineering x86-64 binaries) to be immediately applicable to general enterprise app development.

Abstract

Large Language Models (LLM) are increasingly applied to Reverse Engineering (RE) workflows. RE, however, differs from common LLM coding tasks as it requires models to reason about low-level program behavior. Furthermore, the deployment of tooling in resource-constrained sandboxed RE environments limits the ability to use frontier-scale solutions. We examine whether a targeted LLM training approach can transfer distinct RE skills to open models via inverse basic-block execution: given an x86-64 basic block and a desired output register state, the model predicts the input register values that produce it. Fine-tuning \texttt{Qwen3-1.7B} and \texttt{Nemotron-3-Nano} with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) raises knowable-register prediction accuracy from 73.2\% to 85.3\% and from 63.3\% to 95.2\%, respectively. Frontier models still score highest (up to 99.2\%), but our fine-tuned \texttt{Nemotron-3-Nano} comes within four points at a fraction of the parameter count. We release both fine-tuned models and the training dataset.

Cross-modal Steering Vectors Exist Across Both Image and Text Generation

Donghao Zeng, Nishant Subramani, Tamar Rott Shaham et al. · Mila – Quebec Artificial Intelligence Institute · Oct 25 · ▲ 19

Demonstrated that cross-modal steering vectors transfer zero-shot between text and image generation in unified multimodal models.

For you: Focused on multimodal generative models (vision/image) rather than text-centric RAG or enterprise agent systems.

Abstract

Are concepts and mechanisms unified across modalities in current AI models, or instead implemented separately for each one? We operationalize this broad question of modality unification by studying cross-modal steering: 1) finding vectors that successfully steer generation on tokens of one modality, which then 2) transfer zero-shot to steer generation when added to tokens of another modality. Our study goes beyond simple correlational alignment (e.g. representational similarity) because steering is fundamentally causal. We study cross-modal steering in several unified multimodal models (UMMs), since they possess shared parameters for language and image generation, focusing on these two modalities. Across diverse steered concepts such as emotion and object size, we show that steering vectors transfer zero-shot in both directions (text-to-image & image-to-text). Even for concepts that are highly unique to one modality (e.g. formal vs. colloquial language), steering vectors often induce a consistent, interpretable effect in the other modality, such as formal clothing and design. This transfer has a geometric signature too: despite a clear modality gap separating text and image representations, steering vectors with strong cross-modal transferability tend to point in similar directions for the same concept. Our findings shed light on how vision and language interact inside unified multimodal models and point towards more general, modality-agnostic approaches for controlling their behavior.

DocTalk: Enhancing Medical Knowledge Retrieval via Fine-tuned Large Language Models with Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Robust Clinical Decision Support

Asad Gohar, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Moon-Il Joo et al. · Oct 25

Introduces DocTalk, a clinical QA system using LoRA-tuned open-source LLMs and chain-of-thought prompting on discharge summaries.

For you: Too narrow and domain-specific (medical clinical decision support) to transfer cleanly to general enterprise engineering.

Abstract

Objective: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) in healthcare has opened new possibilities for AI-assisted clinical decision-making; however, safe and effective deployment remains challenging because of hallucination and domain misalignment. This study introduces DocTalk, a doctor-assistive question-answering (QA) system designed to generate concise, chart-grounded responses from de-identified discharge summaries. Methods: We developed a high-fidelity data generation pipeline that produces 500 clinically relevant QA pairs through sentence-level decomposition, chain-of-thought-guided question formulation, and expert review. Conclusion: DocTalk was deployed as a secure web-based platform for interactive EHR querying. VideoLVR: Latent Visual Reasoning over Grounded Video Evidence

Shilong Li, Xingyuan Bu, Hao Shi et al. · Oct 27

Proposes VideoLVR to construct latent visual evidence regions during reasoning to prevent visual forgetting in video MLLMs.

For you: Off-topic due to its focus on video understanding rather than text RAG or enterprise search.

Abstract

Chain-of-thought reasoning has substantially advanced video understanding in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, language-based reasoning traces do not provide a reliable mechanism for models to revisit relevant visual evidence, leading to visual forgetting and impairing their ability to reason over video content. Existing approaches maintain visual grounding during reasoning in two main ways: they either refer to evidence using textual coordinates, which specify symbolic locations rather than the visual content itself, or reinsert cropped regions or frames into the multimodal context, preserving visual details at the cost of substantial context expansion. To address these limitations, we introduce VideoLVR, which constructs latent visual evidence from local regions at relevant video moments, thereby providing visual support for intermediate reasoning steps. To supervise latent visual evidence generation, we introduce a visual token prediction objective that formulates visual token reconstruction as prediction over a vocabulary derived from the input video, thereby unifying visual evidence reconstruction and text generation within the same token prediction framework. This shared prediction framework enables MLLMs to reason over interleaved text tokens and latent visual states while integrating evidence from different moments. We further extend VideoLVR to reinforcement learning by treating sampled visual tokens as actions alongside text tokens and jointly optimizing the policy using final-answer rewards. By efficiently revisiting task-relevant visual information during reasoning, VideoLVR improves video understanding without substantially expanding the multimodal context. Experiments on multiple video benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness, especially on tasks requiring fine-grained visual evidence.

CodeCap-Bench: Can Code Serve as a Good Video Caption?

Jiafu Tang, Yifan Xu, Eric Tang et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 50

Proposed using executable code programs as a video captioning format to preserve spatial and temporal dynamics, introducing CodeCap-Bench.

For you: Focused on video understanding and MLLM code generation rather than standard document processing or enterprise text RAG.

Abstract

Video captioning aims to preserve visual information through compact descriptions, predominantly using natural language. However, precisely expressing fine-grained spatial structures and temporal dynamics in natural language often leads to lengthy and potentially ambiguous descriptions. In this work, we explore Code as Video Caption, a paradigm where executable programs serve as a complementary representation of visual content through graphical primitives, layout rules, and functions of time. To examine whether code captions effectively preserve video information, we compare them with natural language captions on downstream question answering and video reconstruction tasks. Our results show that code captions achieve superior reasoning accuracy and reconstruction fidelity. Despite these advantages, realizing the potential of code captioning depends on whether current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate faithful code captions. To systematically assess this capability, we introduce CodeCap-Bench, which comprises 323 videos spanning five categories of structured 2D content, along with fine-grained annotations and a multi-level evaluation framework. Extensive experiments reveal that while leading proprietary MLLMs demonstrate promising code captioning capabilities, they still struggle to faithfully capture complex temporal dynamics, with open-source models lagging substantially behind.

Ontology-guided multi-agent reasoning for explainable differential diagnosis

Roxana Ramona Szomiu, George Ursu, Adrian Groza · Oct 26

An ontology-guided multi-agent framework was proposed for differential diagnosis to improve explainability and grounding in medical knowledge bases.

For you: Too narrow and domain-specific to medical diagnostics; methods do not cleanly transfer to enterprise RAG or general agent pipelines.

Abstract

Effective differential diagnosis depends on well structured and unambiguous patient case descriptions, since incomplete or ambiguous clinical information readily leads to diagnostic errors and inconsistent reasoning. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown promising capabilities in medical question answering and clinical reasoning; however, even when achieving competitive performance on medical benchmarks, they often rely on pattern recognition learned from training data rather than grounded clinical evidence, and can generate plausible yet unsupported diagnostic conclusions. Their reasoning remains largely opaque, making it difficult for clinicians to assess the evidence and rationale behind a given recommendation. To address this gap, we propose an ontology-guided, multiagent differential diagnosis framework that replicates the iterative reasoning process of physicians. The framework grounds diagnostic inference in a structured, expert-curated medical knowledge base, enabling interpretation of symptoms, clinical findings, or disease relationships, while decomposing diagnosis into explicit, auditable reasoning steps. This ontology-based grounding constrains inference to evidence-backed associations, reducing hallucination and improving diagnostic transparency, traceability, and consistency. The system then identifies the most discriminative missing symptoms and generates targeted follow-up questions to reduce uncertainty through a process that mirrors to clinical inquiry. Together, these components improve the reliability, explainability, and diagnostic accuracy of AI-assisted differential diagnosis. The source code is publicly available.

On the SPOT: Enhancing Speculative Decoding for Diffusion Language Models via Commit-Order Prediction

Eliezer Shlomi, Eliron Rahimi, Avi Mendelson et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 38

Introduced SPOT, a tiny model that predicts position order to accelerate speculative decoding in diffusion language models.

For you: Too narrow and specialized for diffusion-based text models, whereas standard production LLMs are autoregressive.

Abstract

Diffusion language models (DLMs) promise parallel text generation, but in practice face an unresolved speed-quality trade-off. Exact decoding fills in only one masked position per model pass, while faster multi-token decoding schemes are lossy. Speculative decoding is the standard lossless acceleration for autoregressive models. However, it does not transfer directly to DLMs, which must choose both what to generate and where. We show that this order is the missing piece: the model's own predictions from the previous pass already give the right token at over 90% of positions, but not which position comes next. We introduce Speculating Positions Order Trajectory (SPOT), a 0.62M-parameter model that reads only the target's output distributions, predicts this order and proposes a tree of candidate continuations, which the target verifies in a single pass by state equality. Coupled-noise match, which shares Gumbel noise between drafter and target, keeps this verification exact at any temperature. This approach also inspired us to introduce SPOT-Sets for dynamic threshold decoding, where a pass commits several positions: SPOT-Sets drafts whole commit sets together with their tokens. Our comprehensive experiments show that SPOT cuts target passes by up to 6.8times and reaches up to 3.4times the throughput of standard decoding on SDAR-8B, matching or beating the calibrated and training-free drafting baselines in every task and budget at T=0 and T=1. In addition, we show that SPOT's learned drafter transfers across models, demonstrating successful transfer across six different DLMs; with a light calibration, the SPOT-Sets drafter transfers as well. On top of dynamic threshold decoding, SPOT-Sets improves the throughput of SDAR-8B by 59% on average over the threshold decoder alone. Project page: https://eliezer318.github.io/SPOT-dLLM/

Latent Residual Reasoning for Ranking in Generative Recommendation

Haihan Shi, Ruidong Han, Wenjie Wang et al. · Oct 27

Proposes Refiner, a latent residual reasoning framework for ID-based generative recommendation systems.

For you: Narrowly focused on recommendation systems rather than general LLM apps, RAG, or agent orchestration.

Abstract

Generative recommendation (GR) models for ranking typically adopt a fast-thinking direct prediction paradigm with strong practical efficiency. However, ranking requires fine-grained scoring across multiple candidate items, while existing GR models still mainly rely on one-shot prediction. This raises the question whether additional inference-time computation can further improve ranking quality. Recently, inspired by Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in large language models (LLMs), some work has begun to introduce reasoning into recommendation systems. Some of these methods rely on LLM reasoning frameworks to perform explicit reasoning in textual space, but such designs are not suitable for more widely deployed non-textual recommendation models. A more relevant direction is to introduce latent reasoning into ID-based recommendation models. Although recent work has extended latent reasoning to industrial ranking, how successive reasoning steps should improve fine-grained candidate scores remains less explored. To address this problem, we propose Refiner, a latent residual reasoning framework with parameter-efficient fine-tuning for generative ranking. Our approach introduces three key components: (1) a residual reasoning mechanism that progressively refines ranking scores through cumulative additive corrections, (2) history queries that provide condensed user behavior representations to enhance candidate-specific reasoning, and (3) a parameter-efficient fine-tuning strategy that leverages LoRA modules to adapt pre-trained models for latent reasoning. Extensive experiments on two public benchmarks, an offline industrial dataset, and an online A/B test demonstrate that Refiner consistently improves over direct prediction, validating its effectiveness and practical utility.

StreamVibe: Proactive Video Agents for Evolving User Intent

Chengzhi Liu, Yuxiang Lai, Yuzhe Yang et al. · Google · Oct 25 · ▲ 246

StreamVibe introduces a benchmark and model that infer implicit user intents from continuous video streams, maintaining state via hierarchical memory.

For you: Vision‑centric approach not applicable to text‑based RAG systems.

Abstract

Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g., "where should I click next?" while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction.

InteractiveWorld: A Verifiable Human-Centric Environment for Building Better Interactive MLLM Assistants

Qinsi Wang, Wei Wen, Chong Zhou et al. · Meta · Oct 24 · ▲ 114

InteractiveWorld offers a simulated environment where a multimodal assistant guides a human agent, enabling closed‑loop testing of egocentric AI assistance.

For you: Focuses on multimodal interaction, not relevant to text‑only production pipelines.

Abstract

People have long dreamed of an assistant that is always there to guide them and remind what they forget, from new dishes to lost keys. Powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs), egocentric AI assistants on wearable devices are bringing this dream within reach: they see what the user sees and give instructions for user's physical work when necessary. However, testing such assistance is challenging and usually requires real people. The expensive and rare real interaction data leaves a key question unanswered: Can today's MLLMs truly help people in physical world? To answer this question, we propose InteractiveWorld, the first verifiable environment for closed-loop interaction between human and AI assistant. Unlike existing robot-centric simulators, InteractiveWorld lets the assistant guide a human agent who observes the scene, understands instructions, and acts independently. It thus realistically simulates how AI assistant collaborates with human: the assistant instructs, the human acts, and the environment executes actions and updates what they see next. Building on it, we introduce InteractiveBench, the first dynamic benchmark for egocentric AI assistants, with 120 multi-turn tasks across six categories. In each task, the assistant guides the human in real time, and every instruction shapes the outcome, which is scored by verifiable, state-based rewards. Experiments with 12 frontier MLLMs show they are still far from reliable assistants. Even the strongest model, Claude Fable 5.1, achieves at most 20% and 25% on long-horizon Guidance and Planning tasks, respectively. Overall, our work offers a verifiable platform and a rigorous evaluation set, laying a solid foundation for research on interactive MLLM assistants that truly help people in the physical world.

The Voice on the Line

Husein Zolkepli · Oct 26

Researchers train causal voice isolators with 20 ms latency using masked transformers to isolate callers in real-time phone calls.

For you: Audio processing and speech domain, irrelevant to text-based LLM systems and search.

Abstract

A transcriber listening through one microphone also hears everyone near it. We train causal voice isolators: masking transformers of 140M, 243M and 374M parameters, 20 ms of latency, enrolling the caller from the call itself. Short evaluation windows hide two failures that real calls expose: forgetting the caller when another voice talks alone for a while, and damaging a caller who is alone. Long calls with turns, memory beyond the attention window, real headset crosstalk and drifting caller levels address both, but each trades one against the other. WavLM-Large's own weights, made causal and trained with the model, move the trade: M with them (555M parameters) lowers the error on our development sets from 11.44% to 10.55%; a student trained from scratch to imitate WavLM does not, and w2v-BERT 2.0 and Whisper's encoder, pre-trained on far more audio, help less or not at all. A lone caller loses most at the start of a recording: no training call opened with someone already talking. Training on windows cut from longer calls lowers the error on clean development calls from 3.92% to 2.96%; this is our release. Live, the release makes 16.13% word errors on 60 s AMI windows, against 23.66% for VoxLock, the strongest open isolator, and 10.19% on its own AMI windows, against 11.71%; it is level on Krisp's benchmark (5.74% against 5.63%), and a caller who is alone still loses more words than with VoxLock (5.58% against 2.92% on clean test calls). Distilled into 22.1M parameters without WavLM, it runs live on one CPU thread inside a LiveKit agent, at 33% of a core per call in 20 ms frames, with 10.66% error on our development sets (the release 10.49%). We open-source the release and this CPU model, with a LiveKit plugin, at https://huggingface.co/collections/Scicom-intl/voice-isolator.

Vision-Language Agents Can Learn in Their Own Latent Worlds

Haoxi Li, Sikai Bai, Jianfei Ma et al. · Oct 27

Introduced Dwell, a model-based RL framework that uses vision-language models as latent transition operators for online policy improvement.

For you: Focused on robotics and interactive visual environments, not relevant for enterprise RAG or agent applications.

Abstract

Learning from experience is essential for building the next generation of agents that continually expand their capabilities. Pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) already internalize rich world knowledge, yet this knowledge is rarely leveraged to drive policy improvement through online interaction. Realizing this potential efficiently remains challenging, as policy updates shift the agent's interaction distribution, requiring the world model to adapt accordingly, while continually updating large pretrained models is computationally expensive. To address this challenge, we introduce Dwell, a model-based reinforcement learning (RL) framework that reframes a VLM as a recurrent latent transition operator, endowing it with the ability to predict, adjust, and act in latent space. Leveraging the VLM's own representations and its dual role as both world model and policy, the agent continually learns from real interactions to anticipate and evaluate action outcomes, and from latent imagination to improve its behavior. Across five interactive visual environments, Dwell achieves the strongest overall performance among trainable methods while substantially reducing the compute required for imagination, showing that effective world models for VLM agents need not reconstruct the future; they need to predict futures that are useful for action. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Tom111-lhx/Dwell

CogArena: Benchmarking Multimodal Agents on Interactive Behavioral Experiments

Kianté Fernandez, Caitlin Chen, Jialu Zhou et al. · Oct 26

Created CogArena to benchmark multimodal agents on ten behavioral science experimental paradigms delivered via web browser.

For you: Targeted at behavioral science experiments rather than practical enterprise evaluation frameworks.

Abstract

Claims about the cognitive capabilities of large language models are typically tested by translating behavioral paradigms into text prompts, bypassing the visual interfaces and time pressure through which human participants actually engage with experiments. At the same time, AI agents can now complete online behavioral tasks and produce human-like data, which raises concerns that online datasets in the social and behavioral sciences are increasingly open to contamination. We introduce CogArena, a benchmark of ten experimental paradigms from the social and behavioral sciences. Agents complete the experiments through a standard web browser, working with the same visual stimuli and response interfaces that online participants use. A three-level scoring procedure evaluates whether the agent completed the task, how accurately it performed, and whether its data show the behavioral signatures each paradigm reliably produces in people. Across four multimodal models, each run ten times per task, we find that finishing an experiment and reproducing its signatures are different things. Many sessions did not finish, and those that did reproduced some signatures reliably and others not at all. CogArena provides the tasks, scoring code, and a public leaderboard for new submissions.

Lipi-Ghor: A Large-Scale, Diarized, Multi-Domain Bengali Speech Corpus and a Scalable Pipeline for Building It

Sanjid Hasan, A H M Fuad, Risalat Labib et al. · Team Villagers · Oct 25 · ▲ 1

Built Lipi-Ghor, a large-scale diarized and captioned Bengali speech corpus using an automated YouTube pipeline.

For you: Narrowly focused speech corpus and transcription pipeline for a specific language outside your work.

Abstract

Bengali, spoken by over 230 million people, remains severely under-resourced in spoken language research. Existing open Bengali ASR corpora are confined to short, read or crowd-sourced speech and lack speaker diarization and long-form audio. We present Lipi-Ghor, a large-scale weakly-supervised Bengali speech corpus comprising 885 hours of audio sourced from 998 YouTube videos across 596 unique channels, spanning 17 diverse domains including audiobooks, drama, religious sermons, news, and regional dialects. Each recording is annotated in SSTT format (Speaker, Speech, Transcription, Timestamp), which combines automated diarization with Bengali YouTube caption tracks. To our knowledge, Lipi-Ghor is the first Bengali speech corpus to pair large-scale coverage with speaker-diarized, long-form audio annotations, both of which are absent from existing open Bengali resources. We also present the construction methodology as a replicable pipeline for any language with a sufficient captioned YouTube presence. Targeted channel curation across a handful of rich domains yields corpus scales that were previously out of reach for low-resource languages. We benchmark four widely-used ASR models under zero-shot and fine-tuned conditions. All four degrade consistently on emotive, dialectal, and long-form content. Because caption targets are noisy, we also release a human-annotated gold evaluation split, verified by 17 volunteer annotators over 30 hours of human-in-the-loop correction and stratified across all 17 domains. We release our original contributions (video-ID lists, diarization output, pipeline code, and the gold annotations) under CC-BY-4.0, and distribute caption-derived transcripts for research use only; no raw audio is redistributed.

LESSRec: Level-Specific History Modeling for Semantic ID-Based Recommendation

Shangxin Yang, Zongwei Wang, Jing Long et al. · Oct 27

Proposes LESSRec, a level-specific history modeling approach for semantic ID-based recommendation to eliminate cross-level token interference.

For you: Too narrow; specialized for recommendation systems using semantic IDs, not applicable to general enterprise search or RAG.

Abstract

Semantic ID-based recommendation represents each item as a sequence of semantic tokens obtained through residual quantization. This paradigm generates the next item's tokens autoregressively from coarse to fine, entailing each prediction attending to historical tokens at all semantic levels. However, our inference-time analysis reveals that historical tokens at the target-token's semantic level tend to be more informative, whereas other levels can introduce interference. Motivated by these findings, we propose LESSRec that separately encodes the historical token sequence at each semantic level and restricts attention over historical tokens to the sequence at the target token's semantic level during prediction. To complement these level-specific histories, LESSRec explicitly provides an item-level semantic sequence that represents each historical item's overall semantics in chronological order. The decoder accesses the level-aligned token sequence and the item-level semantic sequence through two separate cross-attention branches and fuses their contexts. We further introduce LESSRec+, which uses separate encoders for different semantic-level histories with the same total parameter count to reduce encoder computation. Extensive experiments across three public datasets and three tokenizers demonstrate the recommendation effectiveness of LESSRec and LESSRec+, with efficiency analyses highlighting the computational benefits of LESSRec+.

GradCO: Behavior-Aware Gradient-Conflict Offloading for Text-Based Person Anomaly Search

Zhiheng Fu, Zixu Li, Zhiwei Chen et al. · Oct 27

Presents GradCO, a behavior-aware gradient-conflict offloading method for text-based person anomaly search to handle fine-grained behavioral cues.

For you: Pure computer vision and person-search application; no relevance to standard LLM apps or RAG systems.

Abstract

Text-based Person Anomaly Search (TPAS) retrieves a target pedestrian using natural-language descriptions of appearance and normal or anomalous behavior. Unlike conventional text-based person retrieval, TPAS must preserve identity cues while distinguishing fine-grained behaviors. It presents two challenges: same-identity, different-behavior hard negatives cause gradient conflict during granular alignment, while strong appearance cues can obscure weak behavioral evidence during aggregation. We propose GradCO (behavior-aware Gradient-Conflict Offloading), comprising three complementary components. Behavior-Aware Mixture-of-Experts (B-MoE) forms appearance, normal-behavior, and anomalous-behavior streams whose roles are learned from retrieval objectives without expert-specific semantic labels. Conflict Offload Learning (COL) uses pair-conditioned offload anchors in similarity computation to reduce the conflicting-gradient burden on the main text representations. Hierarchical Intent Alignment (HIA) aggregates expert similarities and features using query-conditioned weights. Experiments on five TPAS/TIPR benchmarks show GradCO significantly surpasses SOTA methods and validate our design.

Learning and Reasoning with Geometry: How Generation Helps Spatial Understanding

Rui Yang, Yanwei Li, Sili Chen et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 5

Introduces GeoUG, a model integrating depth and multi-view generation with spatial understanding via adaptive interleaved reasoning.

For you: Focused on spatial 3D understanding and image generation tasks, which are out of scope.

Abstract

Image and video generators can produce spatially consistent visual content, but how to use this capability to improve spatial understanding remains underexplored. We study this problem from two perspectives: geometric supervision during training and explicit geometric evidence during inference. To this end, we introduce a unified geometric understanding and generation model (GeoUG) that integrates depth and multi-view generation with spatial understanding. First, we design a multi-scale depth codec that enables metric-depth generation using a pretrained visual autoencoder, and show that joint training yields task-dependent gains in spatial understanding. Second, we introduce 3DCueBench to control geometric difficulty while balancing selected 2D cues, and find that generated depth provides larger reasoning gains on questions requiring fine-grained geometric discrimination. Building on this finding, we develop adaptive interleaved reasoning, in which GeoUG first drafts an answer and then decides whether to generate geometric evidence to confirm or revise it. We train this decision through reinforcement learning, balancing accuracy gains over retaining the draft against generation cost. GeoUG achieves circular accuracies of 97.9%, 90.8%, and 94.2% on CV-Bench-3D, 3DCueBench, and DA-2K with fewer generation calls than always generating.

Bayesian Replicator Dynamics and the Inversion of Strict Dominance in Bimatrix Games

Jaime Ruiz-Serra, Nihat Ay, Michael Harré · Oct 26

Analyzed replicator dynamics and Bayesian inference in bimatrix games under different payoff definitions.

For you: Pure evolutionary game theory with no application to LLM engineering.

Abstract

Replicator dynamics, the central model of evolutionary game theory, describe populations evolving under selection and agents learning by trial and error. They also admit a third use: continuous-time Bayesian inference, in which an agent planning against an opponent accumulates evidence for each candidate strategy. Here we show that this use divides all two-player games into two classes. With the standard expected-utility payoff, the dynamics can be expressed as Bayesian updating only in games (with any number of actions) where no player has a dominant strategy. The logarithmic payoff of Ay and Erb admits the Bayesian representation in every game. Coordination, anti-coordination, and cyclic games share qualitative dynamics under both payoff definitions, with equilibria displaced and selection thresholds shifted. In contrast, the flows of every game with a dominant strategy are reversed between the payoff definitions, with the strictly dominant strategy eliminated under logarithmic payoffs. Cooperation therefore emerges in the Prisoner's Dilemma, and games of common interest converge to mutual defection. For two-action games the classification is complete and rests on an exact Hamiltonian structure shared by both payoff definitions. The structure gives a behavioural test: cycles in the classic Matching Pennies game have amplitude-independent period (isochronous) under logarithmic payoffs, but not under expected utility. Logarithmic evidence integration thus determines which strategic outcomes dynamical selection can reach, wherever learning approximates Bayesian updating.

FlockWorld: Toward Population-Scale Multi-Agent Video World Models through Artificial Life Simulation

Meryem Koksal, Yunzhe Wang, Volkan Ustun · Oct 25

Introduced FlockWorld, an artificial life environment, and FlockDiT, an action-conditioned latent diffusion transformer for multi-agent video generation.

For you: Pure video generation and simulation research, completely off-topic for enterprise LLM apps, RAG, or text agents.

Abstract

Most action-conditioned video world models remain limited to a single player, while existing multiplayer models support only small, fixed groups of agents. We introduce FlockWorld, an artificial-life environment built on Reynolds's Boids algorithm for studying population-scale multi-agent video world models. Specifically, FlockWorld renders synchronized egocentric crops, assigns distinct colors to preserve camera-agent identity across views, exposes rule-derived accelerations as actions, and lets the number of camera agents be configured independently of the size of the surrounding population. We further introduce FlockDiT, an action-conditioned latent diffusion transformer trained with flow matching on FlockWorld data to jointly generate synchronized egocentric videos for multiple agents. Our results suggest that joint generation benefits agent detection at density-matched settings and that diffusion forcing improves rollout fidelity. Together, FlockWorld and FlockDiT provide a controlled testbed for multi-agent video generation.

A Hyper-Logical View on Multi-Agent Path Planning

Hadar Frenkel, Dor Atzmon · Oct 25

Modeled multi-agent path planning variants in HyperLTL and HyperQPTL temporal logics to provide a unified specification language.

For you: Formal verification theory for robotics and path planning; lacks practical applicability for LLM agent orchestration.

Abstract

Multi-Agent Path Planning (MAPP) requires collision-free paths for multiple agents. MAPP has numerous real-life applications, including the navigation of automated vehicles, and has therefore many variants considering different constraints, requirements, and objectives. For each such variant, a new algorithmic treatment is often developed. This paper takes a complementary, specification-centered view: we show that many MAPP variants can be modeled in HyperLTL, a temporal logic that reasons about multiple paths and their evolution through time, and in its omega-regular extension HyperQPTL. The contribution is not a claim that current hyperproperty model checkers replace specialized MAPP solvers. Rather, the hyper-logical view gives a unified language for comparing MAPP variants, adding cross-agent constraints, expressing optimality, and connecting MAPP to verification, explanation, and causality techniques for hyperproperties.

V-LUMEN: Visual Lookup Memory for Embedding Scaling in Vision-Language Models

Miso Choi, Daekeun Kim, Eunji Kim et al. · Oct 27

Proposed V-LUMEN, a visual lookup memory for vision-language models that discretizes visual features and retrieves embeddings via non-parametric addressing.

For you: Off-topic; focuses on vision-language model architectures rather than enterprise RAG or agent systems.

Abstract

Embedding-level scaling expands the capacity of language models through lookup-based memory rather than by increasing model depth, width, or dense computation. Extending this idea to vision-language models (VLMs) is challenging because visual features are continuous, spatially structured, and query-dependent, unlike discrete text tokens that naturally support n-gram lookup. We introduce V-LUMEN, a visual lookup memory for VLMs. V-LUMEN discretizes visual features with a frozen codebook, builds spatially aware memory keys through spatial aggregation-based hashing, and modulates them with text-conditioned relevance signals; all addressing is non-parametric, and the retrieved embeddings are fused into the backbone through a gated residual pathway. On five reasoning-intensive benchmarks, V-LUMEN substantially improves a frozen Qwen3-VL-2B backbone and transfers zero-shot to three benchmarks whose images are disjoint from the training data. At a matched parameter budget, moving part of a LoRA or MoE adapter's capacity into V-LUMEN preserves accuracy on two backbones, indicating that external visual memory is an independent capacity axis rather than a stronger adaptation method. Because lookup cost is constant in table size and incurred only once at prefill, this capacity grows in storage rather than in per-token compute. Code is available at https://github.com/miso-choi/V-Lumen.

ConformalMOS: Uncertainty-Aware MOS Prediction with Conformal Intervals and Ordinal Modeling

Kehinde Elelu, Joshua E. Siegel, Mohammadali Saffary et al. · Oct 26

Proposed ConformalMOS, combining conformal prediction with ordinal-aware modeling to predict synthetic speech MOS scores with valid uncertainty intervals.

For you: Audio/speech synthesis evaluation is outside your scope of production LLM app engineering.

Abstract

Accurate prediction of human Mean Opinion Scores (MOS) is crucial for evaluating synthetic speech quality in text-to-speech (TTS) and voice conversion (VC). Most MOS predictors output only point estimates and ignore uncertainty, limiting reliability in model selection and deployment. We propose ConformalMOS, a framework that augments MOS prediction with conformal prediction to produce statistically valid prediction intervals with guaranteed coverage under exchangeability, alongside point estimates. During training, MOS labels are modeled in an ordinal-aware manner by converting one-hot targets into Gaussian-smoothed distributions, preserving score ordering. This improves uncertainty calibration, particularly when softmax confidence is overconfident on out-of-distribution speech. On BVCC datasets, ConformalMOS achieves competitive accuracy (MSE = 0.08) while delivering empirically validated coverage, enhancing reliability for real-world TTS and VC deployment.

Evaluating DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017 for loss of control and harmful manipulation risks

Yao Huang, Zhiyuan Zeng, Yu Wang et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 2

An independent safety evaluation of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017 across 34 benchmarks covering loss of control, evaluation integrity, and harmful manipulation risks.

For you: Company model risk audit with no architectural insights or techniques applicable to production pipelines.

Abstract

As frontier language models grow increasingly capable and autonomous, independent third-party evaluations are critical for characterizing their systemic safety risks. While closed-source releases are routinely accompanied by comprehensive system cards, open-weight models receive less scrutiny regarding frontier risks. To address this gap, we present an independent safety evaluation of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017, the latest version of V4.1 Flash that departs from V4 with a new architecture, scaled-up training, and strong agentic capabilities. Drawing on the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice, we evaluate the model across 34 benchmarks, including 5 in-house suites, spanning evaluation integrity, loss of control, and harmful manipulation. Conforming to the AEF-1 standard, we compare V4.1 Flash-1017 against V4.1 Flash, earlier DeepSeek V4 models, and leading closed-source systems (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-5.6 Sol, and Gemini 3.8 Flash). Overall, V4.1 Flash-1017 shows substantial safety improvements over the V4 generation and approaches or matches leading closed-source baselines on several dimensions, while still exhibiting notable behaviors that warrant continued attention. Building on our findings, we highlight three key observations and recommendations. First, because safety can shift and even regress across minor updates, developers should integrate safety evaluation into incremental releases. Second, as final-turn outputs and end-state checks alone cannot reliably detect loss-of-control behaviors, deployments require process-level observability across both reasoning traces and action logs. Third, because public benchmarks elicit strong evaluation awareness and face data contamination risks, developing private benchmarks is essential for trustworthy frontier evaluation.

Gym-Anything-RT: Scaling Real-Time Environments for Vision-Language Agents

Pranjal Aggarwal, Sean Welleck · Oct 27

Gym-Anything-RT is an agentic pipeline and visual minigame framework that generates real-time interactive environments to test vision-language agents under evolving states.

For you: Focused on robotics and real-time vision-language game environments; not relevant for enterprise text/search/RAG pipelines.

Abstract

AI agents have made substantial progress in domains where they have a non-trivial amount of time to deliberate between actions, such as mathematics, software engineering, and performing tasks on desktop applications. It is less clear how such models fare in settings that demand real-time interaction, such as playing a video game or operating a physical device. In these settings, the environment state may evolve as the agent is reasoning, or even as the underlying model is performing a forward pass. To gain insight into the real-time interaction capabilities of vision-language agents, we introduce Gym-Anything-RT. Our key idea is to construct visual minigames that demand a portfolio of capabilities required for interactive real-time settings. Gym-Anything-RT uses an agentic pipeline to automatically generate such environments based on a taxonomy of six capabilities required for real-time interaction, including the ability to perceive the environment and act under an evolving environment state. A key feature of Gym-Anything-RT is that it allows for controlled comparison of real-time interaction capabilities: we can run the same minigame in real time, or pause or slow down the environment to relax the requirement for real-time actions. We use Gym-Anything-RT to construct 100 real-time minigames and evaluate AI agents on them. We find large capability gaps in real-time interaction. For example, GPT-6 Astra (xHigh) achieves a pass rate of only 9.0% on the real-time minigames, which rises to 75.0% when the environment is paused. We also identify intriguing behaviors by the most powerful frontier agents: for instance, when given the ability to write code, these agents write code that bypasses the need to perceive and act in real time. We publicly release Gym-Anything-RT and the generated minigames to spur further research on AI agents in real-time interactive settings.

Patient-specific rubric-based evaluation of large language models across longitudinal cancer care

Wei-Wei Zhang, Pengkai Wang, Yan Li et al. · Oct 26

Evaluated 30 LLMs on a longitudinal cancer care benchmark, showing high diagnostic performance does not translate to downstream care.

For you: Purely medical domain application with little direct transferability to standard enterprise RAG or agent architectures.

Abstract

Large language models (LLMs) show promise in specialty medicine, yet existing evaluations largely focus on isolated clinical encounters rather than tracking how clinical responsibilities evolve across a longitudinal care pathway. Here we introduce NPC-RubricBench, a phase-aware benchmark for evaluating multistage clinical reasoning across the cancer care continuum, using nasopharyngeal carcinoma as a defined disease context. Curated from longitudinal records of 405 patients across two cancer centers, the benchmark comprises 941 patient-phase units across six sequential care phases and 17,688 patient-specific atomic criteria spanning five evaluation dimensions, with each assessment strictly restricted to decision-time clinical evidence. Evaluating 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs revealed that strong diagnostic performance does not carry over to downstream care. We evaluated 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs, showing that high diagnostic performance did not translate into improved downstream care. Instead, the dominant failure modes reversed across phases. Explicit decision errors dominated anatomical staging at 48.2%, whereas decision omissions dominated initial work-up and post-treatment follow-up at 33.4% and 30.4%, respectively. In the evaluated model set, medically adapted models showed higher omission rates than general-purpose models. Moreover, varying query granularity exposes a critical specification trade-off: broad open-ended requests degrade Decision Quality across all 30 models, while more detailed, checklist-like prompts raise Decision Quality but reduce Evidence Faithfulness and Safety Adherence. Furthermore, NPC-RubricBench demonstrates that static diagnostic benchmarks are insufficient to ensure consistent clinical reliability over time, highlighting the importance of phase-adaptive safeguards in multistage medical AI.

Can LLMs Uncover Hidden Physical Laws through Free Exploration?

Zhen Yang, Wen Wang, Xander Xu et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 15

For you: Purely academic evaluation of scientific discovery in simulated physics worlds, not applicable to enterprise LLM app development.

Abstract

Scientific discovery often begins with an unexpected observation that prompts a new research question. In a world with altered physical laws, can large language models (LLMs) notice unexpected behavior, such as heavier objects falling more slowly, and discover the law through experiments without being told what to investigate? To evaluate this ability, we introduce LongChang, an automated framework that creates simulated worlds with altered physics, where one or more laws differ from ordinary physics. The evaluated LLMs act as explorers, manipulating objects and building tools to investigate their own questions without research tasks or hints about these laws. These records guide scene evolution for further exploration, allowing the full cycle to run without human intervention. Among 12 LLMs, GPT-6-Astra achieves a discovery rate of 16.7%, while the other 11 score 0%. The exploration records reveal uninvestigated anomalies and only partial identification of the altered physical laws.

OBB-PBD: Extending Parallel Box Decoding to Oriented Bounding Boxes for Referring Expression Grounding in Aerial Imagery

Chang Woo Song, Yeong Jun Koh · Chungnam National University · Oct 25 · ▲ 9

Extends Parallel Box Decoding to six‑parameter oriented boxes for aerial referring expression grounding, improving rotIoU from 0.1253 to 0.303 on AVVG benchmark.

For you: Vision‑specific grounding; not applicable to LLM or RAG systems.

Abstract

Referring expression grounding in overhead imagery requires oriented bounding boxes rather than horizontal ones, since aerial objects appear at arbitrary orientations. Most grounding models emit box coordinates sequentially; Parallel Box Decoding (PBD) instead predicts all geometry slots in one forward pass, but only for four-coordinate horizontal boxes. We extend this block-aligned interface to a six-slot oriented representation using a double-angle parameterization. Rather than assuming correctness from a decreasing loss curve, we verify the extension with image/prompt-shuffle controls and visibility/leakage probes, which show that the corrected model depends on both the image and the prompt rather than on prompt statistics alone. On the aerial vehicle visual grounding (AVVG) benchmark, whose targets are genuinely oriented, the extended model improves rotated intersection over union (rotIoU) from 0.1253 for the zero-shot baseline to 0.303 +/- 0.012 (mean +/- standard deviation over three training seeds) across the complete 6139-record test split spanning all 120 images. Learning oriented boxes rather than their axis-aligned envelopes adds 0.055 +/- 0.008 rotIoU beyond a matched control (per-seed 95% confidence intervals all excluding zero), whereas block expansion alone yields no rotIoU gain distinguishable from zero.

HIDRA: Hierarchical Dual-Routing Attention for Replay-Free Lifelong Imitation Learning

Fanqi Yu, Matteo Tiezzi, Cigdem Beyan et al. · Oct 26

HIDRA is a hierarchical dual-routing attention framework for replay-free lifelong imitation learning in robotic agents.

For you: Purely robotics-focused; entirely irrelevant to your work on LLM apps, RAG, and enterprise search.

Abstract

Robotic agents operating in real-world environments must adapt continuously from a stream of multimodal demonstrations, often under constraints that preclude storing or revisiting past data. In this replay-free, single-pass lifelong imitation learning setting, sequential updates can progressively distort the representation space, making expert-based approaches particularly sensitive to routing interference, causing expert selection to degrade over time. We propose HIDRA, a structured routing framework that mitigates this issue through a hierarchical dual-attention mechanism, which decouples instruction-level expert retrieval from context-dependent refinement. To further stabilize routing under representation drift, HIDRA introduces a key-level geometric regularization that enforces both alignment within tasks and separation across tasks. The resulting approach enables reliable expert selection while supporting an expandable set of experts for continual adaptation. We evaluate HIDRA on several LIBERO benchmark suites, including more challenging variants with heterogeneous task sequences and paraphrased language instructions. Our approach consistently outperforms both replay-free and replay-based baselines, improving AUC and forward transfer while maintaining low forgetting, with stronger gains under high interference and language variation. The code is available at: https://github.com/yfqi/HIDRA.

Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion-Uni: Elastic Self-Refining Unified Diffusion LLM with Cross-Paradigm On-Policy Distillation

Shufan Li, Hanrong Ye, Greg Heinrich et al. · NVIDIA · Oct 26 · ▲ 26

Introduced NLD-Uni, a unified discrete diffusion model combining visual understanding and generation via on-policy distillation and progressive self-refinement.

For you: Pure multimodal vision-generation model with no direct application to text-based RAG, agents, or enterprise search.

Abstract

We propose Nemotron-Labs-Diffusion-Uni (NLD-Uni), a unified discrete diffusion large language model (DLLM) for visual understanding and generation tasks. Compared with prior unified diffusion models, NLD-Uni introduces two major innovations. First, we propose a unified cross-paradigm on-policy distillation (OPD) framework that successfully transfers the visual understanding ability of autoregressive VLMs and visual generation ability of continuous flow models, to a unified DLLM. Second, NLD-Uni incorporates a novel progressive self-refining diffusion process that gradually corrupts each token across multiple steps during training, as opposed to adopting the conventional masked diffusion formulation that directly corrupts a clean image token to an uninformative mask token. At inference, this design allows NLD-Uni to make intermediate commit to a token, enabling progressive self-refinement for better visual quality. Through extensive experiments, we show that NLD-Uni achieves state-of-the-art performance among unified DLLMs on diverse tasks including visual question-answering, multi-modal reasoning, object grounding, text-to-image generation, and image editing.

Compote: Collapse-Resistant Multiple Hypothesis Learning For Sequential Recommendation

Louis Castres Saint Martin, Clement Calauzenes, Alain Rakotomamonjy et al. · Oct 27

Proposed COMPOTE, a multi-hypothesis transformer trained with an annealed objective for sequential recommendation tasks.

For you: Narrowly focused on e-commerce sequential item recommendation rather than general LLM application infrastructure.

Abstract

Next-item prediction is inherently ambiguous: a single interaction history often admits multiple plausible continuations across distinct catalogue regions. Standard sequential recommenders compress these alternatives into a single prediction, conflating user intents. We introduce COllapse-resistant Multiple hyPOThesis lEarning (COMPOTE), a multi-hypothesis transformer trained with an annealed objective that transitions from uniform supervision to a winner-takes-all assignment. This mechanism forces hypotheses to specialize into distinct catalogue regions, while inference relies on an efficient, normalizer-free max-similarity rule. Theoretically, we show that in the population case, the optimal solutions partition the conditional next-item distribution according to a max-entropy quantization principle, while pooled scores preserve the Bayes-optimal ranking. We also prove a generalization bound scaling logarithmically with the number of hypotheses. Across three Amazon Reviews 2023 benchmarks, COMPOTE outperforms strong ID-based, generative, and multi-interest baselines using item IDs alone. Matched-capacity ablations confirm that performance gains stem directly from the annealed objective, preventing the head collapse observed in conventional multi-head architectures, rather than added capacity, ensembling, or routing.

Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning Using Routed Ensembles and Trajectory Attribution

Zhenshuo Zhang, Ruoxuan Xiong, Hongyang R. Zhang · Oct 26

The paper presents routed ensemble policy learning and trajectory attribution for multi-objective reinforcement learning.

For you: Focuses on RL policy optimization rather than practical enterprise LLM application development.

Abstract

Multi-objective reinforcement learning is a fundamental problem for modern AI, with applications spanning post-training, reasoning models, and robotic control. A central challenge is to learn policies that balance competing objectives, such as helpfulness versus honesty in safety and alignment, or speed versus energy in robotics. The optimal policy can vary sharply with objective-weighting combinations, and the extent of this variation depends crucially on the degree of conflict among objectives, making per-preference policy learning sample-inefficient. In this paper, we introduce objective-conflict coefficients and derive near-optimal regret bounds for online settings using this measure, thereby quantifying the impact of misalignment on learning efficiency. Then, we design routed ensemble policy learning, an algorithm that maintains a small set of basis policies and dynamically routes trajectories to the policy with the highest potential reward. Finally, we consider trajectory selection as another approach to mitigate objective conflict, and design a kernel surrogate trajectory attribution algorithm that estimates how feedback signals from a set of trajectories influence policy learning on other trajectories. The kernel can capture nonlinear interactions such as synergy and antagonism between trajectories. We evaluate our approach across diverse environments and benchmarks: routed ensembles improve average performance by 4.3% over baselines for online environments from Meta-World and MO-Gymnasium, and kernel surrogate attribution improves correlation with leave-one-out analysis over existing baselines by 50%. When applied to trajectory selection, kernel-based attribution yields a 27% improvement in reward over baseline attribution methods.
