BE-CTDE: Blockchain-Enabled Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Anomaly Detection and Token Economics

Chih-Wei Huang, Hsin-Chieh Lee, Yun-Chen Yu et al. · Oct 25

BE-CTDE integrates blockchain with multi-agent reinforcement learning for verifiable IoT decision-making.

For you: Completely irrelevant domain (IoT and blockchain) for enterprise LLM systems.

Abstract

Multi-agent reinforcement learning supports cooperative decision-making in Internet of Things systems, but strategic deviations can disrupt coordination and undermine confidence in the training process. This paper proposes Blockchain-Enabled Centralized Training and Decentralized Execution (BE-CTDE), which uses blockchain as an active training-management layer. A behavioral verifier compares executed trajectories with shared-policy recommendations; its flags drive a token mechanism combining slashing, burning, rewards, and compensation. Retained balances determine each agent's contribution to the global policy update, connecting auditable behavior to learning influence. We derive a one-round incentive condition that accounts for action-dependent compensation and the payoff from deviation, and a finite expected exclusion-time bound under explicit conditional detection and accounting assumptions, showing perfect detection is unnecessary. An exact finite-population capacity bound characterizes the limits of the population-relative detector. In multi-agent path finding experiments, BE-CTDE outperforms a state-of-the-art learning-based path planner with substantially higher success rates under attack, even when adversaries form the majority. It also outperforms partially defended baselines in all configurations, with collision rates up to 35% lower than under detection without economic consequences and up to 43% lower than under conventional stake slashing. Together, these results characterize how behavioral verification and token feedback can support accountable cooperative learning.

Humans in Space: Can MLLMs Construct Human-Centric Spatial Understanding via Active Exocentric Exploration?

Tianchen Guo, Tengyue Zhang, Chen Liu et al. · Oct 24 · ▲ 5

Introduced SATE-Bench to evaluate Multimodal LLMs on active exocentric view-exploration and visual self-awareness in occluded 3D scenes.

For you: Focuses on 3D spatial understanding and vision benchmarks, which do not transfer to text-centric enterprise search or RAG.

Abstract

While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at zero-shot reasoning, their spatial capabilities are primarily evaluated through static, passive observation. In complex, human-centric scenes, occlusion often hides crucial geometric evidence, and passive benchmarks force models to guess rather than recognize their own visual uncertainty. To bridge this gap, we introduce SATE-Bench, a novel interactive benchmark comprising 316 real-world scenes and 2,525 multi-turn QA pairs to evaluate visual self-awareness and active exocentric view-exploration. It combines a Passive multi-view control with an Active exploration setting, where models start from a severely occluded view and must iteratively query adjacent viewpoints until they gather enough evidence to fully ``sate'' the question, scored by a step-penalized efficiency reward. Evaluating state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals that they terminate exploration prematurely while reporting high confidence, and struggle with fine-grained 3D geometric tasks; matched-view controls further attribute the Active--Passive gap primarily to insufficient evidence acquisition. Moreover, fine-tuning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) shows that rewarding view-seeking increases active exploration but lowers overall accuracy, as models over-explore and fail to fuse expanding multi-view contexts across turns. These findings indicate that scaling passive multi-view perception does not intrinsically yield active spatial reasoning, highlighting the need for models that know where to look, how to integrate what they see, and when to stop. Code and data are available at https://gtc-gh.github.io/SATE-Bench/

ChronoReplay: One Answer Interface Is Not Enough for Streaming Spatial Intelligence

Yihao Hu, Yitong Lin, Pan Wang et al. · LINs lab · Oct 24 · ▲ 337

Proposed ChronoReplay to address interface lock-in in spatial VLMs by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision as chronologically ordered episodes.

For you: Niche spatial vision-language model post-training technique with no relevance to standard enterprise LLM apps.

Abstract

Training a spatial VLM to answer which side an object lies on does not require it to preserve the metric displacement that determines that answer, while supervision for a distance estimate may leave categorical relations largely unconstrained. When post-training emphasizes one readout, improvements can therefore concentrate on that interface while transfer to others remains weak or even degrades; we call this observed specialization interface lock-in. ChronoReplay addresses it by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision as chronologically ordered episodes, retaining source-native targets, and balancing supervision across families. Under the same observation access, a single ChronoReplay checkpoint improves OVO-S-Bench, OST-Bench, and UCS-Bench together, including a 13.41-point gain on OST-Bench, and achieves the highest three-benchmark mean among the supervision settings compared here. Same-data controls further separate the ingredients of this transfer: answer serialization redistributes gains across OST and UCS, family-balanced learning improves over ordinary joint SFT and natural turn weighting, and chronological replay exceeds a shifted-order control by about 2.41 OST points across two paired seeds. Adaptive frame selection then changes evidence access while keeping the trained model fixed, providing an additional inference-time gain at lower visual cost. Streaming spatial performance depends on what distinctions supervision preserves, how heterogeneous histories are organized during learning, and what causal evidence inference makes available at test time.

Rollback: Experience Backtracking in World Models for Policy Improvement

Jiayi Luo, Yang Zhang, Liliang Chen et al. · AgiBot World · Oct 25 · ▲ 81

Introduced Rollback, a policy improvement paradigm that uses action-conditioned world models to rewind and explore counterfactual futures for robots.

For you: Focused entirely on robotics simulation and reinforcement learning rather than software engineering or LLM pipelines.

Abstract

Action-conditioned world models offer a scalable alternative to costly real-world interaction for improving robot policies. However, existing world-model-based methods largely treat world models as forward-only surrogates of the physical environment, leaving untapped a capability no physical environment affords: rewinding to past decision points to explore counterfactual futures. We introduce Rollback, a policy improvement paradigm that turns world models from passive simulators into counterfactual engines: it rolls back to critical decision points, branches into alternative actions, and contrasts their outcomes to attribute success to individual decisions. This concentrates imagination where it matters most and distills counterfactual outcomes into fine-grained, action-level supervision without a separately learned value model. We build a scalable training system that unifies distributed world-model search with asynchronous real-robot execution, forming a self-reinforcing cycle: real-world experience continually sharpens the world model, and a sharper world model in turn drives further policy improvement. We term the world-model side of this cycle test-time physics grounding, a capability that hand-built physics simulators lack: while the world model serves as the training environment, real transitions from the policy it trains correct its dynamics through a lightweight LoRA pipeline. Experiments on three challenging real-world tasks show that Rollback improves success rates over the strongest baseline by an absolute 10.67%, 11.11%, and 11.85% on block extraction, ball tossing, and tabletop curling, respectively, while achieving more stable improvement throughout training. Although initialized from teleoperated demonstrations far below human level, Rollback surpasses the average success rate of human players on both ball tossing and tabletop curling within 8 hours of training.

Timestral

Adrien Petralia, Alexandre Gavaudan, Artjom Joosen et al. · Mistral · Oct 26 · ▲ 11

Timestral family (5M‑209M) sets SOTA on multiple forecasting benchmarks using decoupled horizon forecasting and synthetic pretraining.

For you: Time‑series forecasting not directly applicable to LLM retrieval or agents.

Abstract

We introduce Timestral, a family of four time series foundation models for probabilistic forecasting, with 5M, 28M, 123M, and 209M parameters. Timestral sets a new state of the art on GIFT-Eval, TIME, and fev-bench, where Timestral-209M and Timestral-123M take the top two places on every metric. We achieve this with three ingredients: Decoupled Horizon Forecasting (DHF), which separates prediction tokens from the causally encoded context, so that longer horizons are trained without masking context and each observed value is both a forecast target and context for later forecasts; a covariate framework that natively supports known-future covariates within a causal architecture; and a two-stage pipeline that pretrains exclusively on a diverse, large-scale synthetic corpus before post-training on a mix of real and synthetic data with extended context. This report describes the model design and its benchmark results. Weights for all four models are available on Hugging Face and inference code on GitHub.

MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching

Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 3

MoveBench introduces a rubric‑based benchmark for LLMs in sports/health coaching, evaluating safety, validity, and personalization.

For you: Domain‑specific benchmark unrelated to retrieval or agent orchestration.

Abstract

Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available at https://github.com/SatoSakula/MoveBench.

PhysEvolve: Verifier-grounded Self-evolution for Physical Reasoning with Executable Worlds

Jinyuan Hu, Yilei Jiang, Chen Tang et al. · Oct 25 · ▲ 2

PhysEvolve trains VLMs for physical reasoning by generating executable worlds and using a physics engine verifier to create counterfactuals.

For you: Physical reasoning with simulators, not relevant to text‑centric LLM pipelines.

Abstract

Physical reasoning underpins how embodied systems understand and act in the physical world, yet vision-language models (VLMs) remain unreliable on questions about dynamics and physical causality. Progress is critically constrained by the scarcity of grounded counterfactual training data, as observational videos capture only the factual trajectory that occurred. The self-evolving paradigm offers a route beyond fixed datasets by enabling models to construct their own training problems. Extending this paradigm to physics, however, requires an external source of truth that can both validate model-proposed environments and verify alternative physical outcomes under intervention. We introduce , a verifier-grounded self-evolving framework that trains VLMs for causal physical reasoning from model-proposed executable worlds and simulation-authored tasks. At each round, a shared VLM proposes executable world programs and evidence schemas; a deterministic physics engine freezes each valid world, simulates matched baseline and body-elided counterfactual branches, and synthesizes discrete, answer-bound visual tasks. Exact verifier feedback from task solving updates both the shared policy via GRPO and an adaptive curriculum over physical intents. Starting from Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, achieves targeted gains across PhysBench, CausalPhys, and ContPhy without human intervention data, improving PhysBench Throwing by 8.8 points and ContPhy Counterfactual Removal and Goal-Pit by 7.4 and 24.4 points. These gains concentrate on interaction-sensitive and altered-world questions, demonstrating zero-shot transfer of causal principles to unseen complex dynamics (cloth, fluid, soft bodies) and real-world video.

Impossibility of No-Regret in Multi-Agent Bidding

Zongjun Yang, Rachitesh Kumar, Christian Kroer · Oct 26

Proved the computational impossibility of simultaneous no-regret in multi-agent bidding and internet auction systems.

For you: Pure economic game theory with no application to LLM agent orchestration.

Abstract

We consider $T$ repeated second-price auctions across $n$ pacing bidders with i.i.d. item arrivals, and show that it is computationally impossible for all bidders to achieve no-regret simultaneously while satisfying their respective budget or return-on-investment constraints. Therefore, in the prominent internet auction systems where buyers simultaneously deploy pacing algorithms or automated bidding agents, we cannot hope to achieve joint no-regret guarantees in general. We establish a deterministic relationship that high variance in online bidding systems implies high regret. By showing that one can extract a pacing equilibrium from a low-variance system, we prove PPAD-hardness of attaining joint no-regret. For several widely used bidding algorithms, we also show evidence from dynamical systems and numerical simulations that they can indeed display high-variance behavior through cycling, even on simple market instances.

Feedback autocratic strategies in stochastic games

Masahiko Ueda · Oct 26

Generalizes feedback autocratic strategies as feedback strategies in stochastic games and provides conditions for payoff control.

For you: Pure game theory and control systems research with no practical connection to production LLM engineering.

Abstract

A stochastic game is an optimal control problem for multiple agents in a stochastic environment. Stochastic games contain important practical problems, related to environmental issues or traffic flows. If the number of environmental states is one, such stochastic games are called repeated games. In repeated games, several strategies which unilaterally control payoffs have been proposed, and these strategies work in a mechanism similar to feedback control. In this paper, we generalize these strategies as feedback strategies in stochastic games, and investigate their properties. In particular, we provide a necessary and sufficient condition for such feedback strategies to work as controllers of payoffs under some assumptions. We demonstrate the performance of such feedback strategies in several examples related to social dilemmas. Our findings connect the theory of payoff control in repeated games to the theory of feedback control in stochastic games.

PDRVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model with Physical Dynamics Representations

Hanqing Wang, Zhaotian Cai, Ruichuan An et al. · Oct 26 · ▲ 147

PDRVLA introduces a lightweight physical dynamics module for VLA robotics models to condition action generation without live video generation.

For you: Not relevant. Pure robotics and vision-language-action control domain.

Abstract

Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models generate robot actions from visual observations and language instructions, but their understanding of physical dynamics remains limited. Video world models can capture scene dynamics, yet generating future videos introduces high latency into the control loop. We introduce PDRVLA, whose lightweight Physical Dynamics Module extracts physical tokens from vision--language features to condition action generation without online video generation. The module learns through direct prediction of future RGB, depth, and optical-flow features, with auxiliary supervision through a frozen pretrained video world model. Training relies entirely on publicly available data, expanded through human-to-robot synthesis, subtask annotation, and embodied chain-of-thought (ECoT) annotation. Across four simulation benchmarks, PDRVLA achieves success rates of 99.3% on LIBERO, 89.9% on LIBERO-Plus, 93.6% on RoboTwin~2.0, and 81.5% on SimplerEnv. Ablations show improved future prediction with world-model supervision and evaluate how subtask conditioning, future object-pose supervision, and ECoT training affect action success.

MatAnyone-E: Sparse Memory Propagation for Efficient High-Resolution Video Matting

Shuyun Wang, Shangchen Zhou, Peiqing Yang et al. · MMLab@NTU · Oct 25 · ▲ 18

MatAnyone-E introduces sparse memory propagation for high-resolution video matting by focusing computation only on uncertain regions and reusing low-resolution features elsewhere.

For you: Off-topic; focuses entirely on high-resolution video matting and computer vision frame processing.

Abstract

High-resolution video matting requires preserving fine details, such as hair strands and soft transparency, while maintaining consistency across frames. Memory-based methods achieve strong matting quality by matching current-frame features with information stored from earlier frames, but this process becomes increasingly costly as resolution grows. We present MatAnyone-E, an efficient extension of MatAnyone 2 that focuses computation where it is most needed. Our approach first performs low-resolution propagation to identify uncertain regions that require closer inspection and the memory candidates most relevant to them. It then performs high-resolution matching only for these regions using the selected memory, while reusing low-resolution matching results elsewhere. This strategy reduces both the number of locations requiring detailed matching and the amount of memory searched, while preserving the information needed to produce a complete matte. We further reduce the cost of generating high-resolution mattes with a lightweight decoder designed around measured runtime bottlenecks and trained to reproduce the original decoder's representations. Experiments show that MatAnyone-E maintains matting quality comparable to MatAnyone 2 while achieving an estimated 7.4x inference speedup and reducing peak GPU memory usage to 7.6% of the usage of MatAnyone 2 at 4K. With compilation-enabled inference on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, it reaches 73.07 FPS at 1080p and 28.62 FPS at 4K.

SO-101 Bench: Accessible Spatial Manipulation

Truman Hickok · Oct 26 · ▲ 4

SO-101 Bench is a simulated benchmark for language-conditioned spatial manipulation on a low-cost robotic arm using Isaac Lab.

For you: Robotics and hardware manipulation benchmark with no relevance to text-based enterprise LLM systems or RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Low-cost arms such as the SO-101 have become the most common entry point to robot learning, yet no released policy performs spatially constrained, language-conditioned tasks on them out of the box, and existing SO-101 benchmarks run only on physical hardware with simple tasks and few held-out objects. We introduce SO-101 Bench, a simulated benchmark for language-conditioned spatial manipulation built on an Isaac Lab digital twin of a real SO-101 setup. It has four tasks scored as five families, 138 objects in seen, unseen-instance and unseen-class splits, 916 evaluation episodes with subsets for held-out appearances, rotate-to-fit planning and occlusion memory, and 3,629 simulated demonstrations on which every learned policy is fine-tuned under an equal compute budget. The twin ranks policies as the real robot does: five policies trained without real data, four VLAs and an LLM agent, keep their exact ranking when the same weights run on the physical arm in an unseen environment with unseen objects (Spearman rho = 1.00, MMRV = 0.000), although simulation overestimates success. Across nine VLAs and world-action models, the best learned policies score near 61%, with spatial placement, unseen object classes and rotate-to-fit planning remaining open. An LLM agent that writes code and hands grasping to a learned policy scores 83.2%, and an ablation shows that this lead comes from the choice of harness and the choice of language model.

LociArt: A Unified Paradigm for Free-Style Local Video Stylization

Yuxuan Chen, Feng Xie, Jiagao Hu et al. · Xiaomi Research · Oct 26 · ▲ 45

LociArt introduces a method for local video stylization using polar style‑aware masks and geometry conditioning, with a 100K paired dataset and benchmark.

For you: Focuses on video art; unrelated to LLM retrieval or agents.

Abstract

Video stylization typically transforms entire scenes, while local artistic editing requires precise region--style association, spatial control, and temporal consistency. We introduce LociArt, a unified paradigm for free-style local video stylization that explicitly specifies where to stylize and what style to apply while preserving non-target content. At its core, GRNStyle builds on Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) and introduces a polar style-aware mask that embeds style identities as equal-norm directions within selected regions. Complementary representation--geometry conditioning combines encoded mask features with raw-mask spatial support, integrating spatiotemporal context with geometric control. To provide paired supervision, we develop an automated pipeline combining global stylization, video object removal, matting, and compositing, and construct the LociArt-100K Dataset with 100K local stylization pairs. We further establish the LociArt Benchmark, comprising 80 source videos and 84 target masks covering 168 foreground/background stylization cases, with a Style Prototype Similarity (SPS) evaluation scheme for regional style quality and complementary measures of non-target preservation and temporal consistency. Experiments show that GRNStyle outperforms existing methods in regional style quality and spatial control while maintaining strong temporal consistency. Qualitative examples further illustrate joint stylization of multiple regions with the same or different learned styles, despite training only on single-region, single-style pairs. Together, these components provide a unified foundation for learning and evaluating controllable local video stylization. Project page: https://higherhu.github.io/lociart/.

Preference-Guided Cooperation in Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Qiyue Xia, J. Michael Herrmann · Oct 26

Researchers introduced Meta-Gradient Informed Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MIRL) to prioritize trade-offs in multi-agent systems.

For you: Too theoretical and focused on reinforcement learning control tasks rather than production LLM agent orchestration.

Abstract

Multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) addresses agent coordination and objective trade-offs simultaneously while optimising overall system performance. Existing work tends to treat multi-agent cooperation and multi-objective optimisation as largely independent components of a learning problem, which amounts to an exorbitant complexity except in trivial cases. We study here whether the structure provided by multiple objectives can itself be exploited to facilitate cooperation, rather than being treated as an additional optimisation challenge. We formulate MOMARL at both the team and individual levels and introduce objective-wise counterfactual credit assignment to evaluate each agent's contribution across the team objectives. We propose Meta-Gradient Informed Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MIRL), with static (MIRL-S) and dynamic (MIRL-D) variants. By incorporating meta-gradient information, MIRL enables agents to learn how to prioritise objectives individually while collectively optimising the system-level objective, allowing the multi-objective structure to directly guide cooperation. Experiments on a multi-objective multiple particle environment show that our methods improve Pareto-front quality over baselines, with the dynamic variant achieving the strongest performance.

Hy-Nomas: Towards Free-form Interactive World Generation

Qiaoling Chen, Jiaxiang Cheng, Xueqing Deng et al. · Tencent Hunyuan · Oct 26 · ▲ 119

Researchers proposed Hy-Nomas, an 8B video world model supporting free-form text instructions and camera control across multiple interaction turns.

For you: Pure video generation and robotics world modeling; completely off-topic for enterprise RAG and LLM app development.

Abstract

Interactive world generation requires models to follow successive commands while preserving relevant history and action consequences. We present Hy-Nomas, a video world model with an 8B diffusion backbone that supports free-form text instructions and continuous camera control across multiple interaction turns. It generates variable-length segments conditioned on current inputs and cached history. Our central design aligns structured video captions with action-aware causal attention: descriptive context remains accessible across segments, while action instructions are restricted to their intended temporal windows. A vision-language Prompt Enhancer (PE) translates user requests and multimodal interaction histories into structured conditions for the next segment. A multi-stage curriculum progressively introduces causal generation, text interaction, camera control, and training on model-generated histories, followed by diffusion distillation. In human evaluation, Hy-Nomas shows superior performance over existing world models. We release model weights, code and benchmarks, together with a lightweight adaptation workflow, to support reproducible research on interactive world generation.

ThinkWorld: World Imagination Reinforces Chain-of-Dynamics Reasoning

Chaehyun Kim, Chi-Pin Huang, Jisoo Kim et al. · NVIDIA · Oct 26 · ▲ 52

Introduces ThinkWorld, a VLA learning framework for robotic manipulation that grounds action reasoning in world dynamics using video data.

For you: Irrelevant for software engineers building text/document-based LLM systems and RAG pipelines.

Abstract

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have made substantial progress in robotic manipulation, but their generalization remains constrained by the diversity and coverage of action-labeled robot demonstrations. Human videos provide much broader behavioral and environmental experience, while learning from them faces a fundamental trade-off between scalable data and explicit action grounding. We introduce ThinkWorld, a VLA learning framework that grounds action reasoning in world dynamics and strengthens it through reinforcement learning with world imagination. ThinkWorld represents each action thought as a Chain-of-Dynamics (CoD), a sequence of discrete motion tokens that captures the intermediate dynamics connecting the current world to a future state and serves as a common action representation across embodiments. We first initialize CoD with action-labeled demonstrations, then predict the future induced by each CoD and reinforce those whose predictions better match the observed outcome. This enables videos without action annotations to provide direct supervision from observed state transitions, allowing human and robot experience to refine the same reasoning space. Experiments in simulation and on real robots demonstrate effective human-to-robot transfer and strong generalization to unseen tasks and environments.

Spatial Audio Generation and Editing with Executable Plans and Self-Distilling Transfusion

Kam Man Wu, Yu Zhang, Pengjun Fang et al. · NoizAI · Oct 24 · ▲ 54

Introduces AMBIT for spatial audio generation and editing using a shared transfusion backbone and self-distilling transfusion.

For you: Entirely off-topic for enterprise RAG and LLM application engineering.

Abstract

Spatial audio authoring coordinates source decisions with soundfield synthesis. For editing, plans specify what to change and retain, while reference audio supplies further acoustic detail. We present AMBIT, a unified framework for native First-Order Ambisonics (FOA) generation and editing with three connected contributions. First, a shared Transfusion backbone couples autoregressive planning with rectified-flow rendering through executable ScenePlans that specify source content, activity, and 3D trajectories. For editing, the plan describes the full target scene, including retained sources; contrastive features ground the requested change, while full reference latents supply residual acoustic detail for preservation. Second, an FOA-aware continuous VAE combines shared-decoder omnidirectional recovery, asymmetric grouped KL, and spatial covariance matching to preserve content and inter-channel structure. Third, joint Transfusion on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) refines planning on student-generated prefixes and rendering at sampling states reached under student plans, with target-conditioned teachers supervising both modes of the shared student. Evaluations across speech, music, and sound show improved generation content and spatial quality, acoustic fidelity during instruction-guided editing, and unedited-source preservation over the compared baselines. Project page: https://wjm-george.github.io/AMBIT/

Beyond the Estimate: Wiener-Informed Decoding for Easier Training of Pixel-Space Flow Models

Ziqiao Wang, Yang You · National University of Singapore · Oct 25 · ▲ 11

Wiener‑informed Decoding adds frozen statistical priors to pixel‑space flow models, speeding training and improving generation quality.

For you: Focuses on image generation, not relevant to LLM systems.

Abstract

Learning velocity fields directly in pixel space is difficult and slow. We show that coarse second-order image statistics can ease this task: frozen Wiener estimates improve velocity learning, with gains extending beyond the linear predictions they explicitly provide. Moreover, we observe similar but complementary gains from frozen bases and pixel decoding. These observations motivate Wiener-informed Decoding (WiD), which uses statistical knowledge both for direct prediction and to organize decoder computation without modifying native blocks. WiD combines a trainable Wiener prediction path with coefficient interfaces that inject features into the native decoder and return its predicted coefficient corrections, with these components initialized with data statistics. All introduced parameters, including the spectrum and coordinate transforms, are trained jointly under the original pixel-space velocity objective. Across model scales and decoder architectures, WiD accelerates training and improves generation quality with modest parameter overhead. It also yields smoother gradient trajectories and remains effective with representation alignment. WiD improves class-conditional and text-to-image generation across different resolutions. On ImageNet 256 x 256, PixelDiT-XL with WiD reaches FID 1.53 after 500 epochs, achieving comparable quality to PixelDiT with 37.5% fewer training epochs. Statistical denoisers contribute beyond the estimate: their computational structure can also ease pixel-space training.

RynnWorld-Latent: A Cross-Embodiment World Model Conditioned on Universal Latent Actions

Haoyu Zhao, Yinzhou Tang, Zixuan Wang et al. · DAMO Academy · Oct 26 · ▲ 9

RynnWorld‑Latent learns a cross‑embodiment video world model conditioned on universal latent actions, pretraining on 553M frames.

For you: Robotics‑centric world modeling, not applicable to enterprise LLM work.

Abstract

Learning controllable world dynamics from heterogeneous interaction videos is challenging because robot embodiments use incompatible action spaces, while human videos lack native control labels. We present RynnWorld-Latent, a cross-embodiment video world model that uses the 3D-grounded latent actions introduced by RynnLAM as a shared conditioning interface. This representation enables joint pretraining on human interaction videos, robot demonstrations, and simulation rollouts without requiring a common native action space. Built on a pretrained video transformer, RynnWorld-Latent combines global action tokens with an identity-initialized local pathway that aligns actions with future video latents. We pretrain on 23 datasets comprising approximately 553.5M frames and adapt the model to target robots through post-training. The framework supports two data-generation routes: digital teleoperation uses target robot commands to generate action-video demonstrations, while latent-action synthesis transfers source motion to an edited initial frame and pairs the resulting rollout with retargeted pseudo robot actions. Experiments show improved video prediction metrics on public benchmarks and two real-robot platforms, alongside qualitative action following when different action sequences share the same initial observation. Synthetic mid-training improves policy success rates on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, and both generation routes improve real-world policy success rates over real-demonstration-only training in the evaluated settings. These results support universal latent actions as a scalable interface for learning controllable visual dynamics across heterogeneous embodiments.

Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning of Co-Adaptive Hip Exoskeleton Control (MARL-Exo) for Metabolic Energy Reduction

Yifei Yuan, Jakob Wolf, Ghaith Androwis et al. · Oct 27

Developed MARL-Exo, a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for co-adapting hip-exoskeleton control with a musculoskeletal human model to reduce metabolic walking energy.

For you: Completely off-topic robotics and biomechanics application with no relevance to software engineering or LLM pipelines.

Abstract

Objective: This study aims to determine whether a hip-exoskeleton policy trained in simulation, by co-adapting with a musculoskeletal human model under a metabolic-energy reward, reduces the metabolic cost of walking in new users without per-user tuning, and how mechanical-power shaping affects the learned assistance. Methods: We developed MARL-Exo, a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework in which a full-body human model (304 muscle actuators; randomized anatomy, gait, and speed) and a bilateral hip-exoskeleton policy are trained concurrently in two stages. The exoskeleton observes only delayed hip kinematics and its past commands; its reward includes muscle metabolic power from a physiologically based energetics model, alone (Metabolic) or with mechanical-power shaping (Metabolic + Power). The Metabolic policy was deployed and tested without retraining in eight healthy adults walking at 1.25 m/s, using indirect calorimetry. Results: In simulation, the Metabolic policy reduced modeled muscle metabolic power by 13.8% relative to no-device walking, versus 8.8% for Metabolic + Power. On hardware, MARL-Exo reduced net metabolic rate by 18.3 $\pm$ 6.6% relative to Passive in all eight participants. Relative to No-Exo, the mean reduction was 7.4 $\pm$ 10.0%; six participants more than offset the 13.1% device-wearing penalty, walking up to 24.2% below their No-Exo cost. Conclusion: A metabolically informed policy co-adapted in simulation transferred to users without per-user tuning and consistently reduced the metabolic cost of walking with the device, while explicit power shaping reduced the simulated benefit. Significance: Unlike per-user human-in-the-loop optimization, this approach yields a single population-level controller and allows assistance objectives to be screened in simulation before human testing.

Intrinsic Goals for Autonomous Agents: Hierarchical Exploration Captures Future Volitional Action in Drosophila

Declan Kutscher, Reece Keller, Alyn Kirsch et al. · NeuroAgents Lab · Oct 24 · ▲ 6

Hierarchical intrinsically motivated agents trained with competency-based objectives replicate fruit fly exploratory behavior and neural representations of future actions, supporting reverse-engineering of autonomous decision-making.

For you: Neuroscience paper on fruit fly whole-brain recordings and motor primitives; irrelevant to enterprise LLM systems.

Abstract

Animals have a remarkable capacity for autonomy---driven by intrinsic motivation---without external sensory cues or extrinsic objectives, yet the computational mechanisms behind this ability largely remain a mystery. We combine whole-brain recordings in the fruit fly (Drosophila melanogaster), which reveal neural representations of spontaneous exploratory decisions more than 10 seconds in advance, with a neuromechanical digital twin to test whether optimizing intrinsic goals yields realistically embodied artificial agents that quantitatively match fly behavior and internal representations of future volitional action during exploration. Our hierarchical policy selects from a discrete library of low-level motor primitives, consistent with ethological theories of behavioral syllables. We train agents without external reward using 20 intrinsic motivation algorithms spanning curiosity, entropy, and competency methods. The agents are evaluated in a virtual tethered-ball assay modeled on the fly experiment, comparing spontaneous locomotor statistics and internal representations and decoding future turn direction from kinematics and neural activity on the same timescales observed in flies. DIAYN, a hierarchical competency algorithm learning discrete policies that maximize behavioral entropy and the separability of induced sensory observations, achieves the strongest joint behavioral and neural prospective coding performance relative to flies. Its learned policies closely capture individual variability in fly exploration strategies, suggesting that this diversity serves to maximize state-space coverage at the population level. DIAYN's latent activity also closely captures the sum-difference rule for interhemispheric encoding of kinematics in biological flies. These results establish hierarchical, intrinsically motivated agents as a powerful framework for reverse-engineering animal autonomy.
